Approved on 20 November 2026 (choice 1561) | Go's rule. It gives a library a level for "my own folders see it, the user does not" (`internal` in C#, `pub(crate)` in Rust) with no new word |
| Leaving in the middle of a line, in compiled code | A `match` gives its value inside a larger expression: it is written before the statement and the parts before it are computed first, so the order stays left to right. A value that owns something and has no name (an argument, a field of a struct being built, a value of an enum value) is kept under a name when something after it can leave the function (`try`, `?? return`, a `match`), and the way out frees it. Every compiled program is looked at for leaks by the tests, and the leak check itself is tried on a program that leaks on purpose. Still refused, as limits of the back end: leaving after an item of a list being built or after a side of an operator that owns something, in an argument of a method call, inside `$"..."`, on the right of `&&` and `\|\|`, inside `assert`. Decided on 13 November | Nothing is paid where nothing can leave; one store for each kept value where something can. A value left behind is not seen in the output or by the sanitizers, so it is looked for by name |
| Tests in compiled code | `sg test <path> --compiled` runs the `test` blocks as compiled code; without the word the interpreter runs them, as the reference that needs no C compiler. `sg build <path> --test` keeps the program that runs them. Each test runs in a process of its own, so a panic ends that test only, and the report is the interpreter's, letter for letter, with the exit code 1 if a test failed. A `try` that meets an error in a test writes it and ends the test, freeing what the test owns. `--only N` runs one test in the same process. The tests of the back end compare the two reports, run the compiled tests under the sanitizers and look at every passing test for leaks. For now POSIX only (`fork`). Decided on 13 November | The process per test is what "Test" decided; here it costs one `fork` for each test. The same `.out` file checks both ways of running, so they cannot drift apart |
| The `sg` command, as built | Its exit codes: 0, what `exit` gives, 1 for an error of the program, a panic or a failed test, 2 for a command line that is not a usage and for a path of it that does not lead anywhere (a program that cannot be read, a folder of `-L` or of `-o` that is not there, an output that is a folder: `sg build shop` from the folder above `shop` asks for `-o`, as Go does, 19 November, choice 1398). What the tool writes itself (the C of `emit-c`, the line `built ...`) ends quietly when what reads it closes, as a program does (1399). So does what it tells on the standard error (a check's error, its usage, what it could not do) when no one reads it, by `SIGPIPE` on Linux and macOS; any other failure there loses the line and keeps the code (21 November, choice 1604; it ended with 101 before, a panic of Rust). The folders under a program are walked once each, a link that leads back passed over (choice 1596). A second program, a flag given twice, `-o` or `--target` with nothing after it, and `-o` with `emit-c` are not usages (18 November). What a build makes on the way (the C, the object of a library, the program of compiled tests) is in a folder of the temporary folder made new for it, at a name no one can guess, that only its user opens, and removed at the end (18 November, the hundred-and-sixteenth slice, choice 1333). The C compiler is the one `CC` names, on every system, else `cc` on Linux and macOS and Clang on Windows (20 November, choice 1533, found by the trials of 1418). Tested from outside in `tests/cli.rs` | rustc refuses `-o` twice; Go's flags keep the last, which leaves a mistyped line built as something else |
| Editor support, as built | `sg lsp` is the language server, in `sg` itself with no dependency: as a file is written it shows the first error of the program the file belongs to (one at a time, since the checker stops there), with its notes and hint, and a hint whose code is certain is a fix applied with one click: a conversion, `.clone()` of a borrowed text, `_ = ` before a value dropped on purpose, the missing `use`. An unused `use` is not shown dimmed yet, and there is no hover, going to a definition or completion. `editors/vscode` colours SG in VS Code (a TextMate grammar) and starts `sg lsp`. Built on 19 November 2026, in the hundred-and-twentieth slice (choices 1369 to 1376). What is shown of an error is made inside the guard that catches a mistake of the checker, so such a mistake is shown as one and the server goes on checking; a folder whose links lead back is checked once (21 November, choice 1596) | What the principle at the top asks for: the error shows in the editor while typing, and a mechanical hint is applied with one click |
| Hardening, as built | Before the preview, what is built was tried four ways (choices 1318 to 1325): the `sg` command end to end (`tests/cli.rs`); a review of the soundness of the checker, the generated C, the boundary of `unsafe` and the tool, a finding counted only with a program that shows it; the front end fuzzed with libFuzzer (`fuzz/`), and generated programs run by the interpreter and compiled under the sanitizers (`fuzz/difference`); and mutants of the functions that make the rules of safety (`.cargo/mutants.toml`), then only on what a change touches. Each finding was fixed after a test that shows it. Done on 18 and 19 November 2026, in the hundred-and-fifteenth to hundred-and-nineteenth slices. After the second review (1589), the generator writes programs that borrow too (1593): not written to be right, each is fitted by taking away the statement the checker's first error names until it is taken, and what is run must read no freed memory under the address sanitizer; on the code before the fixes of 1592 it found their shapes in minutes. It lends ranges of lists as `ref` beside other ranges too (1610), and on the code before that fix found the shape in 89 of 648 programs | Rust has Miri, cargo-fuzz and cargo-mutants for the same ends; Go has its built-in fuzzing |
| Limits, as built | A value takes at most 2^61 - 1 bytes (an array, a struct, an enum, a `T?`), counted as C lays it out: a type whose values would take more is a compile error where it is written, with the hint of a `List<T>`. A program nests at most 1000 deep (blocks, expressions, the operators of a chain, the names read one after another, types and patterns, together), and beyond that the error says to give a part a name. The interpreter panics (*stack overflow*) when writing or comparing a value nested too deep for its stack, and when an array is more than the memory holds. In compiled code a function whose values may take a page of its stack or more touches that room a page at a time before its frame is made (`sg_probe_stack`), and `sg build` gives GCC and Clang `-fstack-clash-protection` on Linux, so a frame larger than the stack ends at the page below it, never in another thread's memory. A function whose values may take more than 256 MB of its stack is an error of `sg build` on every machine, with the hint of a `List<T>`: no thread's stack holds it, and Windows on Arm builds none (choice 1346). Built on 19 November 2026, in the hundred-and-sixteenth slice (choices 1338, 1341 to 1346). The code of texts nested deeper than the parser takes is not looked into for names before it parses, and `sg lsp` reads a line of a header up to 8 KB and a message up to 64 MB (21 November, choice 1596) | Rust's bound of a value is `isize::MAX`, cut here to what Clang allows an array; Rust probes the stack on every machine too. Rust and Go grow the compiler's stack instead of bounding the nesting; a bound costs nothing and no program written by hand comes near it |
| Slice type | `read T[]` is a readable window, `ref T[]` a writable one: pointer + length. A bare `T[]` is not a type, compile error; the hint is `List<T>` for an owner and `read T[]` for a window. What `read string` is for text, `read byte[]` is for raw data. Conversion to a slice: `read xs` and `ref xs` give a slice where a slice is expected (`parse(read buffer)`), for `List<T>` and `T[N]`; passing it without writing `read` is a compile error, and the hint is `read xs`. `xs[..]` stays valid as range notation | Rust's `&[T]` and `&mut [T]` types, in our notation. No new name is needed, `read` and `ref` are enough. In C# `T[]` is an owning array on the heap, here it is not: the same name, a different meaning, and the error hint says so. Conversion to a slice is the same rule as `string` → `read string`, and in Rust `&buf` works this way too. The first decision was `xs[..]` only; it made people memorise a separate notation for lists, and it changed on 11 November |
| Range | In the `a..b` notation the ends can be left out: `data[..end]` from the start, `data[start..]` to the end, `data[..]` the whole. The same on `string`, `List<T>` and slices | The same as Rust and C#. `data[0..end]` and `line[i..line.length()]` were noise, and no new concept is needed |
| Text and bytes | `text.asBytes()` views text as `read byte[]`, for free. `string.fromUtf8(bytes)` views bytes as text: it checks the UTF-8, does not copy, and returns `Result<read string, TextError>` | The Rust names (`as_bytes`, `str::from_utf8`). C# has no path without a copy. The `string` guarantee is kept: there is no unchecked conversion. The copying `string.fromBytes` was removed on 11 November, and whoever wants a copy writes `.clone()`: one way, and the copy is visible in the code |
| Fixed-size array | `T[N]`, where `N` is an integer constant: `int[8] values`. It is a value and stays where it is (on the stack or inside the struct), no heap. `length()` is known at compile time. `xs[i]` is bounds checked; if the index is known at compile time and out of bounds it is a compile error, and if it depends on data it panics. Construction: `int[3] { 1, 2, 3 }` writes the items (a compile error if the count does not match), `byte[16](255)` puts the same value in every item (`T` must be `Copyable`), and `byte[16]` alone fills with zero; a zero value exists only for numbers, `bool` (`false`) and `T?` (`null`), and for any other type it is a compile error. There is no uninitialised memory. If `T` is `Copyable` the array is `Copyable` too, otherwise it is moved (the struct rule). Conversion to a slice is in the "Slice type" row | Rust's `[T; N]` type, in our notation. C# has no natural counterpart (`stackalloc` only in locals, `fixed` only in `unsafe`). Without this every buffer goes to the heap through `List`, and C-level performance does not hold. For construction Rust writes `[1, 2, 3]` and `[255; 16]` and has no short form for zero; in C# `new byte[16]` is always zero, and the short form comes from there. Cost: `T[]` (a slice, needs `read`/`ref`) and `T[N]` (a value) look alike; a large array passed by value is copied silently; the cost of zeroing |
| A range that is written through | `xs[a..b]` on a list, an array or a slice is a `read T[]`: it only looks, as a range of text does. `ref xs[a..b]` is a `ref T[]` and needs what `ref xs` needs. `read xs[a..b]` is a compile error whose hint is the bare range. A `for` over a slice lends its items; a list cannot be changed inside a `for` over a range of it | Rust writes `&mut xs[a..b]`. One rule for every range: bare means "look", and the one word that asks for more is `ref`. Approved on 13 November 2026 |
| Arrays and slices, details | `T[0]` is a compile error. `byte[16]` alone builds an array when `byte` names a type and no variable has that name; in a condition `int[3] { ... }` needs parentheses, like a struct literal. An array and a slice have the methods of a list that only look (`length`, `get`, `indexOf`, `contains`), and `sort` and `reverse` since the forty-second slice ("`sort` and `reverse` on a slice"); `==` compares the items in order when they are `Equatable`; `print` writes `[1, 2, 3]`. Only an index on an array is checked at compile time; the ends of a range and an index on a slice panic. Put off: an array of arrays, `read T[]?`, an array as a key | An array without items has no use and no natural form in C. The rest follows what `List` already does, so nothing new has to be learned. Approved on 13 November 2026 |
| `TextError` | A built-in struct, `TextError : Copyable, Formattable, Equatable`, with one field: `public readonly nint position`, the place of the first byte that is not valid UTF-8. Seen from every module with nothing written; the name is reserved | Rust's `Utf8Error::valid_up_to`. The design had named the type and nothing more; a position is what a caller can act on. Approved on 13 November 2026 |
| `Result` with a borrowed success | `Result<read T, E>` can be written; a function that returns one says where the borrow comes from with `from`, like any type that carries a borrow. A borrow as the error is not allowed yet | `string.fromUtf8` gives a `Result<read string, TextError>`, and a function must be able to pass it on. Approved on 13 November 2026 |
| A borrow of a value with no name | It cannot be kept: `var part = make()[..5]`, `var label = make().label()`, `return first(read make())`, and the same put into a field or an item are compile errors whose hint is to give the value a name. Inside one statement it is fine (`print(make()[..5])`). A text constant is exempt | The value a call returns lives until its statement ends, so a borrow kept longer looks at freed memory; compiled code showed it under the address sanitizer. Rust has the same rule ("temporary value dropped while borrowed"). Core principle: caught at compile time, with the fix in the hint. Approved on 13 November 2026 |
| Arrays and slices in compiled code | An array `T[N]` is a C array inside a struct: it lies where its variable or its field lies, nothing is allocated for it, and passing, returning or assigning it copies its items; `length()` is a constant. A slice is a pointer and a count, one C type for `read T[]` and `ref T[]`. `text.asBytes()` and `string.fromUtf8` copy nothing. A range checks its two ends once, and an index into the window is checked against the window's own count, in every build. An array whose items own something is freed item by item, and moving it empties every item. The success of a `Result` can be a `read string`, a slice or a `read` of a struct, an enum or a collection; a `read T?` as the success is not compiled yet, nor is a `for` that is handed an array behind a pointer | What C does by hand, with the checks the checker cannot do before the program runs. Measured: a buffer on the stack and slices onto it take the time of the same program in C (`buffer`, 0.99); there the C compiler could remove every check, and where a length is not known a check is one comparison that is never taken. Approved on 13 November 2026 |
| Consts | `const T Name = e`, the type always written: at the top of a file (`public const` follows the rule of visibility) and in a block, where it is seen to the end of the block; a name it hides is an error, as for a local ("A name that hides another"). It holds an integer, `float`, `double`, `bool`, `char` or a text, or an array, a `Copyable` struct or enum of them; not a `List`, an owned `string`, a pointer, `shared` or `raw`. Its value is made of numbers, texts, other consts in any order (one that needs itself is an error), operators, casts, `MinValue`, `MaxValue`, `Pi` and the others, arrays (`int[3] { ... }`, `byte[256](0)`), structs and variants, `.length()` of a const array or text, and an item or a field of one; an overflow, a division by zero or an index outside is a compile error. Every use reads the one value: `var t = Masks` copies it, `Masks[i]` and `read Masks` read it where it lies, a borrow of it lasts for the run (a function with no borrowed parameter may return one, as a text constant), and writing into it, `ref` and a method that changes it are compile errors whose hint is a copy. An integer const gives the count of an array (`byte[BlockSize]`), and a const of a number, a `char`, a text or a `bool` stands for its value in a pattern, since a local never takes the name of a const it sees. `const int[] Primes = { 2, 3, 5, 7 }` counts the items (`int[4]`); `const byte[] Logo = embed("logo.gif")` holds the bytes of a file beside the source, read when the program is compiled (C23's `#embed`, Rust's `include_bytes!`). In compiled code a number is written where it is used and a table is one `static const`. Approved on 17 November 2026, before the hundred-and-third slice (choices 1080 to 1087, 1098 and 1099) | C23's `constexpr` and Rust's `const`, with C#'s notation. A table known when the program is compiled lies in the data the program only reads, with no cost at run time, and an error in it is found before the program runs. The type is written because it is what the const says. A `List` cannot be one: it owns a block of the heap, so a copy of it would allocate where nothing shows it, and growing it would write into data that is only read; a table is an array, and a list built once at run time is a static (choices 1088 to 1097, after the calls in a const) |
| Consts, as built | A const of an array, a struct or an enum is, where it is used, a `read` borrow of the one value for the whole run, so the rules of `read` refuse what would change it; a const of a number, a `bool` or a `char` is a value, and one of a text is the text itself. The checker finds every value with the interpreter, once each initializer is checked, and a panic on the way is a compile error that names the const. The count of an array is found from the text of an integer const before the types are, and found again later; the two agree. A variant of the enum looked at comes before a const of its name in a pattern. No text inside an array, a struct or an enum yet. `embed` takes a path written out, from the folder of the file, of a file of one byte to 64 MB, and is written only where a const is given its value. In C a table is defined only where the code reads it. Built on 17 November 2026, in the hundred-and-third slice (choices 1109 to 1119) *(19 November 2026, choice 1339: the path is written with `/`, without `..`, `.`, an empty part, a `\` or a `:`, and the file lies in the folder of the file or below it also where a link leads, as Go's `//go:embed` takes it)* | What the decisions asked for, with the rules the language has already; a table of texts waits for a program that needs it |
| No call in a const | A const calls no function: its value is made of numbers, texts, other consts, operators and casts, `MinValue` and the others, `.length()`, an item or a field of a const, and arrays, structs and enums of them, as in C#, Go and C. A value a function finds is a `static` (`static uint[256] Crc = crcTable()`), found the first time the program uses it; a table the program needs from its start is written out, made by a script beside the code (zlib's `crc32.h`). The same holds for the default value of a field, the number of a variant and a `const assert`, each refused in its own words, with the hint of a `static`, of a `test` or of `assert`. Approved on 20 November 2026, during the hundred-and-forty-fourth slice (choice 1606); it takes back the calls of choices 1100 to 1108 (17 November 2026): the rule of what may run, its errors (`` `size` calls `load`, which reads or writes a file here ``), the ban of `sin` and its kin, the budget of 100 000 000 steps, the 64 MB a growing text or list stopped at, a const that reaches itself through the functions it calls, and the bodies that waited for a const and were checked again | A call in a const ran the program inside the compiler: an editor, a package or a build could be held for seconds and gigabytes by what it only checks, and the language had a second way to find a value beside `static`. Without it the consts are found before any body, from what is written, and the compiler runs no code of the program. The cost is a table written out, or a load and a branch where a static is read |
| No call in a const, as built | `const int[] Squares = squares()`, `int[Count]` from such a const, `const assert(f() == 4)`, `int retries = twice(3)` in a struct and `Image = base()` are refused at the call (`a const calls no function`). Every const is found once, before the bodies, and the checker no longer runs a body for one. The compile-time check of a pattern of `Regex` stays (choice 1607): it runs the `std` module's own `Regex.problem`, never the program's code, within the budget of 100 000 000 steps, the only one left (1 000 000 when the front end is fuzzed). The tables of `std` that were found by calls (CRC-32's 4096 entries, deflate's four) are written out in the module by `tools/std_tables.py`, which `--check` compares with the files (choice 1608): a static would cost each call of `crc32` a load and a branch. Built on 21 November 2026, in the hundred-and-forty-fifth slice (choices 1606 to 1608) | What a const holds is what the source says, so the compiler, `sg lsp` and a package's check cost the same whatever the program does |
| Statics | `static T Name = e`: one value for the program, which lives until it ends and is never assigned. Three kinds: read only (every use gives a `read` borrow; `atomic` fields inside it are changed by their methods through it), `static atomic T`, and `static locked T`, the one place `locked` stands without `shared` (409's exception): the value is the program's, so no owner is counted. No static that is assigned freely and none for each thread ("Globals, as decided"). A value given by the rule of a const (inside `atomic` and `locked` too) lies in the data of the program, with no check and no cost; any other is found the first time the static is used, safely for threads and once (Rust's `LazyLock`), and a later read is one acquire load and one branch. Finding every static before `main` was refused: C++'s order of initialization, a slower start, and a library has no `main`. What finds a value cannot `lock`, `wait` or `await`, through the functions it calls (the rule of a `lock` block), so finding cannot deadlock; one that needs itself is a compile error where the calls show it and a panic where a `Func` makes the circle. What a static holds follows the thread rule (no borrow, no `shared checked` value without a lock, no `Task`) and is not `Disposable` or `Closable`: a static is never dropped, so a file in one would never be closed; the hint gives the value as a parameter. A static is never freed (as in Rust), and the leak report and LSan do not count it. The interpreter runs each test with the statics as the program starts; a compiled test runs in a process of its own. A static is written at the top of a file (`public static`) and in a function, where its name is seen only there (C's `static` inside a function). In C a static is a global of the unit; a `locked` one with a constant value has its mutex initialized statically (`PTHREAD_MUTEX_INITIALIZER`, `SRWLOCK_INIT`); one found later has a once of three states in the runtime, with the wait the runtime has, and spins without an operating system. Approved on 17 November 2026, before the hundred-and-fifth slice (choices 1088 to 1097) | Rust's `static`, `LazyLock` and `static Mutex`, C11's `call_once`, C#'s `static readonly` and Go's package variables, kept to what cannot race: the compiler sees every task that reaches a static, as it sees the arguments of `Task.run`. A table found once and read everywhere, a counter of requests, the settings of a server and a kernel's count of ticks need no parameter threaded through every call |
| Statics, as built | The value is the body of a function of its own (`Name#init`), checked as any body; a static of a block sees the consts and statics of the blocks around it and no local. The value lies in the program from its start when it is made of numbers, consts, operators and casts, arrays, structs and enums of them, `atomic` around them, of a type a const can hold; a static given a call is found where it is first used (a const calls none, choice 1606). A number, a `bool`, a `char` or a function is read as a value, anything else lent as `read`; assigning, `ref` and `read` on a number are errors that point at `atomic` and `locked`. A `static locked` value is written only in a `lock`; a local does not take the name of a static; a static that holds a function is copied to a local to be called. The circle is followed through the calls, the `format`, `equals`, `hash`, `compare` and `dispose` that run unwritten, and the statics read on the way. At run time a task that finds a static being found by itself, or by a task that waits for one it is finding, panics, in the interpreter and in compiled code alike. A debug build and the interpreter let go of what the statics hold when the program or a test ends, before the leaks are counted; a release build does nothing. In C a static that holds no atomic value and is known from the start is a `static const`; one found later is read through an inline function that tests the state, the first finder runs under one lock of the runtime that the waiters sleep on; a library always waits that way, since C may call it from several threads. A const cannot read a static. Built on 18 November 2026, in the hundred-and-fifth slice (choices 1135 to 1147) | Reading a static found later in a loop of CRC-32, a load and a branch for each byte, takes 0.99 times the same loop over a global of C on macOS (`statics` in the benchmarks) |
| `const assert` | `const assert(cond)` and `const assert(cond, "message")`, at the top of a file and in a block: a `bool` made as a const is, with no call (choice 1606), which must be `true`. If it is not, the compile error says the message, or the condition as written, and for a comparison the value of each side. `Memory.sizeOf<T>()`, `Memory.alignOf<T>()` are written only here, not in a const, whose value is the same on every machine: such an assert is checked for the machine the program is compiled for, the interpreter's own, and compiled code gives it to C's `_Static_assert` too. Approved on 18 November 2026, before the hundred-and-sixth slice (choices 1151, 1152) | C11's `static_assert`, Rust's `const _: () = assert!(...)`, Zig's `comptime assert`. A layout a file or C expects, or a table written out, is checked before the program runs, at no cost when it runs |
| `const assert`, as built | A const named `assert#` and where it is written, which nothing else can name, checked and found with the consts; nothing of it is in compiled code. It calls no function, as no const does (choice 1606, which made the rule of 1159 for one that measures a type, `Memory.sizeOf`, `Memory.alignOf` or a limit of `nint` or `nuint`, the rule of every one); the message is a text written out, with nothing put into it. Approved on 18 November 2026, after the hundred-and-sixth slice (choices 1157 to 1166) | The C compiler of another machine (`--target`) is the one that knows its layout |
| Loops with a name | `outer: for ...`, `outer: while ...`, and inside, `break outer` and `continue outer`. A name not used is an error, as is the name of a loop around it again; loops side by side may share one; names of loops are apart from those of variables. Leaving goes as `break` does: what the bodies left own is let go, through `lock`, `as`, `unsafe` and an arm of a `match`, and `while true` left only by `break outer` is a loop that ends. In compiled code a `goto`. Approved on 18 November 2026, before the hundred-and-sixth slice (choices 1148 to 1150) | C2y's named loops, Go's and Java's notation; Rust writes `'outer:`, C# has only `goto`. Without it, leaving two loops takes a flag and a test in each round |
| What a loop moved | What is moved after a loop is what was moved where a `break` left it, and, unless it is `while true`, before its first round and where each round ended or a `continue` went on: a loop may run no round or end after any. Each `continue` starts a round, so what it leaves moved is checked as the end of the body is. Found in the hundred-and-sixth slice and approved on 18 November 2026 (choice 1157) | Before, a value moved before a `continue` or a `break`, or moved before a loop that gives it back, was taken as there: the interpreter stopped on it and compiled code read an emptied value. Rust's borrow checker follows every way out the same way |
| Counting down | `for i in (a..b).reversed()`: the numbers of `a..b`, the last first; none when `a` is not below `b`. The ends are found once, the left first. In compiled code the end is taken down by one before each round, so an unsigned counter stops at 0 without wrapping. A range in brackets is written only so, at the head of a `for`. Approved on 18 November 2026, before the hundred-and-sixth slice (choice 1155) | Rust's `(a..b).rev()`, Swift's `reversed()`. Counting down by hand with `while` and a `uint` is a loop that never ends |
| Raw text | C#'s: `"""a "quoted" word"""` on one line; on lines, the opening quotes end their line and the closing ones stand on a line of their own, whose indentation is taken off every line (a line indented less is an error, one with nothing on it may be). No escapes; a line break is `\n`; more quotes hold three. `$"""` puts values in as `$"..."` does, `{{` for a brace; C#'s `$$"""` is not taken. Approved on 18 November 2026, before the hundred-and-sixth slice (choices 1153, 1154) | Tests, JSON, help texts and Windows paths are written as they are read. Rust's `r#"..."#` keeps the indentation; Go's backquote and Zig's `\\` lines are the same idea |
| Enums with numbers | `enum Block : byte { Image = 0x2C` / `Trailer = 0x3B }`: the integer type comes first in the list after `:`, the interfaces after it; each variant on its line. Its variants carry nothing. `= value` is a const of that type; a variant without one has one more than the variant above it, the first 0; a number that does not fit, and two variants with one number, are compile errors. An enum that names no type has no numbers and is not cast to one. `(byte)block` gives the number, and a cast to another integer type follows that type's rules; nothing widens on its own. `Block.fromValue(b)` gives a `Block?`, `null` for a number that is no variant; `(Block)b` is an error whose hint is `fromValue`. In compiled code it is its integer type, with no tag for a moved value. `: Comparable` orders by the numbers, not by the lines; printed it is the name of its variant; `match` takes the names of the variants. Not in a signature of C, a struct of plain data or raw memory, where C could give a number that is no variant: it goes as `(int)mode` and comes back through `fromValue`. Approved on 18 November 2026, before the hundred-and-seventh slice (choices 1167 to 1176) | C#'s notation and Rust's rules (`#[repr(u8)]`, the next number, no two variants with one); a number written into a file keeps its meaning when the lines are moved, which is why an enum without a type has none. C# casts any number to an enum without a test, Rust has no cast back; `fromValue` is Zig's `std.meta.intToEnum`. An enum open to numbers that are no variant (Zig's `_`) can come later |
| Enums with numbers, as built | The number of a variant is a const of its own (`Image#` and where it is written), found with the consts, before any function is checked: it calls no function, as no const does, and names consts and, through a cast, other variants (a circle is an error). The cast reads the number where the value is, without moving it. `fromValue` is a function the language gives, and one written in the enum is an error. An enum with numbers that is `: Comparable` and writes no `compare` is given one that compares the numbers: the row "`Comparable` is written" holds for every other type. C's `long` is not a type of numbers. In C the tag is the number in the enum's integer type; `fromValue` is one test of the two ends when the numbers follow each other, a `switch` when they do not. Built on 18 November 2026, in the hundred-and-seventh slice (choices 1184 to 1191), and approved the same day | What the decisions asked for, with the consts and the casts the language has; the generated `compare` is what 1174 means for the order |
| The bits of an integer | C23's `<stdbit.h>` as methods: `countOnes`, `countZeros`, `leadingZeros`, `trailingZeros`, `leadingOnes`, `trailingOnes`, `rotateLeft(n)`, `rotateRight(n)`, `reverseBytes`, `reverseBits`, `isPowerOfTwo`, `nextPowerOfTwo`, `log2`. A count, and `log2`, is an `int`. Counting, turning and rotating work on every integer type, a signed one by its bits; the powers of two only on the unsigned ones (hint `((uint)n).isPowerOfTwo()`); C's `long` has none. The count of a rotation is cut to the width of the type, with no panic. `nextPowerOfTwo` follows the rule of overflow; `log2` rounds down and panics on 0 in every build. In compiled code the builtins of the C compiler, with 0 guarded. Approved on 18 November 2026, before the hundred-and-seventh slice (choices 1177 to 1183) | Rust's names and rules (`ilog2` panics on 0; C#'s `Log2(0)` is 0, the same as `Log2(1)`). A count is an `int` as in C#'s `BitOperations`, not Rust's `u32`: a length in SG is signed, and an `int` and a `uint` do not mix, so a `uint` count would need a cast in `32 - n.leadingZeros()` and as an index. Each is one instruction or a few, as C writes them |
| The default value of a field | `struct Config { int port = 80 }`: a literal that leaves the field out gives it that value, and the value written wins; with a default on every field, `Config {}`. No mark at the literal. A default is what a const can be, found once when the program is compiled (a call included), or a text, or an empty collection (`List<int>()`, which allocates nothing), put in as if it were written there. A field that is not `public` keeps a literal out of other modules, with a default or without (the row "Consequences of visibility" is unchanged). Approved on 18 November 2026, before the hundred-and-eighth slice (choices 1193 to 1195) | C#'s and Go's form, without Rust's `..` (`default_field_values`, not stable yet): the default is read in the struct. Rust's rule for what a default is: a value known when the program is compiled, so a literal costs nothing it does not show; C#'s initializer that runs on every literal was refused. A private field with a default does not open the literal, or adding a default could quietly let other modules past what `create` checks |
| The default value of a field, as built | Each default that is not `null` or an empty collection is a const of its own (`port#` and where it is written), checked by the rules of a const, with a message that speaks of the field; a `T?` field holds one of `T`. `null` and an empty collection are checked where they are written. A field that borrows, and one of a union, has no default. Built on 18 November 2026, in the hundred-and-eighth slice (choices 1202 to 1203), and approved the same day | The consts the language has, as the numbers of an enum are; a borrow given by a default would have nothing to borrow from |
| `with` | `p with { x = 3 }`: a new value of the struct, with the fields written changed. `p` is used up unless it is `Copyable`; a copy is written where it is made (`p.clone() with { ... }`). The values the fields written held are dropped where `with` is, and a `Disposable` one is disposed of there. `with` is a literal for the rule of visibility: a struct with a field another module does not see is not copied with `with` there. In a condition it needs parentheses. Approved on 18 November 2026, before the hundred-and-eighth slice (choices 1196 and 1197) | C#'s notation for Rust's `Point { x: 3, ..p }`, which moves what it takes; no copy that does not show. Rust's rule for privacy: C# needs only the fields written to be seen, and that would let another module change a `readonly` field |
| `with`, as built | It binds tighter than any operator between two values and looser than one in front (`try load() with { ... }` changes what `try` gives). `p` runs first, then the values written in their order, and only then are the old values dropped. `with { }` is an error. Built on 18 November 2026, in the hundred-and-eighth slice (choices 1204 and 1205), and approved the same day | C#'s place among the operators |
| `?.` | `a?.b.c`: `null` when `a` is, and the rest of the chain does not run (C#, Swift, JavaScript), never a `T??`. A field that is not `Copyable` is lent, not moved: `a?.name` is a `read string?`; `a?.name.clone()` owns. `a?.b is not null` narrows `a`. Only on a `T?`; on another value the hint is `.`, on a `Result` `try` or `??`. A method that gives nothing is called through `?.` only on a line of its own, and the value of one that gives something is used, as with `.`. No `a?.b = 5` and no `a?[i]` for now. Approved on 18 November 2026, before the hundred-and-eighth slice (choices 1198 to 1201) | C#'s rule, which Kotlin does not have: one `?.` covers the chain. Rust writes `as_ref().map(...)`. Lent, because moving out of a place that is only looked at would empty it |
| `?.`, as built | `a?.rest` is read as `match a { null => null, var held => held.rest }`, with a name no program can write. A place is lent: `read`, or `ref` when the first method of the chain, reached through fields and items, changes what it is called on; a method that takes `this` as the first step uses the place up, as `.` does. From a value with no name a field is taken, as `make().name` takes it. A `Copyable` value is copied. Built on 18 November 2026, in the hundred-and-eighth slice (choices 1206 and 1207), and approved the same day | The `match` the language has: the borrows, the moves and the code of C are its own |
| A range with an end outside | A range with an end that lies outside the items panics with "range 4..3 is out of range: the length is 3", for text and for items, in the interpreter and in compiled code. "ends before it starts" is left for a range whose two ends are both inside (`xs[2..1]`) | `xs[4..]` on three items used to say "ends before it starts", which sends the reader to the wrong end. Approved on 13 November 2026 |
| Bit operators | `&`, `\|`, `^`, `~`, `<<`, `>>` and `&=`, `\|=`, `^=`, `<<=`, `>>=`, on integers only; two `bool`s have `&&`, `\|\|` and `!=`, and the error says which. `&`, `^` and `\|` bind tighter than a comparison, so `flags & mask == 0` tests the masked value; among themselves the order is C's (`\|`, `^`, `&`, shifts, `+ -`, `* / %` from loosest to tightest), and a bound of a range takes everything tighter than a comparison. `a & b` follows the rule of arithmetic (`byte & byte` is a `byte`), `~x` has the type of `x`. In compiled code they are the operators of C, cut back to the type. Approved on 13 November with the interpreter's "thirty-second slice" | In C and C# `flags & mask == 0` means `flags & (mask == 0)`, a leftover its authors called a mistake; Rust, Go, Swift and Zig read it as here. With C#'s order the line would be a compile error here, so the choice was between an error on a line that can mean one thing and the line working. Go's fewer levels would change what `1 << n + 1` means for a reader from C or C#. Cost: none at run time |
| Shifts | A shift has the type of its left side and the count is any integer: `b >> 4` on a `byte` is a `byte`, and the expected type reaches the left side alone (`long wide = 1 << 40` shifts a `long`). The count follows the overflow rule: below zero, or not below the bits of the type, it is a compile error where it is known, a panic in the interpreter, in tests and in a debug build ("shift by 32 is out of range: int has 32 bits"), and cut to the bits of the type in a release build. The bits that `<<` pushes out are gone in every build. `>>` carries the sign on `int`, `long` and `nint` and brings in zeros on `byte`; there is no `>>>`. `x << (n & 31)` means the same in every build; there are no `wrapping...` or `try...` methods for shifts. Approved on 13 November | Rust's rule. The cut is what the shift of the machine does by itself, so a release build pays nothing. In C such a count is undefined and `1 << 40` is an `int` shifted too far; C# always cuts and never tells; Go tests in every build. Measured with `bits`: the sieve half takes the time of C |
| Writing a number | Decimal, hex (`0xde`), binary (`0b1010`), with `_` between digits (`1_000_000`, `0xdead_beef`). The prefix is a small letter, the hex digits may be either; no octal. Such a number takes the type it is used as, like any other, and one that does not fit is an error that shows it as written with its value (`int mask = 0xffff_ffff`). Approved on 13 November | Nothing at run time, a dozen lines in the lexer. In C# `0xffff_ffff` quietly becomes a `uint`; Rust refuses it as here. C's octal (`010` is 8) is a trap and is left out |
| A number as a condition | `if flags & 2 {` is "expected `bool`, found `int`" with the hint "a number is not a condition; compare it: `flags & 2 != 0`". Approved on 13 November | The habit from C; the error was already there, the hint says what to write |
| The other integer types | `sbyte`, `short`, `ushort`, `uint`, `ulong` and `nuint` come together, next to `byte`, `int`, `long` and `nint`. Decided on 13 November | The names of the "Number types" row; one line each in compiled code, so the four that are rarer cost little more than the two that hashes and rotations need |
| Mixing integer types | A type widens to another on its own only when the other holds every one of its values, on every machine (`nint` and `nuint` count as at least 32 bits). A signed type never becomes an unsigned one on its own. Two types where neither holds the other (`int` and `uint`, `long` and `ulong`) do not mix: a compile error whose hint is the cast. Decided on 13 November | Rust and Go widen nothing on their own; C# turns `int + uint` into a `long`; C turns the `int` into an `unsigned` without a word, so `-1 < 1u` is false. Core principle: caught at compile time, with the fix in the hint. Cost: none at run time |
| A number next to another type | A number written in the program takes the type of the other side, on either side: `1 + count` and `count - 1` with a `uint count` are `uint`s. A number past every `long` on the left is typed from the right side (`0xffff_ffff_ffff_ffff == top`). Approved on 13 November | Without it `1 + count` would need a cast, since `int` and `uint` do not mix. Cost: none at run time |
| A number is never unsigned on its own | With no type where it goes, a number is an `int`, or a `long` if it does not fit in an `int`; one past every `long` is an error whose hint writes the `ulong` type. Approved on 13 November | C# quietly makes `0xffff_ffff` a `uint` and a larger one a `ulong`; Rust refuses as here |
| Negative and unsigned | `-x` on an unsigned type is an error with the hint `-(long)x`, as on `byte`. A constant that cannot fit is a compile error with the note "a `uint` is never negative" (`(uint)-1`, `ushort port = -1`); a cast whose value is known only at run time follows the overflow rule. Approved on 13 November | Rust refuses `-x` the same way; C# makes `-uint` a `long`; C wraps |
| `>>` on unsigned types | `>>` brings in zeros on every unsigned type and carries the sign on the signed ones, so a rotation is `value << by \| value >> (32 - by)` on a `uint`. There is no `>>>`. Approved on 13 November | The type decides, as in C#, Rust and Go |
| An index stays a `nint` | `uint`, `nuint` and `ulong` are not `nint`s on their own, so an index of those types needs `(nint)`. Approved on 13 November | Follows from "Mixing integer types": a `uint` does not fit in a 32-bit `nint`. Rust indexes with `usize` only |
| Unsigned types in compiled code | `int8_t` to `uint64_t`; `nint` and `nuint` are `int64_t` and `uint64_t`. A failing operation hands its numbers to the panic as `__int128`, so a `ulong` is written in full, and a cast tests its value the same way. The interpreter keeps every integer as its value in 128 bits. Approved on 13 November | `__int128` is a clang and gcc extension like the overflow builtins already used, and costs nothing measurable. Measured with `hash` (XXH64 on `ulong`): the time of C, once the eight bytes it reads are cut out of the data first |
| Writing a fractional number | Digits on both sides of the point or an exponent: `1.5`, `1e9`, `6.674_30e-11`; a small `e`, `_` between digits, no suffix (`1.5f` is an error). It takes the type it is used as, `double` where nothing says otherwise. A number that would become infinity or 0 in its type is an error (`1e400`); a whole number where a fractional one goes becomes one if the type holds it exactly. Approved on 13 November | The rule of the integers: the type is where the number goes. C# needs `f` for a `float`; Rust infers as here |
| Fractional and integer types | A number becomes fractional on its own only when the type holds every value of it exactly: `float` to `double`, the integers up to 16 bits to both, `int` and `uint` to `double`; never `long`, `ulong`, `nint` or `nuint` (hint `(double)n`), never `double` to `float`, never a fractional number to an integer. Approved on 13 November, after looking at the cost | The mixing rule of the integers carried on. Cost: `sum / xs.length()` needs `(double)`, a length being a `nint`. C# converts a `long` to a `double` without a word and loses digits past 2^53; Rust and Go convert nothing on their own. A value that is sometimes whole and sometimes not is an enum written out |
| Fractional arithmetic | The hardware's: `+ - * / %` and `-x` never panic, a division by zero gives `Infinity` or `NaN`, `%` keeps the sign of the left side, a `float` is computed as a `float`. Each operation is rounded on its own; nothing is fused into a multiply-add, in the interpreter and in compiled code (`-ffp-contract=off`). No bit operators. Approved on 13 November | Compiled code and the interpreter give the same digits; clang on arm64 fuses by default. A fused multiply-add on request (`x.fma(y, z)`) can come later |
| Equality with fractional parts | `-0.0` equals `0.0` for `equals` and `compare` too. `==` on a struct, a list or an array compares its fractional parts with `equals`, so a value that holds `NaN` equals itself. `sort`, `indexOf`, `contains` use the total order. A fractional number cannot be a `match` pattern. Approved on 13 November | C#'s records and default comparer; every `Equatable` value equals itself. Cost: `p == q` is not always `p.x == q.x` |
| Fractional to integer | `(int)x` drops the fraction. A value that does not fit, `NaN` included, follows the overflow rule: a panic in the interpreter, tests and a debug build; in a release build the nearest value the type holds, 0 for `NaN`. A constant that cannot fit is a compile error. `(double)n`, `(float)n`, `(float)d` round and never fail. Approved on 13 November | Rust's `as` for the release build; in C the same cast is undefined behaviour |
| Printing a fractional number | The fewest digits that read back as the same number (as a `float` for a `float`), laid out as JavaScript does: `0.1`, `2`, `1e+21`, `1e-7`, `NaN`, `Infinity`, `-0`. Of those digits the nearest, a tie between two to the even digit (*changed on 15 November 2026, in the sixty-eighth slice, choice 496:* until then the number was rounded to 1, 2, ... digits until it read back, which next to a power of two can give one digit more; compiled code finds the digits with Dragonbox, the interpreter with Rust's shortest digits, and they agree by the rule). Methods: `abs`, `sqrt`, `floor`, `ceil`, `round` (half away from zero), `truncate`, `isNaN`, `isInfinite`. Approved on 13 November | The digits are those of C# and JavaScript; C# changes to an exponent from 10^15, Rust never. The constants and the math methods came in the next slice (see "Constants of the number types", "Math methods") |
| Fractional numbers in compiled code | C's `float` and `double`, `<math.h>` and `-lm`; one helper per integer type for a cast from a fractional number. Measured with `nbody`: the time of C. Approved on 13 November | Nothing on a fractional number is checked, so the overflow-checked build takes the same time |
| Reading a number out of text | `T.parse(read string text)` on every number type gives `Result<T, NumberError>`, called on the type: `int.parse(text)`, `double.parse(text)`. A whole number is a sign and digits and nothing else: no spaces, no `_`, no `0x`; a `-` on an unsigned type is an error. A fractional number may also have a point (`.5` and `5.` too) and an exponent (`1e3`, `1E-3`), and `NaN`, `Infinity`, `-Infinity` as `print` writes them. The value is the nearest number of the type; a fractional one past the largest is `Infinity`, as in Rust, C# and C. Approved on 13 November | What `print` writes, `parse` reads back. Data writes `.5` and `1E5`, so the reader is looser than a number written in the program; spaces are trimmed by the program, not ignored silently |
| The error of `parse` | The built-in `enum NumberError : Copyable, Formattable, Equatable { Invalid(nint position), OutOfRange }`: `Invalid` is the byte where the text stops being a number (its length when it ends too early, so an empty text is `Invalid(0)`), `OutOfRange` a whole number the integer type does not hold. Every byte is looked at before the size. Seen from every module, cannot be defined again; `try` puts it into the caller's error type. Approved on 13 November | Two cases to handle, and a position to show. Rust has five kinds and Go two, neither with a position |
| Constants of the number types | `MinValue` and `MaxValue` on every integer type (`nint.MaxValue` is the machine's); on `float` and `double` also `NaN`, `Infinity`, `Pi`, `E`, and `MinValue` is the most negative finite number. A constant is a value of its type, not a number written out: `uint u = int.MaxValue` needs `(uint)`. `Epsilon` is left out, and the error says why. Approved on 13 November | C#'s names. C# lets a constant that fits go to any type, but `nint.MaxValue` depends on the machine; one rule for all. `Epsilon` is the smallest number above zero in C# and the gap after 1 in C and Rust |
| Conversions that do not panic | `x.tryToInt()` gives `null` if the value does not fit, checked in every build; `x.wrappingToInt()` keeps the bits that fit, in every build; the same for every integer type, named after it (`tryToUlong`, `wrappingToByte`). On a fractional number only `tryTo...`, toward zero. One that cannot fail (`b.tryToInt()` with a `byte b`) is an error with a hint. Approved on 13 November | The pair of `tryAdd` and `wrappingAdd`, for a conversion: `tryTo` where a value that does not fit is an error, `wrappingTo` where cutting is what is meant (a hash, the low byte) |
| Panics name the way out | The panic of a cast names `tryToX()` and `wrappingToX()`; the panic of `+`, `-`, `*` names `tryAdd` and `wrappingAdd` (and the others), of `/` `tryDivide`. Compiled code writes the same lines. Approved on 13 November. An index past the end of a list, an array or a slice names `.get(i)`, which gives `null` (19 November, choice 1401). The text of a `panic`, the code of an `assert` and the values it compared, and a key that is not in the map are the program's own and are shown as written, where the tool's own messages choose `a` or `an` before code (21 November, choice 1596) | The row "Narrowing conversion" asked for it. `-x` and the shifts have no such method yet |
| Math methods | On `float` and `double`: `exp`, `log` (natural), `log2`, `log10`, `sin`, `cos`, `tan`, `asin`, `acos`, `atan`, `y.atan2(x)`, `x.pow(y)`, `a.fusedMultiplyAdd(b, c)` (rounded once); two numbers meet as with `+`. From the C library in the interpreter and in compiled code, so on one machine they agree to the last digit. On an integer the hint is `((double)x).sqrt()`. Approved on 13 November | Methods, as `sqrt` already is, not `Math.Sin(x)`. Across machines `sin` and the others may differ in the last digit, as in every language that uses the C library |
| `min` and `max` | On integers the two meet as with `+`. On fractional numbers `NaN` if either side is one, and `-0` is less than `0` (C#, IEEE 754-2019 `minimum`). Approved on 13 November | C's `fmin` and Rust's `f64::min` give an answer that depends on the order and on the machine |
| Reading fractional numbers in compiled code | Clinger's fast path (digits that fit in 53 bits, a power of ten up to 10^22: one correctly rounded operation), otherwise `strtod`; correctly rounded like the interpreter, compared on 8000 random texts. Measured with `numbers`: reading takes the time of `strtoll` and `strtod`. Approved on 13 November | Rust takes the same fast path. Building the text with `toString` is slower than C; printing a `double` is on the list of slow places |
| `char` is not a number | No arithmetic on a `char`, no comparison with an integer, no widening: `int x = c` is an error whose hint is `(int)c`. A cast goes between `char` and the integer types only. `(char)n` panics in every build when no character has the code (hint `n.tryToChar()`, which gives `null`), and is a compile error when the code is known; `(byte)c` follows the integer rule. `char` is `Copyable`, `Equatable`, `Hashable`, `Comparable` and `Formattable`, and `match` takes characters. Inside a collection it is written `'a'`. Approved on 13 November 2026 | Rust's way; C# adds a `char` like a number. A bad code written into a text would break the `string` guarantee, so that check stays in release, like the bounds check |
| `\u{...}` | A character by its code, in a text and in a character constant: `'\u{e9}'`, `"\u{1F600}"`, one to six hex digits. Approved on 13 November 2026 | Rust's and Swift's form, and what `print` writes for a control character inside a collection. C#'s `\uXXXX` cannot reach past 0xFFFF in one escape |
| ASCII tests of a character | `isAscii`, `isAsciiDigit`, `isAsciiLetter`, `isAsciiAlphanumeric`, `isAsciiUpper`, `isAsciiLower`, `isAsciiWhitespace`, `toAsciiUpper`, `toAsciiLower` on `char` and on `byte`; `isWhitespace` (Unicode's White_Space) and `utf8Length` on `char`. `isDigit` is not there, and gets `isAsciiDigit` as a hint. Approved on 13 November 2026; the methods that need Unicode tables came on 16 November ("Unicode tests of a character") | One answer everywhere; C#'s `char.IsDigit` says yes to the digits of many scripts and `ToUpper` depends on the culture |
| Going over a text | `text.chars()`, `text.split(sep)` (a `char` or a `read string`, never empty), `text.splitWhitespace()`, `text.lines()`. The parts are `read string`s that look at the text; `split` keeps the empty parts, `splitWhitespace` skips them, `lines` leaves out the `\r` before `\n`. What they give is an iterator: `for` goes over it and uses it up, `it.next()` gives the next part or `null`. It is not copied, and its type cannot be written yet (it carries a borrow of the text). Approved on 13 November 2026 | Rust's meanings. Nothing is copied, and the iterator waits for generics to get a type that can be written |
| A part of a text and its text | A part borrows the text, not the iterator: after `var name = parts.next()` the iterator goes on. While a `for` goes over a text the text cannot be changed or moved; `split` keeps its separator too. An iterator over a text with no name is an error. Approved on 13 November 2026 | The rule of a `from source` method, and of a `for` over a collection |
| Trimming and looking in a text | `trim`, `trimStart`, `trimEnd` give a `read string` without Unicode's White_Space at the ends. `indexOf`, `lastIndexOf` (a `nint?` in bytes), `contains`, `startsWith`, `endsWith` take a `char` or a `read string` (the two positions a `byte` too). `equalsIgnoreAsciiCase` folds the ASCII letters only; the design's `equalsIgnoreCase` is renamed. Approved on 13 November 2026 | Positions in bytes, because `text[a..b]` takes bytes. Ignoring the case of any text needs Unicode tables |
| `try` on a `read T?` | Gives a `read T`, as `??` does: `var level = try parts.next()`, in a function that returns a `T?` or a `read T?`. Approved on 13 November 2026 | Nothing is taken out; the borrow is passed on. It was an error before |
| Text in compiled code | A `char` is a `uint32_t`; an iterator a small struct whose `next` the C compiler inlines; a part a pointer and a length into the text. Measured with `fields`: splitting, trimming and reading a million lines takes 1.08 times C written with `memchr` and `strtoll`. Approved on 13 November 2026. A text of more than one byte is looked for (`indexOf`, `contains`, `split`, `replaceAll`, and a regular expression's plain text) by its two rarest bytes, ranked by a table of how common each byte is in text and code, compared at sixteen starts at a time with the processor's vector instructions where the C compiler has them, the text itself only where both are found; one byte by `memchr` (choice 1549, 20 November 2026) | What C does by hand, with the UTF-8 read where a character matters |
| Building a text | A `string` is its own builder: `text.write(x)` adds at its end whatever `print` takes, as `f.write` does, and a `$"..."` is written straight into the block. No builder type beside it. It needs the text where it may change (not through `read`, not a `readonly` field), and the borrow rules hold as for any `ref this` method. Approved on 13 November 2026 | A `string` already is a pointer, a length and a capacity, so the finished text needs no copy. Rust's `String` is its own builder; C# and Go keep a separate one |
| Writing a text into itself | `s.write(s)`, `s.write(s[..3])`, `s.write(s.trim())` and a value that borrows from `s` are errors whose hint is `.clone()`; a number, a `bool` or a `char` computed from `s` is fine, since the values of a line are computed first. Approved on 13 November 2026 | The text may move to a larger block while it grows, and what is written would be read from the old one. Rust's rule |
| `clear` and `withCapacity` | `text.clear()` empties the text and keeps its block; `string.withCapacity(n)` takes room for `n` bytes at the start (0 takes none, a negative `n` panics in every build). Without it the block starts at 32 bytes and doubles. A constant is copied on the first write. Approved on 13 November 2026 | A line built again in every round of a loop takes memory once; a text whose size is known grows once |
| `+=` on text | Stays an error, with the hint `text.write(...)`; `StringBuilder`, `append` and `push` get the same hint, `a + b` keeps `$"{a}{b}"`. Approved on 13 November 2026 | One way to add to a text |
| Building text in compiled code | `write` is the code of `f.write`; the formatted pieces are written straight into the block where it has room. Measured with `numbers` and `fields`: building a million lines takes the time of `sprintf` into one buffer in C. Approved on 13 November 2026 | The cost a text built from many small ones had ("Slower than C") is gone |
| Files read and written whole | `File.readText(path)` gives a `Result<string, IoError>`, `File.readBytes(path)` a `Result<List<byte>, IoError>`; `File.writeText`, `File.writeBytes` (make or empty the file) and `File.appendText` (add at the end, make a missing file) a `Result<void, IoError>`. A path is a `read string`; one with a zero byte in it is `NotFound`, never cut off. `readText` checks the text: a bad byte is `InvalidText(position)` from the start of the file. Approved on 13 November 2026 | The names follow the design's `File.readText`; a path is text, as in C# and Go. A file read piece by piece needs `Closable` and comes later |
| `IoError` | Built in: `NotFound`, `PermissionDenied`, `IsDirectory`, `NoSpace`, `InvalidText(long position)`, `Other(int code)`; `Copyable`, `Formattable`, `Equatable`. The system's codes map the same on Linux and macOS. It does not carry the path. Approved on 13 November 2026. Since 15 November also `Cancelled(Cancelled cancelled)`, for async input and output whose token was cancelled ("A cancellation in a `Result`"), and the network's `ConnectionRefused`, `ConnectionReset`, `AddressInUse` and `HostNotFound` ("The network, as written"), and `LineTooLong` ("The longest line over a connection"); since the seventy-fourth slice `AlreadyExists`, `NotDirectory` and `DirectoryNotEmpty`, and a file on the way of a path (`ENOTDIR`) is `NotDirectory`, where it was `NotFound` (interpreter README, 569); since the seventy-seventh slice `InvalidInput`, for what cannot be handed to the system, as an argument of a program with a zero byte in it (604); since the eightieth slice `MessageTooLong`, a datagram too long to send as one message or for the buffer it is received into (655), and `ENOTCONN` is `ConnectionReset` (660). Written as a sentence in lower case since 20 November (choice 1537): `not found`, `invalid text at byte 812`, `system error 5`, so that a tool's message reads `grep: nope: not found` | The four a program tells apart, and the code for the rest. With the path it would own a text and stop being `Copyable`; the caller has the path for its own error (Rust leaves it out too; Go and C# keep it) |
| The command line | `main(List<string> args)` gets the words after the program's name, without the name (C#'s way); `void main()` stays. In compiled code nothing is copied. A word that is not UTF-8 stops the program before `main`, exit code 1. `sg file.sg a b` runs a program with `a b`. Approved on 13 November 2026 | The list is the program's own. `args[0]` is the first word, not the program |
| Reading the input | `Stdin.readLine(ref line)` puts the next line into `line` without its `\n` or `\r\n` and gives `Result<bool, IoError>`, `false` at the end; `Stdin.readText()` gives the rest. A line that is not UTF-8 is `InvalidText` from the start of the input, is used up and leaves `line` empty. On a terminal what was printed goes out first. `Stdout` and `Stderr` are kept for the writers. Approved on 13 November 2026 | One text for every line, as `reader.readLine` (row "File API"); no loop meets the same error forever. `Stdin` is the name of C, Go, Rust and Zig, the input is often a pipe and not a console, and it will be a reader of the type `File.openText` gives |
| `printError` and `exit` | `printError(x)` writes as `print` does, to the standard error, after what was printed; it gives nothing. `exit(code)` ends the program with a `byte` code after the output goes out, frees nothing and gives no value (`x ?? exit(2)`); in a test it fails the test. Approved on 13 November 2026. A line of `printError` is one write of the system, however long, so lines that several programs append to one file do not mix; a standard error whose reader is gone ends the program by `SIGPIPE` on Linux and macOS, as `print` and C's and Go's programs do, also when the program was started with the signal ignored, and on Windows the line is lost (choice 1601, 20 November; the interpreter went on before, compiled code ended) | There is no one to tell that the standard error failed, and a program whose messages no one reads is ended as one whose output no one reads is. A `byte` makes `exit(256)`, which the system would turn into 0, a compile error. A compiled test runs in a process of its own, where `exit(0)` would pass it |
| Files and input in compiled code | The calls of the system (`open`, `read`, `write`), not `FILE*`; the input in blocks of 64 KB that the program keeps, a line found with `memchr`. Checking UTF-8 passes sixteen bytes at a time while they are ASCII, and from sixteen that hold a byte past ASCII goes straight to it by counting zero bits (choice 1550, 20 November 2026). Measured with `lines`: reading two million lines takes 1.08 times `getline`, which does not check them. Approved on 13 November 2026 | The difference is the guarantee: without the check SG reads faster than `getline` ("Slower than C") |
| Files read piece by piece | `File.openText(path)` gives a `Result<StreamReader, IoError>`, `File.createText(path)` a `Result<StreamWriter, IoError>` (made or emptied). A reader has `readLine(ref line)`, `readText()` and `lines()`; while `lines()` is used the reader cannot be used beside it. A reader has no `close`. A writer has `write(x)` and `writeLine(x)` (what `print` takes), `flush()` and `close()`, each with a `Result<void, IoError>`; writing needs the writer as `ref`. A folder is `IsDirectory` when it is opened. Approved on 13 November 2026 | The rows "File API" and "Close error" made real. A writer that appends piece by piece, and bytes read piece by piece, come later *(14 November: the rows "A writer that appends" and "Bytes piece by piece")* |
| The writer's buffer and its first error | 64 KB, the same in the interpreter and in compiled code: a write that fills it writes it out. The first error is kept, and every later `write`, `flush` and `close` gives it again. A writer that ends without `close` writes its buffer and loses the error; a panic and `exit` write nothing. Approved on 13 November 2026 | A program that reads its own file back sees the same bytes in both. A kept error makes `close` report a full disk even when the writes before it were dropped with `_ =`, as C's `ferror` does |
| Closing a writer, checked | The `Closable` check of the row "Close error" covers a local variable, a parameter taken by value, a name of a pattern and the item of a `for`, at the end of its block, `return`, `break`, `continue`, `exit`, and before the variable gets another writer. Closed on one path only is an error of its own. `return Error(...)` is let go like `try`. Approved on 13 November 2026 | The caller gets the error that made the function stop; without the exemption every `return Error` would need writers closed by hand. `exit` runs nothing after it, so a writer open there would lose its buffer |
| `Stdin`, `Stdout` and `Stderr` are streams | `Stdin` is a `StreamReader`, `Stdout` and `Stderr` are `StreamWriter`s, which every function and test has as `ref` parameters the program gives: `count(ref Stdin)`. They are never moved, given another value or closed, and no variable may have their names. `Stdout` writes where `print` writes, in order; `Stderr` where `printError` does. `print` and `printError` stay. Approved on 13 November 2026 | One function serves a file and the program's own streams. Go has `os.Stdin` as a variable, Rust `io::stdin()`, C# `Console.In` |
| Streams in compiled code | A reader is a block of 64 KB with its file (`Stdin` the same struct over the input); a writer is a text that serves as its block, written with `write` of the system. `Stdout` and `Stderr` go through C's `stdout` and `stderr`, so they keep their order with `print`. Measured with `copy`: a log of 125 MB copied line by line takes 0.93 times `getline` and `fprintf`. Approved on 13 November 2026 | The reading is faster than `getline` even with the UTF-8 check |
| A writer that appends | `File.openAppend(path)` gives a `Result<StreamWriter, IoError>` that writes after what the file holds; a missing file is made, a folder is `IsDirectory`. It is a `StreamWriter` like any other, with the `Closable` check. The file is opened to append, so every block lands at the end even when another program writes to it too. Approved on 14 November 2026 | C#'s `File.AppendText(path)` is this stream, but `File.appendText(path, text)` is the whole write here, and one name cannot mean both without overloading |
| Bytes piece by piece | `reader.readBytes(ref buffer)` puts bytes at the start of a `ref byte[]` and gives how many (`Result<nint, IoError>`); it fills the buffer unless the end comes first, so fewer means the end, and the next call gives 0. `writer.writeBytes(bytes)` writes a `read byte[]` as it is, through the writer's buffer, in order with `write`. Both go through the `StreamReader` and `StreamWriter` of text, so `Stdin`, `Stdout` and `Stderr` read and write bytes too; bytes and lines from one reader share its block, and an `InvalidText` position counts the bytes read as bytes. No type and no opening function of their own. Approved on 14 November 2026 | One meaning for a short count; Rust's `read` and Go's `Read` may give fewer at any time, and a program that needs the buffer full loops. Cost: on a pipe or a terminal the call waits until the buffer is full or the input ends (see "Open questions"). A second reader type or `File.openBytes` would be two ways to do one thing |
| Bytes in compiled code | `readBytes` takes what the block holds first, and a rest of 64 KB or more is read straight into the buffer; `writeBytes` of 64 KB or more writes the block and then the bytes straight. Measured with `bytes`: a log of 125 MB copied in pieces of 4 KB takes 0.94 times `fread` and `fwrite`. Approved on 14 November 2026 | Large reads and writes are not copied twice |
| A width and a letter in `$"..."` | After the value, as in C#: `{value,width:letter}` (`{n,5}`, `{name,-10}`, `{b:X2}`, `{price,8:F2}`). Part of the program, checked at compile time. Inside `{ }` a `:` outside the value's own brackets starts the letter, and a `,` outside them starts the width when a number follows it; otherwise the `,` stays part of a type (`HashMap<string, int>`). Takes up "a format specifier inside `$"..."`", which had been left for after the first version. Approved on 14 November 2026 | `$"..."` is already C#'s. Rust writes `{:>8.2}`, C and Go `%8.2f`. Nothing is read while the program runs |
| The letters | `x` and `X` hex, `b` binary, `D` decimal, `F` digits after the point; only `x` and `X` differ by case. The number after the letter is the digits there are at least for `x`, `X`, `b` and `D` (zeros in front, the sign before them: `{-7:D3}` is `-007`) and the digits after the point for `F`, which must have one. At most 999. A letter that does not fit the value is a compile error with a hint (`{(double)n:F2}`, `{(int)c:X4}`); `N`, `E`, `G`, `C`, `P` and formats made of characters are left out. Approved on 14 November 2026 | `N` puts separators between groups of digits that change from one country to another, and C#'s default for `F` comes from the country settings; a program here writes the same text everywhere |
| A negative number in hex and binary | Written as the bits of its type: `{small:x}` with `sbyte small = -1` is `ff`, with an `int` `ffffffff`. No `0x` is put in front. Approved on 14 November 2026 | C# and Rust write the same |
| Rounding of `F` | The exact value to the nearest, a tie to the even digit: `{2.5:F0}` is `2`, `{0.125:F2}` is `0.12`. `NaN` and the infinities as `print` writes them; `-0.0` and a small negative number keep their minus. Approved on 14 November 2026 | Rust, C's `printf` and .NET since 3.0 give the same digits |
| A width counts characters | Not bytes: `{"Zürich",8}` has two spaces. Positive: spaces in front; negative: after. A longer value is not cut. Every value `print` takes, a struct with its own `format` and a list included. The fill is a space only, no centring, and the width is a number written in the text. No `padLeft`: `$"{name,10}".toString()` is that text. Approved on 14 November 2026 | C#'s alignment. A variable width would be a second way to build the format at run time |
| `parseHex` | `T.parseHex(text)` on every integer type gives `Result<T, NumberError>`: small and capital digits, no `0x`, the sign rule of `parse`, and the value must fit the type (`"80000000"` on an `int` is `OutOfRange`). An error on a fractional type, hint `parse`. Approved on 14 November 2026 | Rust's `from_str_radix` agrees; C#'s `HexNumber` takes the digits as bits. `parse(text, 16)` would be a parameter and a check more for a base that is almost always 16, and `0x` read by `parse` would change what `"0x10"` gives today |
| Writing a number in compiled code | Hex and decimal digits are `printf`'s conversions with the width in them (`%8.4llx`); binary digits by a small function; `F` by a function of the program that rounds exactly in 128 bits (`snprintf` past 18 digits or out of its range); a width on text is one `%*.*s` whose width in bytes counts the characters. Measured with `table`: two million lines take 0.89 times `printf`. Approved on 14 November 2026 *(18 November 2026, the hundred-and-sixteenth slice: the lines the code SG writes, `print`, `printError`, `panic`, `$"..."` and `f.write`, no longer go through C's `printf`: the runtime writes the few conversions that code uses itself, a text with its length, the byte 0 and more than 2 GB included, and a width counted in characters; `table` takes 0.52 times `printf`; choice 1336)* | The first version (1.30 times) wrote `F` with `snprintf` and copied it with `printf`, and put the spaces of a width in a conversion of their own |
| `addAll` | `list.addAll(items)` copies the items of a `read T[]` to the end of the list: `read ys` for a list or an array, a range (`ys[2..]`), `text.asBytes()`. The items must be `Copyable`; on a `List<string>` it is an error whose hint is `add(item.clone())`. Other languages' names (`extend`, `addRange`, `extendFromSlice`, ...) are errors whose hint is `addAll`. Approved on 14 November 2026 | The design's name, already in the HTTP example (05); Java and Kotlin call it the same. A copy of `Copyable` items is a copy of their bytes, so the call is one `memcpy`; for text it would be an allocation per item that the call does not show. Moving every item of one list into another is a method of its own, not here yet |
| A list is not added to itself | `xs.addAll(read xs)` and `xs.addAll(xs[1..])` are compile errors; the hint is `xs.add(xs[i])`. Approved on 14 November 2026 | The rule of a text written into itself: the list may move to a larger block while it grows, and the items would be read from the old one. Rust refuses the same and has `extend_from_within`, which can come when a program needs it |
| `sort` and `reverse` on a slice | An array and a `ref T[]` have `sort` and `reverse`; the items change places where they are, in the list or the array the slice looks at. Through a `read T[]` it is an error whose hint is `ref xs`. The sort is the list's: stable, and in compiled code it takes a block of the slice's size from the heap while it sorts. Approved on 14 November 2026 *(18 November 2026, choice 1337: the interpreter sorts with the merge sort of compiled code, step for step, so a `compare` that gives no single order leaves the same items in the same places in both, and panics in neither)* | They change the order and not the number of items, which a window can do and `add` or `clear` cannot. A sort that does not allocate can come when a measurement asks for it |
| A range in front of `sort` | `xs[2..5].sort()` and `xs[..3].reverse()` lend the range as `ref`, with what `ref xs[2..5]` needs (written, not `readonly`, not borrowed, not gone over by a `for`). A bare range anywhere else only looks. Approved on 14 November 2026 | A variable in front of a method is lent as the method needs with nothing written (`xs.sort()`), and the range follows that rule; `(ref xs[2..5]).sort()` would need brackets. Rust writes `xs[2..5].sort()` too |
| `addAll` and slice sorting in compiled code | `addAll` tests for room once, grows the block to twice its room or to what is needed, and copies with `memcpy`; `sort` on a slice is the list's merge sort on the slice's pointer and count, `reverse` swaps from both ends. Measured with `blocks`: a gigabyte gathered in pieces of 1 to 255 bytes takes the time of `memcpy` in C (1.00); one `add` per byte takes ten times as long. Approved on 14 November 2026 | What C does by hand |
| `copyFrom` | `dst.copyFrom(src)` copies the items of a `read T[]` over as many: `dst` is a list, an array, a `ref T[]` or a range in front of it (`buf[0..n].copyFrom(...)`, lent as `ref` as in front of `sort`), the items `Copyable`. Lengths that differ are a panic (`` `copyFrom` is given 2 item(s) to copy over 3 ``). The two may be windows of one place (`buf[0..n].copyFrom(buf[k..k + n])`): the items given are read before any is written over, as C's `memmove`, the one exception to "a `ref` borrow is the only one", since `Copyable` items hold nothing a write could free (1609). `copyFromSlice` and `copy_from_slice` are errors whose hint is `copyFrom`. Compiled as a test of the lengths and one `memmove`. The loops of `std` that copied item by item (deflate's window and input, inflate's output, the slots of the Pike machine, SHA-256's block, the groups of an IPv6 address) are calls of it. Approved on 20 and 21 November 2026 (1597, 1609) | Rust's `copy_from_slice` panics on lengths that differ and `copy_within` copies inside one slice; Zig's `@memcpy` checks the lengths and refuses overlap, `@memmove` allows it; Go's `copy` copies the shorter length and allows overlap. One method that allows overlap keeps the language one name smaller than Rust and Zig, and a count that differs is a mistake Go would hide |
| Patterns inside patterns | Inside the parentheses of a variant goes a pattern where a name goes: a variant with its own parentheses (`Add(Number(0), right)`), a variant that carries nothing (`Paint(Red)`), a number, a character, a text (`Word("let")`), `_` or a name, at any depth, in `match` and in `is`, through a `T*` too. An arm with a pattern inside does not settle its variant: the arms below still run for its other values. Ownership is the rule of `match`: on an owned value the names take what they name, what no name takes is dropped and a box that was looked into is given back; on a borrowed value the names borrow, and through a `read` field they are `read`. No pattern inside a value that may be `null` (that came in the forty-fourth slice: "`null` as a pattern"). Approved on 14 November 2026 | They cost nothing at run time: an arm tests what a person would test by hand. This takes up "nested patterns" from the things left for after the first version. C#, Rust and Swift have them |
| A name alone inside the parentheses | It is the variant when the enum of that value has a variant of that name, otherwise a name that takes the value. A name that starts with a capital letter and is not a variant there is an error (`Paint(Rd)`: hint `Red`). `var` is not written inside the parentheses. Approved on 14 November 2026 | A mistyped variant must not quietly take every value (the row "Patterns"). Variants begin with a capital letter and names with a small one; the rule leans on that only here. Rust has the same question and answers it with a warning |
| What a `match` misses | With patterns inside, a `match` that does not take every value is a compile error that names one it misses (`does not handle Add(Name(_), _)`), and an arm the arms above take together is an error too. Without patterns inside the messages stay as they were. An arm with `when` takes nothing for this. Approved on 14 November 2026 | The row "`match`": a new variant shows every place that needs a look; with patterns inside the value that is missing is named, not only the variant. The search is Maranget's, the one Rust uses, and runs in the checker only |
| `match` on text | On a `string`, a `read string` and a `ref string`: the arms are texts as they are written, `_` and `var name`; `_` or `var name` is required and the same text twice is an error, as on a number. `$"..."` is not a pattern. A `match` on an owned `string` moves it, as every `match` does (`match read text` keeps it). Approved on 14 November 2026 | A lexer or a command line sorts words this way; a chain of `==` says the same at more length. No new rule for ownership |
| Methods on enums | Written in the enum body, after or between the variants, with `this`, `read this`, `ref this` or none, and `public`, as in a struct. A line that is a name alone or a name followed by `(` is a variant, any other line a function.
