A function cannot have a variant's name; an enum has no fields (the hint is to put the value in each variant). `Shape.unit()` calls a function without `this`. Approved on 14 November 2026 | The same place and the same rules as in a struct, so nothing new is learned. Rust writes them in an `impl` block, C# on a record |
| Interfaces on an enum | An enum writes its own `format` (`Formattable`), `equals` (`Equatable`) and `compare` (`Comparable`, which needs it) under the rules a struct has; a `Hashable` enum with its own `equals` or `hash` stays out, as for a struct. Approved on 14 November 2026 | Settles the row "`Comparable` is written": an enum could not be `Comparable` until it had methods |
| Patterns and enum methods in compiled code | An arm with patterns inside is one condition: the tests of the tags and the values joined by `&&` in the order they are written, so a value is read only after the tag of its variant was tested; each arm tests from the top. A text arm is a test of the length and then of the bytes. A method of an enum is a C function like a struct's, and the helpers that write, compare and order an enum call its own methods. Measured with `keywords`: a million words sorted forty times by a `match` with twelve texts take 1.03 times a chain of `memcmp` in C. Approved on 14 November 2026 | What C does by hand. A decision tree, or a switch on the length of a text, can come if a measurement asks for it |
| Ranges in patterns | `'a'..='z'`, `0..=9`, `-5..=-1`: from the first value to the last, both taken, on an integer and on a `char`, also inside the parentheses of a variant. `..` in a pattern is an error whose hint is `..=`; `..=` outside a pattern is an error whose hint is `a..b + 1`. The ends are numbers or characters as they are written, the first not greater than the last, each fitting the type; no constant (`int.MaxValue`) and no open end (`5..=`). Decided before the forty-fourth slice and approved with it on 14 November 2026 | `..` stops before its end everywhere else (`for i in 0..n`, `text[a..b]`), so `'a'..'z'` would quietly leave out `z`. Rust wrote patterns this way until 1.80 |
| `or` in patterns | `Add(l, _) or Mul(l, _)`, `'+' or '-'`, `Word("if" or "else")`, in an arm and inside the parentheses, in `match` and in `is`. Every pattern of an `or` names the same values with the same types; a pattern that the arms above and the patterns before it take is an error (inside the parentheses that is not looked for yet). A line may end with `or`. `or` is a keyword; `|` or `||` between patterns is an error whose hint is `or`. Approved on 14 November 2026 | C#'s word: SG writes its patterns as C# does (`is not null`), and `|` is an operator on numbers in SG, so a pattern and a value look different (`x is A or B \|\| ready`). First written `|` as in Rust, changed during the slice |
| `null` as a pattern | On a `T?`, as a field or as the value of the `match`, `null` takes `null` and any other pattern takes a value that is there and tests it as on a `T` (`Node(null, Leaf(label))`, `match shape { null => ... Circle(r) => ... }`). `null` on a type without `?` is an error. A `match` on a `T?` takes `null` with an arm or with `_`. Approved on 14 November 2026 | Takes away "no pattern inside a value that may be `null`" of the row "Patterns inside patterns". C# and Swift test `null` in a pattern too |
| A name below `null` | In a `match` on a `T?`, a name in an arm under an arm `null` without `when` has the type `T` (`match s { null => 0  var text => text.length() }`); above it, or without such an arm, the name is a `T?`. Approved on 14 November 2026 | The same narrowing `if s is not null` gives, and nothing to unwrap by hand |
| `true` and `false` as patterns | `match done { true => ... false => ... }`, `Flag(true)`; both arms make the `match` whole without `_`. Approved on 14 November 2026 | A `bool` is a type of two values like an enum of two variants |
| What the search names | The search of "What a `match` misses" knows `or`, ranges, `null`, `true` and `false`, and runs for every `match` on a number, a character, a `bool` and a `T?`. The values of a number type are cut where the arms' ranges begin and end, so the search stays small however wide the type. A `match` on a number or a character that misses values says which (`no arm takes 128..=255`; the ends of the wide types by name: `int.MinValue..=-1`). Approved on 14 November 2026 | The value that is missing is named, as for an enum. The row "`match` on a number" changed with it: arms that take every value need no `_` |
| The rest of the patterns in compiled code | A range is `c >= lo && c <= hi`, without the half whose end is the first or the last value of the type; an `or` is `\|\|`; on a `T?` the flag (or the null pointer) is tested before the value. An `or` that names nothing drops an owned value as `_` would; an `or` with names is opened into one pattern for each way to match, the names are declared once, and the way that matched sets them and drops what it leaves. Measured with `classes`: ten million bytes sorted twenty times into the classes of a lexer take 0.87 times a chain of `if`s in C. Approved on 14 November 2026 | What C does by hand. Many `or`s inside one pattern open into their product; no limit is set, as none was needed yet |
| The names of `is` along `&&` | An `is` on the left of `&&` names values for its right side and for the block of the `if` or `while` whose condition the chain is: `if int.parse(read text) is Ok(value) && value > 0 { ... }`, `while tokenAt(i) is Word(w) && w.length() > 0 { ... }`. A chain anywhere else (`return s is Circle(r) && r > 10`, a `var`, a `when`, an `assert`, inside `!( ... )`) gives its names to its own right side and ends them with itself. `else` and the code after the `if` do not see them, nor does `\|\|`; `(a && b) && c` is `a && b && c`. Decided before the forty-fifth slice and approved with it on 14 November 2026 | C#'s rule (`if (o is int n && n > 0)`); Rust 2024 chains `if let` with `&&` the same way. Until then the names existed only when the `is` was the whole condition |
| Narrowing along `&&` and `\|\|` | On the left of `&&`, `x is not null` makes `x` a `T` on the right and in the block; on the left of `\|\|`, `x is null` makes it a `T` on the right. An `if` whose condition is a `\|\|` chain of `is null` tests narrows them in `else`, and after the `if` when the block always leaves (`if a is null \|\| b is null { return }`); a `&&` chain of `is not null` tests narrows them after the `if` when `else` always leaves. Only the parts of the chain itself count, not `!(x is null)` or a test inside the other operator. Approved on 14 November 2026 | The `&&` half was decided before the slice; the `\|\|` half is its mirror image, added during it. C# narrows the same way |
| No names where nothing sees them | An `is` that names values and is joined by `\|\|` is an error whose note says that the block also runs when the test does not hold; an `is` with names under `!` or as a whole value (`var round = s is Circle(r)`) is an error whose hint is `_` or `match`. A name of an `is` used in `else`, after the `if` or after the chain is an error that points at the `is` that named it and says where its names are seen. Approved on 14 November 2026 | Before, the error said only that the names exist when `is` is the whole condition, and a name used too late got a guess at a near name |
| Chains in compiled code | A chain whose `is` names values declares the names before the statement and sets them inside the condition where the test holds: `(s.tag == 0 && (r = &s.u.v0.m_radius, 1)) && *r > 10`; an `or` with names sets them by the way that matched. A value made by a call at the start of the chain stays until the block is done; an `is` with names on a value made by a call after the first `&&` is refused with "not in the C back end yet" (the interpreter runs it). Narrowing writes nothing new. Approved on 14 November 2026 | One pointer set in the condition, the C a person writes with the same names; not measured on its own |
| The order of an assignment | An assignment computes its right side first, then finds the place and writes it, for `=` and for `+=` and the others: `xs[place()] = value()` calls `value` first (see "Order of evaluation"). Decided during the forty-sixth slice and approved with it on 14 November 2026 | Rust's order; C# and Java find the place first. The place is found right before it is written, so its address cannot be stale when the right side grows a list, and the right side can be made from the value it replaces. Compiled code had taken the place first, so the two programs printed in different orders and an item could be written through a moved address |
| A borrow read by the right side | A borrow that the right side of an assignment reads last ends before the write: `b = Box { n = r.n + 1 }` where `r` borrows `b`, `while s is Circle(r) { s = Shape.Circle(r + 1) }`, `t = part.clone()` where `part` is a range of `t`. Approved on 14 November 2026 | Rust accepts the same. They were refused with "`b` cannot be changed here" |
| The place after the right side | What the place of an assignment uses counts as used after the right side: a borrow read by the place (`counts[r.length()] = grow(ref xs)`) or written through (`r.x = grow(ref ps)`) cannot have its owner changed by the right side, and the error has a note saying the right side is computed first. Inside the place the parts keep their order from left to right. Approved on 14 November 2026 | Follows from the order: the place is found after the right side, so a borrow it uses must still hold then |
| Assignments in compiled code | When the place is more than a variable, the right side is computed into a C variable first, and the address of the item is taken after it. Approved on 14 November 2026 | Nothing is paid: the C compiler removes the variable, and the measured programs that write items compile to the same instructions, up to the names of registers |
| `replaceAll` | `text.replaceAll(old, new)` gives a new text with every place of `old` replaced by `new`; the text stays as it was. Not `replace(old, new)`: `x.replace(value)` already puts a new value in a place on every type, so the same name would change the text with one argument and not with two. `line.replace(a, b)` is an error whose hint is `replaceAll`. Decided before the forty-seventh slice and approved with it on 14 November 2026 | Go's `ReplaceAll`; C# and Rust say `Replace`/`replace`, where the name is free |
| Replacing: what is looked for | `old` and `new` are each a `char` or a `read string` (not a `byte`). The places are found from the left and do not overlap (`"aaa".replaceAll("aa", "b")` gives `"ba"`), and what was put in is not searched again. An empty `old` is refused as the separator of `split` is: a constant `""` is a compile error, an empty text at run time a panic. Approved on 14 November 2026 | Rust and Go put `new` between every two characters, C# and Zig stop; a loop over `chars()` says the first plainly |
| `repeat` | `text.repeat(n)` gives the text `n` times over as a new text. `n` is a `nint`, as an index is. A negative constant is a compile error, a negative number at run time a panic, and a text too big for the memory a panic that says so. A `char` has no `repeat` (`c.toString().repeat(n)`). Approved on 14 November 2026 | Rust's and Go's `repeat`; the panic in place of an abort holds in both programs |
| A new text that is not used | A call of `replaceAll` or `repeat` whose result is not used is a compile error (`line.replaceAll('\t', " ")` alone changes nothing); the hint assigns the new text back when the text is a variable or a field. The other methods that give a new value (`trim`, `clone`, `toString`) are left as they were for now. Approved on 14 November 2026; the forty-eighth slice took the rule to every built-in method that only gives a value ("The value of a built-in method") | Rust warns (`#[must_use]`), Zig refuses every value thrown away; SG has no warnings. No cost at run time |
| Replacing and repeating in compiled code | `replaceAll` finds each place with the search of `indexOf` (its two rarest bytes, then `memcmp`, choice 1549) and copies the pieces into a block that doubles when it is full; `repeat` allocates the whole size once and copies what it has written after itself. Approved on 14 November 2026 | Rewriting lines with them takes 0.94 times the same program in C |
| The value of a built-in method | Every built-in method that only gives a value is a compile error when the value is not used: `line.trim()`, `name.clone()`, `count.toString()`, `distance.abs()`, `xs.contains(3)`, `xs.length()`, `total.tryAdd(1)` and `string.join(", ", read names)` alone do nothing. It takes the methods that give a new value and the ones that answer a question alike, so it is one rule with no list to learn. `_ = xs.contains(3)` throws a value away on purpose. Approved on 14 November 2026 | Zig refuses every value thrown away; Rust marks most of these `#[must_use]` and warns. No cost at run time: the checker alone |
| Methods that change and give | *Replaced on 16 November 2026 by "Every value of a call" below.* A built-in method that changes its value and gives one too may stand alone: `xs.removeLast()`, the `add` and `remove` of a `HashSet`, the `remove` and `getOrAdd` of a `HashMap`, `next` on an iterator. The change is what they are called for. Approved on 14 November 2026 | As in Rust, where `Vec::pop` and `HashSet::insert` may stand alone |
| Functions of your own | *Replaced on 16 November 2026 by "Every value of a call" below.* A function or a method you write may stand alone even when it gives a value: it can print, change through `ref` or stop, and the checker does not know which ones do nothing else. A `Result` stays an error everywhere. Approved on 14 November 2026 | Zig asks for `_ =` here too; SG keeps the error for what it knows does nothing |
| The words of an unused value | A method that gives a new value of the type of the variable or field it is called on says "it leaves `x` as it was", and the hint assigns it back (`x = x.abs()`); `trim`, which gives a look at the same text, has `var text = line.trim()` as the hint; the others name what was not used ("the copy that `clone` makes", "the value that `contains` gives"). Approved on 14 November 2026 | The hint is the line to write |
| Narrowing under `!` | A condition is read as a whole: each says which variables are not `null` where it holds and which where it does not. `!` swaps the two, so `if !(x is null)` narrows like `x is not null`; where `a && b` holds both sides held, where it fails only what both sides say when they fail is known, and `\|\|` is the other way round (`!(a is null \|\| b is null)` narrows both, `!(a is null && b is null)` neither). The same reading serves a part of a chain, `else` and the code after an `if` whose branch leaves. Approved on 14 November 2026 | C# reads a condition the same way. Until then `!(x is null)` and a test inside the other operator narrowed nothing. No cost at run time |
| Narrowing after a `while` | A loop ends when its condition fails, so after `while x is null { ... }` the variable is a `T`, and after `while a is null \|\| b is null { ... }` both are; `while x is null && tries < 3` narrows nothing. A `break` that leaves the loop, wherever it stands, turns it off; one of an inner loop does not. A variable left a `T?` by a `break` gets the usual error; a note that names the `break` is not here yet. Approved on 14 November 2026 | C# does the same; Rust has no `null` to narrow |
| A `break` inside an expression | The check for a `break` in a loop looks inside every expression (a `match` arm in `1 + match k { ... }`, the condition of an `if`, the range of an inner loop). Before, such a `break` was missed: `while true { ... }` counted as never ending, and a function ending with it was accepted without a `return` and gave whatever was in a register in compiled code. Approved on 14 November 2026 | A hole found during the forty-ninth slice, closed in it |
| A variable the condition empties | What a whole condition says (in `else`, after the `if`, after a `while`) leaves out the variables the condition empties with `take` or assigns in a `match` arm: `if b is null \|\| b.take() is null { return }` does not narrow `b`. Along the chain itself `take` already ended the narrowing. Approved on 14 November 2026 | There since the forty-fifth slice: `b` was read as a value after `take` emptied it |
| The names of `is not` | The names of `x is not Circle(r)`, and of `!(x is Circle(r))`, are seen where the test fails: on the right of `\|\|`, in `else`, after an `if` whose block leaves (`return`, `break`, `continue`, `panic`, `exit`), and after a `while` that only its test ends: `if int.parse(read text) is not Ok(value) { return -1 }` and then `value`. A `while` with a `break` sees them nowhere, and the error points at the `break`. Approved on 14 November 2026 | C# 9 (`if (o is not int n) return;`); Rust writes it `let ... else`, which moves the value out where `is` only looks. Until then `is not` with names was an error |
| Names after an `if` whose `else` leaves | `if s is Circle(r) { ... } else { return 0 }` and then `r`: the code after the `if` sees what the way that reaches it is sure of, as narrowing already did. Approved on 14 November 2026 | The mirror image of "The names of `is not`" |
| Names along `\|\|` | On the left of `\|\|` an `is not` names values for its right side (`if r is not Ok(n) \|\| n < 0 { return }`); after the `if` the names of every `is not` of the chain are seen. An `is not` with names on the left of `&&` is an error. A `!` around a whole chain is not read for names. Approved on 14 November 2026 | C# also reads `!(a && b)`; left for later |
| Where the names after a statement live | In the scope that holds the `if` or `while`, to its end, as a `var`; a name already declared in that scope is an error that points at both. They stay borrows of the tested value. Approved on 14 November 2026 | In an inner block a name of a block around them is an error, as it is for a `var` (since 17 November 2026: "A name that hides another") |
| A value with no name after an `if` | The value with no name that such an `if` looks at (`if fetch() is not Text(body) { return }`) stays until the block around the `if` ends; in compiled code its drop moves there. After a `while`, a name that borrows from such a value is an error: the loop makes it again in every round. Approved on 14 November 2026 | What lets `int.parse(read text) is not Ok(value)` be written without a `var` first |
| Names in compiled code | `x is not Circle(r)` is the test of `x is Circle(r)` that sets the names, under a `!`; the names are declared before the statement (before the loop for a `while`). A value made by a call after the first `&&` or `\|\|`, for an `is` with names, is compiled when it owns no memory: declared before the statement and made inside the condition. Approved on 14 November 2026 | One that owns memory is still refused ("not in the C back end yet") |
| Numbers out of bytes | `T.fromLittleEndian(bytes)` and `T.fromBigEndian(bytes)` on `short`, `ushort`, `int`, `uint`, `long` and `ulong`: a function of the type, as `parse` is, that takes a `read byte[]` and gives the number the bytes write, the lowest byte first or the highest first; a signed type reads the highest bit as the sign. Approved on 14 November 2026 | Rust `u64::from_le_bytes`, Go `binary.LittleEndian.Uint64`, C# `BinaryPrimitives.ReadUInt64LittleEndian`. The name says the order in whole words and leaves out "bytes", which the argument already is. What a file format needs (a GIF keeps its sizes in two bytes, the lowest first) |
| Exactly as many bytes | The window holds exactly as many bytes as the type takes (2, 4 or 8). A width the program shows (an array, `xs[..k]`, `xs[a..b]` with constants, `xs[at..at + k]` where `at` is a variable or a field of one) and that is another is a compile error whose hint is the window of the right width; a width that depends on data panics with both numbers. Approved on 14 November 2026 | Rust (`[u8; 8]`) and Zig (`*const [8]u8`) want exactly so many; Go and C# read the first ones and leave the rest. A window of another width is nearly always a wrong position or a wrong type. Cost: the width is written (`data[at..at + 8]`), which also shows the reader what is read |
| Numbers into bytes | `n.toLittleEndian()` and `n.toBigEndian()` give the bytes of the number as a `byte[N]` where the value is; nothing is allocated. `out.addAll(read n.toBigEndian())` puts them into a list. Approved on 14 November 2026 | Rust `to_le_bytes`. Rust's `to_le` gives a number with its bytes turned around; SG has no such method, so the shorter name is free for the bytes |
| What has neither | `byte` and `sbyte` (one byte has no order), `nint` and `nuint` (an address has the size the machine gives it, a number kept in bytes has a size of its own) and the fractional types have neither function nor method; each is an error with a hint (`data[i]`, the `long` form and a cast). The names other languages use (`from_le_bytes`, `toBytes`) get the hint of the name here; a call whose value is not used is an error, as for every built-in method that only gives a value. Approved on 14 November 2026 | A fractional number out of bytes is not here yet |
| Bytes and numbers in compiled code | One C function for each type and order: the count is compared with the width, then `memcpy` into a `uint64_t` (or narrower) and a byte swap where the machine keeps its bytes the other way. Where the window was cut with a constant width just before, the C compiler removes the test; writing is one store into the array. Approved on 14 November 2026 | Measured: XXH64 reads with it in 1.03 times the time of C, and its overflow-checked build went from 3.6 to 1.23 times C, since the overflow tests of `at + 8` no longer stand between eight reads |
| An index written `a + c` | In a checked build, an index of a list, an array, a slice or a text, or an end of a range, written `a + c` (or `c + a`) with `c` a constant and the sum signed, is tested for overflow with the place: the sum wraps, one that overflowed is negative and the check of the place refuses it, and only then is the overflow told, with the message, the numbers and the line the test of the sum gave. The program stops where and as it did; nothing a program can see changes, and a release build is as before. Approved on 14 November 2026 | One comparison where there were two; XXH64's checked build went from 0.154 s to 0.129 s, the time of its release build |
| What keeps its test | `xs[i - 1]` (a difference that overflows wraps to a large positive number, refused only while no length nears 2^63: a fact about machines, not a promise of the language), `a + b` with two variables, an unsigned index (it wraps to a small number), `i * 2`, and a sum that is not the place itself (handed to a function, compared). Approved on 14 November 2026 | Measured: those cost nothing in `hash`. The checked `buffer` and `vector` pay for the tests of a sum kept in a loop (`sum += b`), which are real; the "Slower than C" list keeps them |
| The start of a range | The overflow of the start is found after the end is computed, so the start is tested with the range only where the end can neither stop the program nor do anything else: a constant, a variable, a field, or such a value plus a constant (`xs[at + 1..at + 3]`: both sums with the range, the start's overflow told first). `xs[at + 1..loud(2)]` keeps the test of its start. Approved on 14 November 2026 | Keeps the order of evaluation: nothing prints before the overflow is told |
| Index sums in compiled code | Each such place has a `static const sg_sum` (the constant, the type, the line of the sum) and calls the helper it called with one pointer more (`sg_ats_...`, `sg_cuts_...`, `sg_slices`); the pointer is read only on the way out, where `a` is the wrapped sum less `c` in the type of the sum. Approved on 14 November 2026 | Fifteen tests, each its own process, compare the interpreter and compiled code, also under the sanitizers |
| A sum kept in a loop | In a checked build, before a loop that adds to a sum starts, compiled code works out from the sum's value how many rounds cannot overflow it (the room to each end of its type divided by the most a round adds or takes). The loop is written twice: the first copy runs that many rounds without the test, the second takes over at the same position with it, so a sum that does overflow stops the program at the same step, with the same message and line. Nothing a program can see changes, and a release build is as before. Approved on 14 November 2026 | The checked `buffer` went from 0.733 s to 0.290 s (C 0.282 s), the checked `vector` from 0.682 s to 0.529 s; gcc 16: `buffer` 0.91 s to 0.62 s |
| Which sums | An integer variable declared before the loop, which the body changes only with `+=` and `-=`, every value a constant or of a narrower integer type (`long += byte`, `int += 1`). A sum assigned otherwise or lent as `ref`, a `ref` parameter, a field, `sum = sum + x` and a value of the sum's own type keep their test. Approved on 14 November 2026 | A `short` adding `byte`s has room for 128 rounds; the rest runs in the second copy |
| Which loops | `for i in a..b` and `for` over a list, an array, a slice, a map or a set, lent or handed over. Not a `while` or a `for` over the parts of a text (no count before they start), not a loop with a loop inside (a step runs at most once a round, no loop is written more than twice), and not a loop whose body calls anything: a call keeps the C compiler from doing several rounds at once anyway, and a second copy may make it stop putting the function where it is called. Approved on 14 November 2026 | Without the last rule `classes` (checked) went from 0.30 s to 0.40 s |
| Two copies in compiled code | The count is `sg_upto` of the position and `sg_steps` of each sum (the least of them), computed once on 128 bits; the first copy is `for (; at < stop && at < end; at++)` with the sums written as a release build writes them, and a `break` in it leaves both with a `goto` to a label after the second. Approved on 14 November 2026 | Eleven tests, each its own process, compare the interpreter and compiled code, also under the sanitizers; every loop of every program written twice passed the whole suite |
| The variable of `for i in a..b` | A copy of the count made at the start of each round: the body may change it, and that changes only the round it is in (`for i in 0..4 { i += 1; print(i) }` prints 1, 2, 3, 4), as Go's `for i := range n` and Rust's `for mut i`. The interpreter already worked so; compiled code changed the count and printed 1 and 3 until the fifty-third slice. Approved on 14 November 2026 | C#'s `for` would print 1 and 3; its `foreach` variable cannot be assigned |
| `shared` is read until `checked` | What a `shared` value points to is only read: assigning a field, `ref node.value`, a method that takes `ref this`, a method that changes a collection in it and lending it as `ref T` are compile errors whose note names `shared checked T`; so are moving a field out (hint `read node.name`) and a method that takes `this`. A `shared Node` lends what it points to where a `read Node` goes, as a `T*` does. Approved on 14 November 2026 | The design's error "Write through `shared`"; until `checked` comes nothing is changed through several owners |
| `shared` and `weak` in a type | In front of the name, after `read` or `ref`: `read shared Node`. `shared Node?` is a `shared Node` that may be missing. A `shared` cannot hold a `T*`, a `T?`, a `shared` or `weak` value, a slice, or a type that carries a borrow; any other value can be shared (`new shared 5`). Approved on 14 November 2026 | The `?` goes outside, as in `shared checked DNode? next` |
| `same` looks | `same(a, b)` does not move its arguments, with or without `read`, as `print` does. Both are `shared` values of one type; a `T?` gets the hint to test it, a `weak` the hint `share w`. `==` between two `shared` values whose type is not `Equatable` has the hint `same(a, b)`. Approved on 14 November 2026 | The cities example writes `same(read cur, goal)`; both forms work |
| A `shared` value is not a key | Never the key of a `HashMap` or an item of a `HashSet`, whatever it points to; the hint is a field that tells the values apart (`HashSet<int>`, `city.id`). It counts for `==`, not for `Hashable`, as a collection does. Approved on 14 November 2026 | What it points to will change through `checked`, and a key that changes breaks the table. The design refused `HashSet<CityRef>` only because `City` was not `Hashable` |
| A `weak` value shows nothing | A `weak` value cannot be printed, compared or reached into (`w.value`); each error has the hint `share w`. A struct with a `weak` field is not `Formattable` or `Equatable` field by field. Approved on 14 November 2026 | What it looks at may be gone, and following it may come back to where it started |
| Type alias details | `using` at the top level of a file, one on a line, `public` or not as a type is. It builds what its type builds (`P(1, 2)`, `new NodeRef(args)`; `NodeRef { ... }` without `new` has the hint to add it). It cannot be a borrow, generic, the name of another type, come back to itself, or get `shared` around one that is `shared` already; a collection is built by its type, not its alias. Compiled code takes an alias as the type it names. `shared`, `weak`, `share` and `using` are words of the language, not names. Approved on 14 November 2026 | The row "Type alias" did not say how an alias builds a value |
| A moved place is empty in the interpreter | A value moved out of a variable or a field leaves it empty in the interpreter too, as in compiled code. Approved on 14 November 2026 | A copy left behind would count as one more owner of a `shared` value and keep a `weak` one alive |
| The leak report comes with `checked` | The report of the row "`shared` cycle" is built with `checked` (the second part of the work on several owners), not with `shared` and `weak`. Approved on 14 November 2026 | Without `checked` no two values can point to each other, so nothing can leak and the report could not be tested |
| `shared checked` as written | `shared checked T` and `weak checked T`; `checked` comes after `shared` or `weak`, `new shared checked x` makes one and an alias of it builds it (`new FolderRef(...)`). `checked` alone (`checked int x`) is an error that says where it goes; `shared T` and `shared checked T` are two types. Approved on 14 November 2026 | `checked` on a value with one owner (Rust's `RefCell` outside `Rc`) is not needed yet: `ref` already changes such a value |
| How long a `checked` borrow lasts | A read or a write is checked once and borrows nothing after it (`cur.bytes += amount`). A place lent to a call is borrowed until the call returns, or until its line ends when the result borrows from it; the head of a `for` for the loop; the value a `match` or an `is` with names looks into for the whole `match` or `if`; an `as` block for the block. A borrow also covers each `shared checked` value on the way to it (`read a.next.items` borrows `a` too). Narrows the row "`checked` borrow duration". Approved on 14 November 2026 | Taken word for word, "for the duration of the expression" makes the design's own `cur.bytes += amount` conflict with itself. Covering the way to a value keeps it from being freed while it is lent |
| Two steps for a receiver | The receiver of a method that changes it is lent to read while the arguments are computed, then to change: `a.items.add(a.items.length())` works; an argument still lent when the method starts stops the program. Approved on 14 November 2026 | Rust's two-phase borrows; the same line works on a plain list |
| A certain `checked` conflict | Through the same variable and the same fields in front of the value (`folder`, `a.next`), while a borrow that does not allow the access is in progress: a compile error that says the line would stop the program every time it ran, with a hint by what holds the borrow (loop, block, `match`, call). An item of a list (`xs[i]`) is left to run time. Approved on 14 November 2026 | The row "`checked` conflict", made exact |
| A `checked` conflict at run time | Through another variable: "this `Folder` is being read, so it cannot be changed here", with the line of the access and the line where the borrow began. Approved on 14 November 2026 | The row "`checked` conflict" asked for both lines |
| A borrow does not outlive its line | Keeping a borrow of what a `shared checked` value holds in a variable (`var kids = read folder.children`, hint `read folder as value { ... }`), assigning it to a place, returning it, or seeing the names of a condition after its `if` is a compile error. A result that borrows (`map.getOrAdd(key, 0)`) is used inside `ref x as m { }`. Approved on 14 November 2026 | The run-time borrow ends with its line; a name that kept it would look at what may be changed or freed |
| The `as` blocks | `read x as n { }` and `ref x as n { }` open a `shared checked` value for the block; `read ... as` also a plain `shared` one. Any other value, a `T?` and a `weak` value are errors with a hint. The name borrows for the block, so the value is not moved or assigned in it. Approved on 14 November 2026 | The form of `lock x as c { }` |
| `this` with a type, details | `read FolderRef this` borrows the owner, `FolderRef this` takes one; `ref` is an error. The type is `shared` or `shared checked` around the method's own struct, or an alias of it. Called on anything else the error names the type, hint `new FolderRef(...)`; a method that takes the owner, called on a borrow, has the hint `share`. The method does not open the value for the call. Approved on 14 November 2026 | Code that walks up the tree from the value does not conflict with itself |
| What `checked` changes | As `ref` does: fields, a method that takes `ref this`, the methods that change a collection, `for x in ref`, `ref a` where a `ref T` goes (`read a` there gets the hint `ref a`); also through a `read FolderRef`, since what is borrowed is the owner. A field is not moved out (hint `replace` or `take`), and a method that takes `this` by value is not called through it. Approved on 14 November 2026 | The other owners still look at the value, so it is never left with a hole |
| Names of `match` and `is` borrow | Of a `Copyable` value too: `if d.shape is Circle(r) { d.shape = ... }` is an error; without names the value is read once. A `match` on a field that owns needs `read`, as on a plain `shared` value. `print(a)`, `$"{a}"` and `a == b` read it for the call or the line. Approved on 14 November 2026 | One rule for every name a pattern gives |
| The leak report, details | Every `shared` value, `checked` or not, still alive when a program ends (after `main`'s values are gone) or when a test that passed ends: how many, where most were made (and the next place), the field through which they hold each other, and a hint with that field's type written with `weak`, aliases spelled out. A program that leaks ends with exit code 1, a test that leaks fails; nothing is reported after a panic or `exit`. In the interpreter until compiled code has `shared` values. Approved on 14 November 2026 | The row "`shared` cycle"; a leak that only prints would pass unnoticed in a test run |
| The cities without a lambda | The example's `remove` builds the list of the cities to keep with a `for` and `same`, since lambdas were removed on 12 November. Approved on 14 November 2026 | The rest of the example runs as written |
| A `shared` value in C | A pointer to the value, with the count of its owners and the count of its `weak` lookers in front of it (the owners together count as one looker, as in Rust's `Arc`). `shared T?` and `weak T?` are the same pointer, `NULL` for `null`; `share w` adds an owner only while there is one. Approved on 14 November 2026 (choice 347) | A `shared T` is the size of a pointer, and what it holds is reached as through a `T*` |
| Atomic counts, measured | The counts stay atomic, as "Shared ownership" decided; in the folder tree they are about 0.010 s of the 0.033 s between SG and C (1.53 times). A count that is not atomic while a program has one thread is a candidate in "Slower than C". Approved on 14 November 2026 (choices 348, 354) | Paid ahead of threads; the reverse would be the hard direction |
| Borrows of `checked` in C | A `shared checked` value has the borrows of it in progress in front of its counts (16 bytes). A borrow that lasts lives in the frame of the function that lent it and is let go where the checker said it ends and on every way out (`return`, `break`, `continue`, `try`); a conflict panics with the interpreter's two lines. The checks stay in a release build. Approved on 14 November 2026 (choice 349) | Like the check of an index: it keeps memory safe, so it is not turned off |
| Two steps in C | The receiver of a method that changes it, and then each argument, are computed into variables before the receiver is lent to change, for the methods you write and the built-in `add`, `addAll`, `remove` and `getOrAdd`. Approved on 14 November 2026 (choice 350) | The same order as the interpreter (336) |
| The leak report in a debug build | A debug build and the tests keep the `shared` values that are alive in a list (48 bytes in front of the counts) and report them with the interpreter's text; a release build keeps no list and reports nothing. Approved on 14 November 2026 (choice 351) | As the design wrote: "in test and debug builds" |
| What leaks on purpose in `cargo test` | A program that leaks by design reports it itself; its blocks are still reached from the list, so the system's leak check does not count them. Approved on 14 November 2026 (choice 352) | Every other program frees all it took |
| A `for` takes the owner in C | `for word in words` over a `shared` collection that owns it takes that owner and lets it go when the loop ends, as the interpreter does. Approved on 14 November 2026 (choice 353) | A `weak` look after the loop finds the same in both |
| Test | `test "name" { }`, a block written at the top level of a file. Only the test command compiles and runs it, it is not part of a normal build. `test` is a keyword only at the top level and when followed by a string, and it can be used as a name. A test belongs to the module of the folder it is written in: it sees every name of the module, and only the `public` names of other modules. In the block `try` works with every error type; an error fails the test and is printed (it must be `Formattable`, as for `main`). A failing test is a panic, and the test command runs each test in a separate process | Zig's notation. In Rust `#[test] fn` and `#[cfg(test)] mod tests`, in C# a separate project and a library, in Go a naming rule (a `_test` file, a function starting with `Test`). A separate `main` program cannot see what is not `public` and stops at the first error. Go's naming rule adds no word but makes people memorise two invisible rules, and a test name cannot be a sentence. The visibility follows from the module rule, it is not a new rule. Cost: one word; the only place where `try` works without a target error type being written; the time to start a process per test (a tooling decision) |
| `assert` | `assert(condition)`: panics if it is false. The message prints the text of the expression and, if it is a comparison, the values of both sides (if they are `Formattable`). Valid outside tests too, and on in release too. There is no `assertEqual` | A single form. In Rust three macros (`assert!`, `assert_eq!`, `debug_assert!`), in C# `Assert.Equal` is in a library and `Debug.Assert` is deleted in release. In the same direction as the rule that safety checks cannot be turned off. Cost: since there are no macros the compiler knows `assert`, like `$"..."` |
| Test command | `sg test file` compiles the file with its tests and runs them: one line per test, `ok    name` or `FAIL  name` followed by the panic or `error: ...`, then a count; exit code 1 if any failed. A test file needs no `main`. Two tests with the same name are a compile error. `try` on a `T?` in a test is a compile error (there is no caller to give `null` to; the hint is `... ?? panic("...")`, since `assert` does not narrow; 19 November, choice 1401). The `test` blocks are checked only by the test command: `sg file.sg` and `sg build` leave them out, as `cargo build` leaves out `#[cfg(test)]`, and `sg lsp` checks them (1402). With a file, the tests of the modules it reads run too. `test "..."` inside a function is a compile error. Decided on 12 November | What the "Test" row left open. The interpreter runs the tests one after the other in one process, each with variables of its own; with no global state the difference to a process per test cannot be seen yet |
| End of line | No semicolons, the end of a line ends the statement. A statement continues on the next line in these cases: inside `(` or `[`; if the line ends with an operator, a comma, a dot or an opening parenthesis; if the next line starts with `.` (a method chain). A line cannot start with an operator, and the hint says to move the operator to the end of the previous line | Go's rule + Python's parenthesis rule; the leading `.` is for the chain notation of C# and Rust. A leading operator creates ambiguity (`- x`), a leading dot does not. Approved on 12 November together with the interpreter |
| Place of `{` and `else` | `{` at the end of the line, `else` on the same line as `}`: `if x {`, `} else {`. Writing them on a new line is a compile error, and the hint shows the right place | The Go way: a single form. C#'s usual layout (`{` on a new line) was deliberately not taken, there would be two ways to write the same thing |
| Statement | Only a call, an assignment and a declaration are written as a statement; `a + 2` alone is a compile error. A name at the start of a statement, followed by `*` and `?`, followed by a second name, is a declaration: `int x = 1`, `Node*? list = null` | The C# rule (CS0201). This way the distinction between `Node* n` and `a * b` is resolved with lookahead: `a * b` cannot be a statement anyway |
| Struct construction in a condition | In the head of an `if`, `while` and `for`, `{` starts the block; struct construction there needs parentheses: `if p == (Point { x = 1 }) {`. Everywhere else without parentheses | Rust's rule. `if x == Node { ... } {` cannot be told apart otherwise. Cost: two parentheses in a rare case |
| Assigning to a `ref` and a `read` variable | Assigning to a `ref` variable writes to what is borrowed: `void clear(ref int? slot) { slot = null }`. Assigning to a `read` variable rebinds the variable: `cur = read cur.next` | This is how a `ref` parameter works in C#, and no separate "write into it" operator (`*x = ...`) is needed. Since nothing can be written through a `read`, the only meaning of assigning to it is rebinding |
| Looking does not move | The two sides of an operator, a method receiver and the arguments of `print`, `assert` and `$"..."` are borrowed only for that moment: `name` can be used after `print(name)`. A value is moved when it is given to a variable, a field, a parameter or a `return` | `a == b` already means `a.equals(b)` and takes `read`; `print` too reads the `Formattable`. In Rust `println!` gives the same result because it is a macro |
| Language name | SG. Source files end in `.sg` | The author's initials; short, and free as an extension. Decided on 12 November 2026 |
| Method names `replace` and `take` | A struct cannot define a method named `replace`. `take` can be defined | `x.replace(value)` exists on every type, so a method of that name would clash with it. The built-in `take` exists only on `T?`, and a method is never called on a `T?`. Approved on 12 November with the interpreter's third slice |
| Name clashes | A method and a field of the same struct cannot share a name. A function cannot have the name of a type | A name on a struct should mean one thing. `Stack(...)` always means `Stack.create(...)`. Approved on 12 November |
| `Name.f()` | A variable called `Name` wins over a type called `Name` | The nearer name wins, as everywhere else. Approved on 12 November |
| `take` and narrowing | `take` on a variable known not to be null ends that knowledge: the variable is `T?` again | `take` puts `null` in its place. Approved on 12 November |
| `ref this` on a new value | A `ref this` method can be called on a value that was just built: `Stack().push(1)` | The same as Rust. No variable is needed just to make the call. Approved on 12 November |
| `this` by value through a `T*` | A method that takes `this` by value, called through a `T*`, uses the pointer up | Taking `this` by value moves the value, and the pointer is its owner. Approved on 12 November |
| Moving a field out of an owned value | A field can be moved out of an owned value. Moves are tracked per field: the other fields stay usable, and the value as a whole is unusable until the field is assigned again | Follows from the `take`/`replace` rule, which restricts only a move through a `ref`: an owner may give up a part. Implemented in the interpreter's third slice on 12 November |
| Starting a task | `Task.run(work, share counter)` starts a function in parallel and gives a `Task<R>`, `R` being what the function returns. The function and its arguments are given apart, and the arguments are checked against its parameters as in a call; a `Func` can be given too. There is no `spawn` keyword. Approved on 14 November 2026, before the fifty-seventh slice | C#'s way. C# writes `Task.Run(() => Work(counter))` with a lambda, which SG does not have; `Task.run(work(counter))` would read as if `work` ran first and gave its result to `Task.run`, which is the kind of surprise the language avoids |
| `Task<R>` | The handle of a task. `t.wait()` waits for it and gives its result, so `try t.wait()` passes on its error; async code will write `await t` ("Async, the direction"). A task may run on a thread of its own or in a pool of threads, and no program can tell which | One handle for threads now and async later, as in C#. The runtime can move from a thread per task to a pool without changing what any program means |
| Every task is waited for | A `Task` that is not waited for on every path is a compile error, as a `Closable` value that is not closed; `Task.run(...)` alone on a line is one too. No task is ever cancelled without being told, by dropping its handle or otherwise. Approved on 14 November 2026, before the fifty-seventh slice | Structured concurrency, as Swift's task groups and Kotlin's `coroutineScope`: no task is left without an owner, no error of one is lost, and no work is cut off when `main` returns. In Rust a future that is dropped stops at any `await` without a word, a source of bugs known as "cancellation safety" |
| A task stays with its starter | A `Task` does not go to another task: it is not an argument of `Task.run`, not what a task gives back, and not held in a `locked` value. Approved on 14 November 2026, before the fifty-seventh slice | Only the task that started one can wait for it, so the waits make a tree and never a circle |
| A `lock` block does not wait | Inside a `lock` block there is no other `lock`, no `wait`, later no `await`, and no call of a function that does one of these, followed through the calls; a call of a `Func` there is refused when it could be such a function. Two values are locked together with `lock a as x, b as y { }`, which takes them in one fixed order (by address); the same value twice is a panic. Approved on 14 November 2026, before the fifty-seventh slice | A task that holds a lock never waits, so it lets the lock go, and with the rule above no circle of waits can form: locks and waits cannot deadlock, and no task can take the same lock twice. C# refuses `await` inside `lock` (CS1996) but lets a lock be taken inside another; Rust allows both. The cost: a lock is not taken inside another, the two are taken together |
| Where `locked` is written | Like `checked`, only after `shared` or `weak`: `shared locked T`, `weak locked T`; `locked` alone is an error. What it holds follows the thread rule. Approved on 14 November 2026, before the fifty-seventh slice | The same rule as `checked` ("`shared checked` as written"); a struct that needs a locked field makes the field `shared locked` |
| Globals, as decided | The language has no variable that lives outside a function and can be assigned freely, and none that each thread has of its own (Rust's `static mut` and `thread_local!`, C's globals and `_Thread_local`), and does not get them. What lives for the whole program is a `static` ("Statics"): read only, `atomic` or `locked`, never assigned, and every task reaches it under the thread rule. Approved on 14 November 2026, before the fifty-seventh slice; rewritten with the statics on 17 November 2026 (choice 1088) | A global that any task may assign is a data race the compiler cannot see; one that each thread has of its own does not follow a task, which moves between the threads of a pool, so a task would find another task's value after an `await`. What two tasks share is then in the arguments of `Task.run` or in a static, both of which the thread rule sees, and the owners of a `checked` value stay inside one task, which the compiler can prove. This is what lets a task move between threads in a pool. In Rust a task that holds an `Rc` cannot move (`Send`), because nothing proves the `Rc` stayed inside it |
| A panic in a task | Stops the process, as every panic does ("Panic"). The message says which task and which line; a second panic at the same time does not mix into it, and no other task writes after it. There is no lock poisoning. Approved on 14 November 2026, before the fifty-seventh slice | The other tasks could reach a half-changed state |
| `print` from several tasks | Each `print` writes its line whole, so the lines of two tasks never mix; their order is not fixed. Approved on 14 November 2026, before the fifty-seventh slice | The order of two tasks is theirs; a line cut in half is a surprise |
| The stack of a task | 8 MB on every platform, as `main`'s. A system that refuses a new thread is a panic that says so. Approved on 14 November 2026, before the fifty-seventh slice | A thread other than the first has 512 KB on macOS and 8 MB on Linux, so the same recursion would run on one and crash on the other |
| Tasks in the interpreter | Each task runs on a thread of the interpreter, but one at a time: they change hands only where one waits (`wait`, a lock held by another; later `await` and channels). What a program prints is the same on every run. Approved on 14 November 2026, before the fifty-seventh slice | The tests are the same on every run, and nothing changes when async and channels come. Compiled code runs the tasks at the same time |
| A task runs when it is waited for | In the interpreter a task runs when it is waited for, on a thread of its own while the one that waits is blocked, until it ends; `Task.run` only records the function and what it is given, so the starter goes on first. Approved on 14 November 2026 (choice 356) | Which task runs where tasks change hands is the one waited for, so the output is the same on every run and the waits make a tree |
| An exit with an error waits for a task | An exit with an error (`try`, `return Error(...)`) lets a task that is not waited for go: it is waited for there and what it gives is dropped. Every other exit is a compile error ("Every task is waited for"). Approved on 14 November 2026 (choice 357) | As a `StreamWriter` is let go there: the caller gets the error that made the function stop. Without it `try first.wait()` could not leave `second`, as the examples' two files do |
| Where a `Task` is kept | Only as it is, in a local variable, a parameter or a return value: not in a field, a variant, a collection, a `T?`, a `T*`, a `shared` value, and not behind `read` or `ref`. Approved on 14 November 2026 (choice 358) | Only there can the compiler see it waited for; a list of tasks comes with `Task.whenAll` |
| `wait` takes the task | A task is waited for once, and a borrowed one cannot be. What it gives is used or dropped on purpose: `t.wait()` alone is an error when the function returns a value (hint `_ = t.wait()`); a `Task<void>` is waited for alone. Approved on 14 November 2026 (choice 359, changed the same day: first written to let a value go unused) | The rule of the built-in methods that only give a value ("The value of a built-in method") |
| What `Task.run` checks | The first argument is the name of a function or a `Func` (`Task.run(work(1))` is an error whose hint is `Task.run(work, 1)`); the rest are checked as the arguments of a call, and the thread rule on the types of the function's parameters and on what it returns, naming the field where it is broken. Approved on 14 November 2026 (choice 360) | The type of the function says what crosses, also for a `Func` |
| The thread rule, as written | A borrow, a slice, a type that carries a borrow, an iterator over a text, a `shared checked` or `weak checked` value and a `Task` do not go to another task, also inside fields, items, `T?`, `T*` and `shared` values. A `Func`, a `StreamReader`, a `StreamWriter` and a plain `shared T` go. Approved on 14 November 2026 (choice 361) | A plain `shared T` is only read and its count is atomic; a stream owns its file |
| What a `locked` value holds | Follows the thread rule, checked once every type is known, naming the field; `shared checked locked`, `locked` alone, `new locked` and a `locked` around a `Task` are errors. Approved on 14 November 2026 (choice 362) | "Where `locked` is written" |
| Nothing reached outside `lock` | What a `locked` value holds is not reached outside a `lock` block, not even read: a field, a method, `for`, `match`, `print`, `==`, `read x` and `read x as` are errors whose hint is `lock`. `share`, `weak`, `same` and `.clone()` work. Approved on 14 November 2026 (choice 363) | They do not touch what it holds |
| The `lock` block, details | Each name is a `ref T` for the block. The same variable twice is a compile error, the same value through two variables a panic, two names alike an error; a `weak locked` value is locked through `share`, a `shared locked T?` after a test. Approved on 14 November 2026 (choice 364) | "A `lock` block does not wait" |
| What waits, found on the whole program | After the checker: a function waits when it takes a lock or waits for a task, or calls one that does. A `Func` called in a `lock` block is refused when any function whose address is taken, with as many parameters and the same return type, waits; the error shows the chain of calls. Starting a task in a `lock` block is allowed. Approved on 14 November 2026 (choice 365) | A little wider than "could be such a function", never narrower |
| A panic in a task, shown | Under the message, the line of `Task.run` that started the task; in a test what a task writes with `printError` shows under the test's `FAIL` line. Approved on 14 November 2026 (choice 366). The task named is the innermost one, and a panic that shows a second place of its own (a `checked` conflict) keeps it (choice 372, 15 November) | "A panic in a task": which task and which line |
| A lock in the interpreter | No lock is ever held by another task when one is taken, since tasks change hands only at `wait`, which a `lock` block cannot reach; nothing is kept for a lock, and the same value twice is found by comparing the values. A system that refuses a thread is a panic. Approved on 14 November 2026 (choice 367) | Follows from "Tasks in the interpreter" |
| `lock` and `locked` are words | They cannot be names, as `shared` and `checked` cannot; `spawn` is a name like any other. Approved on 14 November 2026 (choice 368) | There is no `spawn` |
| The leak report of `locked` | It covers `locked` values, which are `shared` ones; its hint is `weak locked T?`. Approved on 14 November 2026 (choice 369) | "`shared` cycle" |
| A task is a thread of the system | In compiled code a task is a thread of the system, started by `Task.run` at once, with a stack of 8 MB on every platform. `Task.run` takes one allocation, for the thread, the function, its arguments and what it will return; `wait` joins the thread, takes the value and frees the block. A system that refuses a thread is a panic that says so. Approved on 15 November 2026 (choice 371) | "Starting a task", "The stack of a task"; the interpreter runs a task when it is waited for, so its output is the same on every run |
| A panic holds the output | A panic holds the standard output and the standard error from its first line to the end of the process, so two panics do not mix and nothing is written after one; a line of `print` is one call of the system and comes out whole. Approved on 15 November 2026 (choice 373). A panic in a program whose output was closed early (`program \| head`) is still told and ends the program with 1: compiled code leaves `SIGPIPE` out from the panic's first line, as the interpreter has it (choice 1602, 20 November; it ended by the signal before the message) | "A panic in a task"; the order of taking the two streams is always the same, so holding them cannot deadlock |
| A `locked` value in compiled code | A `shared locked` value has a mutex of the system in front of its counts (64 bytes on macOS, 48 on Linux, with the alignment). `lock` takes several values in the order of their addresses and gives them back on every way out of the block (its end, `return`, `break`, `continue`, `try`); one value named twice is the panic of the interpreter. Approved on 15 November 2026 (choice 374) | "Lock"; the order of addresses is a second guard beside "a `lock` block does not wait" |
| Standard streams from several tasks | `Stdout`, `Stderr` and `Stdin` are safe from several tasks: a write is put together in a block of the call's own and written with one call of the system, a read takes a lock of the input, and the failure of a stream is kept atomically. Approved on 15 November 2026 (choice 375) | No code of the program runs while a stream is held |
| Memory kept by each thread | In a program with tasks the block where printed values are written is one for each thread, given back when a task ends, and the list of `shared` values alive (the leak report of a debug build) is changed under a lock. Approved on 15 November 2026 (choices 376, 377) | The block of one program was a data race between two tasks that print a struct; the thread sanitizer found it |
| Threads only where there are tasks | A program pays for threads only when it has tasks or `locked` values: only then does its C hold locks, values of each thread and tests of a stream. A task started through a `Func` finds the name of its function by its address. Approved on 15 November 2026 (choices 378, 379) | A program with one thread runs as it did before tasks came |
| The order of tasks in the tests | Where tasks print in an order of their own, a `.unordered` file next to the program says so, and the compiled output is compared line by line in any order; the interpreter's output is compared as it is. Every program with tasks also runs under the thread sanitizer, which is first tried on a C program that races on purpose. Approved on 15 November 2026 (choice 380) | A check that cannot see a race does not pass |
| Leaks after a panic in the tests | A compiled program that ends in a panic is not looked at for leaks: a panic does not unwind, so the system takes back what the program owned, and whether a leak tool still sees a pointer to it is up to the C compiler. Such a program still runs under the sanitizers and gives the interpreter's output; the test counts how many there were. The tests pass on Linux as on macOS (tried in a container, by hand; there is no CI). Approved on 15 November 2026 (choices 382, 383) | "Copy and cleanup": panic does not unwind; the same rule as a compiled test that fails |
| Async comes in parts | First `async` functions, `await`, `Task.delay` and the rules of "Async, the direction" that need nothing more, in the checker and the interpreter; then cancellation with input and output that is async, where how a cancellation shows in a `Result` and the names of the functions are still open; then compiled code (state machines, a pool of threads, a loop that waits for input and output). Approved on 15 November 2026 (choice 384) | "Async, the direction" |
| An async function | `async` is written in front of a function at the top level, and the type after it is what the body returns: `async int count()` gives a `Task<int>`, the same `Task<R>` as `Task.run`. `async void f()` gives a `Task<void>` that is awaited like any task, not C#'s `async void`. An `async` method, and `async` in front of anything but a function, are not here yet. `main` can be async. Approved on 15 November 2026 (choices 385, 396) | "Async, the direction" |
| An async call starts at once | A call without `await` starts the task at once, and it runs on the caller until it first waits, as in C#; a task of `Task.run` still runs when it is waited for in the interpreter. `await f(x)` runs `f` as part of the caller's task, with no task of its own. Approved on 15 November 2026 (choices 386, 387) | "Starting", "`await f()` takes no allocation" |
| What an async call is given | A call without `await` follows the thread rule, as `Task.run` does; `await f(read xs)` ends before the caller goes on, so it can borrow. What an async function returns follows the thread rule wherever it is called. Approved on 15 November 2026 (choice 388) | Rust's line: `tokio::spawn` needs owned values, an awaited future borrows |
| `await` and `wait` | `await t` takes the task as `wait` does and is written only in an async function (elsewhere the hint is `t.wait()`); its value is used or dropped with `_ =`. `wait` in an async function is an error whose hint is `await t`; code that is not async waits for an async task with `wait`. Approved on 15 November 2026 (choices 389, 390) | "Waiting the wrong way" |
| Nothing holds the thread in async code | In an async function `wait`, the functions of `File`, reading `Stdin`, a file stream, and a call of a function that does one of them (found on the whole program, the chain in the message) are errors whose hint is `await Task.run(work, x)`. Writing to `Stdout`, `Stderr` and `print` are not counted. Approved on 15 November 2026 (choice 391) | C# writes to its console from async code; the promise is about waits that can take long |
| Async and locks, async and itself | `await` in a `lock` block is an error, and an async function that awaits counts as waiting there. An async function that awaits itself, directly or through others, is an error; only awaited calls make the circle. An async function is not a value (`Task.run(answer)`, `var f = answer`). Approved on 15 November 2026 (choices 392 to 394) | "Locks", "Calling itself"; the size of a state machine is known where it is called by name |
| `Task.delay` | `Task.delay(ms)` gives a `Task<void>` that ends after that many milliseconds, an `int`; a negative number is a panic; code that is not async can `wait` for it, which sleeps. Its form with a token comes with cancellation. Approved on 15 November 2026 (choice 395) | "Async, the direction" |
| Async in the interpreter | Every task that runs code is a fiber, a thread of its own of which one runs at a time; the turn changes hands only where one waits, in the order the fibers became ready. The clock of the run moves only when every fiber waits, so the output is the same on every run. A panic in any task stops the run at once; when nothing could end a wait, the run stops with a panic. Approved on 15 November 2026 (choices 397 to 399) *Changed on 15 November 2026, in the seventy-second slice, and approved the same day (choice 550):* the clock of the run gives the order of the timers, and each also waits until its time has really gone by since it was made, so a delay asked for after a long computation still waits | "Tasks in the interpreter" |
| A `lock` block that returns | A `lock` block that returns on every path ends the function, as any block does (found on the way). The design's example of `countLines` gives a `Task<Result<int, IoError>>`. Approved on 15 November 2026 (choices 400 to 402) | A fix of the check that a function returns |
| Methods of a source and a token | `CancellationSource()`, `CancellationSource.after(ms)` (a negative time is a panic), `source.cancel()`, `source.token()`, `isCancelled()` on both, `token.check()`, `token.clone()`, `CancellationToken.None`. Methods with parentheses, since the language has no properties (`text.length()`); the design's example writes `source.token()`. Approved on 15 November 2026 (choices 403 to 405) | One way to reach what a built-in value gives; a constant is written as `int.MaxValue` is |
| A source has one owner, a token is cloned | A source is not `Copyable` and has no `clone`; another task that cancels it is given it, or it is shared (`new shared CancellationSource()`), since `cancel` works through a `read` borrow and so through a `shared` value. A token is not `Copyable`: `token.clone()` gives one more. A token goes to other tasks; it is not `Formattable`, `Equatable` or a key. Approved on 15 November 2026 (choices 406, 407) | Cancelling twice is cancelling once, as Rust's `cancel(&self)`; a copy that costs a count is written where it is made |
| A built-in async call lends and is awaited | A built-in function takes the token as `read CancellationToken`, as it takes a path, and keeps one of its own as long as it needs it. A built-in async call given a borrow is awaited where it is written, by the rule of "What an async call is given"; the async methods of a reader lend the reader. Approved on 15 November 2026 (choices 408, 409) | The thread rule holds for the built-in functions as for the program's own |
| `Task.delay` with a token | `Task.delay(ms, read token)` gives a `Task<Result<void, Cancelled>>`: `Ok()` when the time comes, `Error(Cancelled { })` as soon as the token is cancelled, at once when it already is. A cancel ends its delays in the order they were started; `CancellationSource.after(ms)` is a timer on the clock of the run, and a source that is gone is not waited for. Approved on 15 November 2026 (choices 410, 411) | The order of a run stays the same on every run |
| Async input and output in the interpreter | It is done when it is called, the token looked at first, so a cancelled token reads and writes nothing. A read of `Stdin` that waits for a line holds every task meanwhile, which compiled code is to remove. Streams opened, written and closed the async way come later; a reader has no `linesAsync`, since a `for` does not await. The hint against a call that holds the thread names its async version where there is one. Approved on 15 November 2026 (choices 412 to 414) | The interpreter stays simple and its output the same on every run; the tests give the input from a file |
| A struct without fields, written | By its name alone, as in Rust: `Marker`, `Cancelled(Cancelled)`; in compiled code too. Approved on 15 November 2026 (choices 415, 416) *(18 November 2026, the hundred-and-sixteenth slice: it holds one byte, as in C++, in C, in a header and in the interpreter: `Memory.sizeOf` is 1; choice 1335)* | It was `Marker {  }`. Rust gives it the size 0; C has no such struct (GCC and Clang give it 0, Microsoft's compiler 4) |
| An async function in C | A machine: a frame holds its parameters, every variable and temporary it declares and what it returns, and the frames of the async functions it awaits in place and its delays share one room inside it. Each `await` is a numbered place, and a jump at the start of the function goes back to where it stopped. Approved on 15 November 2026 (choices 417 to 419) | Rust's way, with no allocation for each `await`; every variable in the frame is simpler and right by construction, at the cost of a larger frame |
| `await` in compiled code | Wherever its value is computed before the statement: in an argument, an operand, the condition of an `if` or a `while`, the value of a `match`, `try await`. On a side computed only sometimes (the right of `&&`, `||`, `??`) it is refused with the hint to give the value a name first; the interpreter runs it. Approved on 15 November 2026 (choice 420) | As `try` is refused there |
| A task and the pool in C | A task is one block, the `Task<R>` with the frame after it, allocated when an async function is called without `await`; it runs on the caller's thread until it first waits. A pool of threads, one for each core and started as needed, each with a stack of 8 MB, runs the tasks that can go on from one queue, first in first out; a task woken while it runs runs once more. One pointer says who waits for a task: the async task that awaits it, or a thread asleep in `wait`. A `Task.run` task stays a thread. Approved on 15 November 2026 (choices 421 to 423) | C#'s start; no work stealing until a measurement asks for it |
| Waking a task, as built | The thread that ends what a task waits for (the clock, the loop of the network, a thread of work, a task that ends) is done with the task before the task can go on: it tells the end, wakes the task, and only then lets it go, and the task that sees the end before that waits the moment until it is let go. A wait of its own says so with `settled` (2 while it is woken, 1 after), a task that ends with `SG_ENDING` before `SG_ENDED`, and a delay through the clock's lock, which the task takes once when it sees the delay over; input and output did so already (`done` 2, then 1). Before, a task could see its wait over, go on, end and be freed while the thread that ended the wait was still to wake it, which then wrote into freed memory: seen by the thread sanitizer on Linux, and shown with the address sanitizer. Fixed on 17 November 2026, between the hundred-and-second and the hundred-and-third slices (choices 1120 to 1123) | The rule the input and output of the sixty-second slice kept, now kept by every waker. The cost is a short wait in the rare case where a task sees its wait end while it is being woken, and one lock taken when a delay is over. The tests build the async programs with `-DSG_PAUSE_WAKES`, where the waker pauses before it wakes and a task that has just begun to wait looks again after a pause, under the thread and the address sanitizers |
| Delays and a real clock | One thread keeps the delays, ordered by when they end and then by the order they were started. `Task.delay(0)`, and a delay whose token is already cancelled, end at once without giving the turn away, as `Task.Delay(0)` in C#; in the interpreter a delay of zero lets the others go first. Compiled tasks run side by side on a real clock, so tests that wait keep their times far apart, or compare lines in any order. Approved on 15 November 2026 (choices 424, 425) | Only the order of lines can show the difference |
| Cancellation, `main` and a way out in C | A source and its tokens are one block with an atomic count of owners, a flag and the delays that look at it; the clock holds a source of `after` until its time. An async `main` is a task started on the main thread, which waits for it. A way out with an error from an async function awaits the tasks it lets go: the rest of a `try`'s way out is written after the body. The pieces of an async function have names of their own in C. Approved on 15 November 2026 (choices 426 to 429) | No task is cut off, as in the interpreter |
| What async costs, measured | A task that never waits, started and awaited for each number below a million beside an `await` in place, takes 1.08 times the same calls in C; the task's allocation and atomic steps are the difference, in "Slower than C". Approved on 15 November 2026 (choices 430, 431) | |
| Async input and output in compiled code | The work of `File.readTextAsync` and the others, and of the async methods of a reader, is done on a thread of input and output that may block, which puts what it gives into the task's frame and wakes the task; the task holds no thread meanwhile. The work, its arguments and the room for what it gives are one block in the frame, so nothing is allocated for it. Approved on 15 November 2026 (choices 432, 438) | Every such call is given a borrow, so it is awaited where it is written, and that is the only way it is compiled |
| The token before the work, compiled | A cancelled token gives `Cancelled` at once and nothing is read or written, as in the interpreter; work that has started goes to its end. Approved on 15 November 2026 (choice 433) | A read of the input that waits for a line waits for it after a cancel too, until the loop that waits for the network can wake it. *Changed on 15 November 2026, in the sixty-fifth slice:* a cancel ends a wait for a socket, and a wait for a line of an input that can make the program wait ("A cancel ends a wait") |
| The threads of input and output | Started when work is queued and none is free, up to 512; above that the work waits in the queue, first in first out. A thread with nothing to do for 10 seconds ends. Tokio's numbers for blocking work. Approved on 15 November 2026 (choice 434) | A refused thread is a panic only when there is none |
| A read the block can answer, in place | A reader keeps a block of 64 KB; a read the block already answers, and any read after the end, is done on the task's own thread without waiting, as Tokio's `BufReader` does. The input is tried, not waited for, so a read it cannot take at once goes to a thread. A task that waits for the input holds no thread of the pool, which the interpreter cannot show. Approved on 15 November 2026 (choices 435, 436) | Compiled code differs from the interpreter here on purpose |
| `try` and `await` on the right of `&&` and `\|\|` | Compiled: the right side, which runs only sometimes, becomes an `if` of its own after the left side. A left side that made a value needing a temporary is still refused, with the hint to give it a name. Approved on 15 November 2026 (choice 437) | |
| Reading the input the async way, measured | Two million lines read with `await Stdin.readLineAsync` take 1.37 times the same program in C; handing every read to a thread would take 84 times. What is left is mostly the trip to a thread and back for each block of 64 KB, in "Slower than C". Approved on 15 November 2026 (choices 439, 440) | |
| A list of tasks, as written | `List<Task<R>>` is kept only where a task is kept (a local variable, a parameter, a return value) and does not go to another task; it has `add` and `length` and nothing else. An index, `for` (a `break` would leave tasks behind) and the other methods are errors whose hint is `Task.whenAny`, and a list without a name cannot be given a method. Approved on 15 November 2026 (choices 441, 442) | "Where a `Task` is kept": only there can the compiler see every task waited for |
| `Task.whenAll` and `Task.whenAny`, as written | `Task.whenAll(tasks)` takes the list and gives what each task gave, in the order of the list, an error being an item of it; an empty list gives an empty list. `Task.whenAny(ref tasks)` takes the first task to end out of the list and gives what it gave; an empty list is a panic. Both are awaited, or waited for with `.wait()`, where they are written. Approved on 15 November 2026 (choices 443 to 446) | No error is lost, as C#'s `await Task.WhenAll` loses all but the first; a task of either kept for later is not here yet |
| Lists of tasks in the interpreter and in C | The interpreter waits for the tasks of `whenAll` in the order of the list, and `whenAny` takes the first in the list that has ended, waiting for all at once when none has, so the output is the same on every run. Compiled, each list has a group (a mutex, a condition and the tasks in the order they ended) that a task tells as the last thing it does, so the group is freed only after every task has told it. Approved on 15 November 2026 (choices 448 to 450) | A task that ends on another thread never meets a freed group |
| Taking tasks out of a list, measured | Eight tasks taken with `whenAll` and eight with `whenAny` in each of 200,000 rounds take 1.24 times C, where the same tasks awaited one by one take 1.06; the group of each list and its lock are the difference, in "Slower than C". Approved on 15 November 2026 (choices 451, 452) | |
| Streams the async way, as written | `File.openTextAsync`, `File.createTextAsync` and `File.openAppendAsync` give the stream as a task; a writer has `writeAsync`, `writeLineAsync`, `writeBytesAsync` and `flushAsync`, each with a token that may come last, and `closeAsync`, which takes the writer, meets the `Closable` check and takes no token. All are awaited where they are written. A cancelled token writes nothing and the writer goes on. Approved on 15 November 2026 (choices 457, 458, 460, 461) | One name for each piece of work, as for the reads ("The names of async input and output") |
| A writer dropped in async code, compiled | A writer an async task drops goes on a list in the task, and the function hands the list to a thread of input and output and awaits it after a statement that reaches a writer and after the drop of a variable that owns one; inside a `lock` block, after the block. A function that reaches no writer pays nothing. Approved on 15 November 2026 (choices 453, 455, 459, 462, 463) | No thread of the pool writes a file, and the next line finds the file whole |
| A function that is not async drops a writer | It is not counted as holding the thread when an async function calls it: the writer it drops goes on the task's list and is closed after the line of the call. Approved on 15 November 2026 (choice 456), in place of what was decided before the slice | Nothing is written on the thread of the pool, and a function that takes a writer and gives it back is not refused |
| The names of a block end in order | In the interpreter too, the names of a block end in the reverse order they were declared, and a task left behind on a way out with an error is awaited where its name ends, so writers and tasks end in the same order as in compiled code. Approved on 15 November 2026 (choice 454) | The interpreter kept a block's names in no set order |
| Copying a file the async way, measured | The log copied line by line the async way takes 1.09 times the same program in C (0.94 without async); the hand-over of each block to a thread is the difference, in "Slower than C". Approved on 15 November 2026 (choices 464, 465) | |
| `receive` and `send` | A `TcpStream` receives what has come (how many, 0 at the end) and sends all its bytes; `read` is a word of the language, and these are C#'s names for a socket. A listener is reached through `read`, so several tasks can take connections from one `shared` listener; a stream through `ref`. A socket has no buffer and no `close`: it closes where it ends. Approved on 15 November 2026 (choices 466-470) | Apart from a reader's `readBytes`, which fills the buffer |
| The network in the interpreter | A socket does not block; a task whose socket is not ready gives the turn away, and when none can run the scheduler waits for the sockets with `poll` until the first timer is due, then the clock goes on to the time that has gone by. A task of `Task.run` that has not run starts when a task is about to wait for the network or the input.
