Approved on 15 November 2026 (choices 473, 474) | Otherwise a server started with `Task.run` never runs while `main` waits for it; programs without the network keep the order they had |
| The network in compiled code | One thread waits for the sockets with epoll or kqueue (since the eighty-first slice, a thread of the pool with nothing to do); a cancel takes the socket off, and the loop wakes the task once it is done with the events it holds. A listener is waited for through a copy of its socket (*changed on 16 November 2026, in the eighty-second slice:* a socket goes on the loop once, the first time a task waits for it, edge-triggered, and a wait is no call of the system; several tasks wait for one socket in its slot, with no copy, and a cancel takes the task out of the slot and wakes it at once). A socket blocks until an async call first uses it, so a call without async is one call of the system. Names are looked up on a thread that a cancel leaves. Approved on 15 November 2026 (choices 471, 472, 475-477) | The threaded echo takes the time of C |
| The network the async way, measured | An echo server with eight clients takes 1.86 times C with blocking threads, 0.99 without async; two threads are woken for each wait where C wakes one, in "Slower than C". Approved on 15 November 2026 (choices 478, 479). *Measured again on 16 November 2026, in the eighty-first slice (choices 673, 674):* 1.21 times C on macOS, 17 times on Linux in a container (28 before), where Tokio takes 5.5 times C; with work in each message, the time of C. *Measured again in the eighty-second slice (choice 688):* 1.03 times C on macOS, still 17.7 times on Linux, where the time is in the one queue of the pool; datagrams 1.16 times on Linux (1.54 before). *Measured again in the eighty-third slice (choice 700):* 0.89 times C on macOS and 7.4 times on Linux (Tokio 5.5); datagrams 1.06 and 0.57 times C | |
| The reader of a connection, as built | `conn.reader()` is a `StreamReader` that carries a borrow of the connection: lent where a reader is lent, refused in a field, a return or a task. `send` goes through `read`, `receive` through `ref`, so a `receive` while the reader is used again is a compile error; neither `send` nor `reader()` through a `shared` connection. A line or a text longer than the limit (64 KB, or `conn.reader(limit)`) is `LineTooLong`, and every read after it; `readSome` is on every reader. Approved on 15 November 2026 (choices 480-485) | What has come stays in the block until a read can take all it needs, so a cancel loses nothing |
| The reader of a connection, in the interpreter and in C | The interpreter keeps what has come in a block of its own and gives the turn away while the socket is not ready. | Lines over a connection, measured | Two million lines over the loopback read with `readLineAsync` take 1.46 times `getline` in C, which does not check that they are text; 0.89 without the check, which is in "Slower than C". Approved on 15 November 2026 (choices 488, 490) | |
| A task woken after it was freed | A thread of input and output marks its work done with 2 while it wakes the task and with 1 once it has let go of it; a task that sees 2 waits for the 1. | Checking UTF-8 a block at a time | A reader checks the rest of its block at once and keeps how far it is known to be text; the lines inside that part cost nothing. A line starts after a `\n`, so what is found is what a check of each line finds; a line two blocks share is checked whole, and a read of bytes lets go of what is known. Compiled: `T*`, `calloc`, `free`, `memmove`. Approved on 15 November 2026 (choices 513, 514) | |
| Raw memory, measured | A ring buffer over raw memory takes 1.00 times the same program in C over `calloc`. Approved on 15 November 2026 (choices 515, 516) | |

| A C file, as built | `extern "./native.c" { ... }`: a file of the module's folder or below it, ending in `.c`, looked for beside the file of SG; compiled code takes it in at the end of the unit and calls its functions by name from adapters written after it, so a `static inline` one is inlined; the interpreter puts it at the top of its wrappers *(16 November, choice 748: after its declarations, as compiled code does)*. `_GNU_SOURCE` on Linux and `-std=c11` in both. After `...` a call gives `int`, `uint`, `long`, `ulong`, `nint`, `nuint`, `double`, `raw T`, `CFunc`, `ref T` and `read T` of plain data, and `read CString`; what C would widen without a word (`sbyte`, `byte`, `short`, `ushort`, `bool`, `float`) and a `char` are errors whose hint is the cast, a struct by value and a text are errors, and so is a number, a fractional number or `null` written without a type (`(long)30`, not `30`, where C reads a `long`). The name of an `export` function there is its `CFunc`. A function that takes `...` is no `CFunc`. The format of a `printf` is not looked at. Approved on 16 November 2026 (choices 763 and 776) | What C hands a callback is a pointer and a length; a loop over its bytes would cost what the copy of C does not |

Approved on 16 November 2026 (choice 774) | Without it a server task never answered the request its own program was making with libcurl |

Safe by default: only `http` and `https`, on redirects too; at most 5 redirects; certificates always checked, `caFile` for one's own authorities; 30 s and 10 s; a limit of 16 MB on what a response brings, headers included; a line break in the type of a post refused. Approved on 16 November 2026 (choices 761 to 773; from the slice 775, an adapter of `...` for each list of the types of SG, 777, the memory libcurl keeps past a call, and 778, the headers counted) | The second program of the threshold |
| Signals, as built | `Signals.listen(read List<Signal> { Signal.Interrupt, Signal.Terminate })` gives a `SignalListener` (`Disposable`, given to a task if need be); while it lives, those signals do not end the program, and once the last listener of a kind ends, the kind does what it did before. `listener.next()` waits for the next and gives it; `await listener.nextAsync(read token)` does not hold the thread, a cancel ends the wait and a later signal stays. Several of one kind before a call are one, kinds come in their order, every listener gets each. `Signal` is `Interrupt`, `Terminate`, `Hangup`, `Quit`, `User1`, `User2`, `WindowChange`, without numbers. `Signals.raise(s)` is C's `raise`, what was printed written out first; `process.signal(s)` sends one to a program started with `Command`. The handler only writes the kind into a pipe; a thread started by the first listener hands it on, in compiled code and in the interpreter. `examples/shutdown` is a server that stops taking connections on the first Ctrl-C or SIGTERM, waits for the open ones, and cuts them on a second, tested with real signals from outside. Decided on 16 November 2026 before the eighty-eighth slice (choices 779 to 793); 794 to 799 come from the slice; all were approved the same day | Go's `signal.Notify` and Tokio's `signal`; a handler of the program's own is left to C |
| The layout of C, as built | `union Bits { uint word` / `float number }`: built with one field, written whole, read inside `unsafe`, never lent, not compared or written out by its fields. `uint version : 4`: a bit field of a struct, of an integer type or `bool`; `uint _ : 4` leaves bits empty, `uint _ : 0` starts a new unit; a value that does not fit is a panic in the interpreter and a debug build and keeps its lowest bits in a release build, a number written in the program that does not fit is an error; a bit field has no address. `packed struct` has no padding, its fields are copied out, not lent, it holds no bit fields and no `align(N)` type. `align(N) struct` lies at a multiple of N wherever it is kept, the heap included. `Memory.sizeOf<T>()`, `Memory.alignOf<T>()`. An item of raw memory reached where its type may not lie is a panic in the interpreter and a debug build, free in a release build; `Memory.readUnaligned` and `writeUnaligned` reach one anywhere; `Memory.viewRef(p, n)` is a window that writes. `Header.fromBytes(bytes)` reads a type every pattern of bits is a value of, with no `unsafe`. The interpreter lays bit fields out by the rule of its machine: macOS and Linux on x86-64, Linux on 64-bit Arm, or Windows; a test compares its layouts with C on every system. Decided on 16 November 2026 before the eighty-ninth slice (choices 800 to 816, four of them changed for Windows the same day); 817 to 829 come from the slice; all were approved the same day | C's own types, and Rust's `#[repr(C)]` unions, `#[repr(packed)]`, `#[repr(align)]` and `read_unaligned`; Rust has no bit fields |
| Atomics and `volatile`, as built | `atomic T` for an integer, `bool`, `float`, `double` or `raw T`, written anywhere a value is and made with `atomic e`; as large as `T`, aligned to its size. `load`, `store`, `exchange`, `compareExchange` and `compareExchangeWeak` (a `Result<T, T>`), `fetchAdd` and `fetchSub` on numbers (checked for overflow from the value they give, in the interpreter and a debug build; `wrappingFetchAdd` and `wrappingFetchSub` wrap), `fetchAnd`, `fetchOr` and `fetchXor` on integers and `bool`; each through `read` and `shared`, sequentially consistent unless a `MemoryOrder` is written as a variant, an order the operation cannot keep a compile error. `count += 1`, `count = 2` and `print(count)` are errors whose hints are the methods. Not `Copyable`, `Equatable` or `Formattable`, not `readonly`, not in a union, a `packed` struct or a bit field. `Atomic.fence`, `Atomic.compilerFence`, `Atomic.spinLoop()`. `struct T : unsafe Sendable`: the writer's promise, and a value of it goes to another task with its raw pointers. `unsafe Memory.readVolatile(p[i].status)` and `writeVolatile`, a place in raw memory read or written once. Compiled with the `__atomic` builtins, which Clang has on Windows too; in the interpreter one task runs at a time, every order acts as sequentially consistent, and the turn goes to another task every 64 atomic operations and at `spinLoop`. `examples/queue` is a queue without locks, many producers and many consumers, with no `unsafe`. Decided on 16 November 2026 before the ninetieth slice (choices 830 to 847); 848 to 859 come from the slice; all were approved the same day | Rust's `AtomicI64` and `Ordering`, C11's `_Atomic` and `atomic_thread_fence`, Rust's `read_volatile`; C#'s `Interlocked` changes a plain field, which SG does not let a program read without an atomic operation |
| A library built from SG, as built | `sg build folder --library` gives `libfolder.a`, `--shared` gives `libfolder.so` (`.dylib` on macOS), each with `folder.h`; `sg emit-c folder --library` prints the C. A library has no `main` and exports at least one function; only its `export` functions are seen from outside (the runtime is `static`, the shared library hides the rest), so two libraries of SG live in one program, each with its runtime, which starts on first use from any thread. The header: `stdint.h` types as compiled code has them, plain structs written out as SG lays them out, pointers to functions written out, `extern "C"`, and a macro for Windows' DLLs; each type under its name in SG, a clash of names or a word of C an error. A struct of SG that is not plain data, or an enum, goes to C by its address: an `export` function gives `T*` (C keeps it), takes back `T*` or `T*?` (it ends there, its `dispose` too), and is lent `read T` or `ref T` for the call, with no `unsafe`; C sees `typedef struct Calculator Calculator;`. A plain struct comes after those it holds by value; one that a struct written before it points at (`raw Node next`) is named first with a `typedef` (18 November, choice 1337). A text from C is a `read CString`, its `const char*`, which `string.fromCString(text)` copies with no `unsafe`. `NULL` where none is taken is a panic; the interpreter also keeps the addresses it gave and panics on one it did not give, of another type, given back already, or lent against the rules of borrowing. A panic in a library aborts; a shared library is not unloaded. `examples/calclib` is a calculator in SG used by a program of C. Decided on 16 November 2026 before the ninety-first slice (choices 860 to 875, the address given as Rust gives a `Box<T>`); 876 to 885 come from the slice; all were approved on 17 November 2026 | Rust's `staticlib`, `cdylib` and cbindgen, Go's `c-archive` and `c-shared`; a value of SG behind an opaque pointer, as a library of C keeps its own |
| Without an operating system, as built | `sg build folder --freestanding` gives `folder.o`, compiled with `-ffreestanding` for the machine `--target` names (by Clang) or for this processor, the C flags after `--` given on; `sg emit-c folder --freestanding` prints the C. No `main`, at least one `export` function, which the code that starts the machine calls. `void onPanic(read string message)` in the root module is given the message of a panic (the text a program with a system writes to its standard error, in a buffer of 1024 bytes), after which the processor waits for ever, also when `onPanic` returns or panics; the other cores go on running, stopping them being `onPanic`'s. `raw byte allocate(nuint size, nuint align)` and `void release(raw byte block, nuint size, nuint align)`, both or neither, give every block and take it back, told its size and alignment, which the runtime keeps in front of the block; a program without them allocates nothing, and `allocate` and `release` allocate nothing either, both checked statement by statement in the C the back end writes. What needs a system is a compile error where it is written, through a function of the language's own module too: output, files, the network, tasks and `async`, `lock`, the clock, the system's random bytes, `HashMap` and `HashSet`, the environment, folders, other programs, signals, `errno`, `<math.h>` and `double.parse`; the functions of the module that need one and that nothing calls are left out. The object takes nothing of C's library: the runtime's `vsnprintf`, `strlen` and the others are written in, and `memcpy`, `memmove`, `memset` and `memcmp` are weak functions of its own, and so, since the ninety-fourth slice, is the division of 128 bits a C compiler calls its library for (`__divti3` and its kin), so that the object links alone; of the C compiler's library only what that compiler calls on its own is left to the machine (GCC's atomics on 64-bit ARM). Each function in a section of its own, for a link with `--gc-sections` (choice 927). With `--target`, the Clang `CC` names (choice 921). 64-bit machines only (the runtime uses `__int128`). `examples/bare` is run by a machine of this computer. Written on 17 November 2026 while the author was away, on their word to go on with the next work (choices 895 to 913), which were approved afterwards, the same day *(17 November 2026, the hundred-and-second slice: on Windows too, where the object asks for no library of C, and the kernel boots in QEMU there; 1068, 1069, 1072)* | Rust's `#![no_std]` with `#[panic_handler]` and `#[global_allocator]`; C's freestanding environment, which promises a few headers and no library |
| The kernel shown, as built | `examples/kernel`: QEMU's `virt` machine with no firmware, machine mode, one core. `_start`, the vector of traps (the registers a call may change kept on the stack, the fractional ones too where the core has them), the registers of the core and `wfi` are the C file beside it (`extern "./machine.c"`), compiled into the same object; the serial port (a 16550), the timer (the CLINT's `mtime` and `mtimecmp`) and the device that powers off are driven in SG with `Memory.readVolatile` and `writeVolatile`. With no globals, the kernel's state reaches the handler through `mscratch`: `onTrap(raw void state, ulong cause, ulong pc)` lends it with `unsafe (read Kernel)state` and counts in an `atomic ulong`. It writes, takes five interrupts of the timer 10 ms apart, counts the blocks of its heap and powers the machine off; any other trap is a panic told on the serial port. Built with `sg build --freestanding --target riscv64-unknown-elf`, linked by `clang -nostdlib -fuse-ld=lld -T kernel.ld` with nothing but its object. Tested by booting it in QEMU, debug and release, and with a fault asked for; a missing tool fails the test. 2.31 times the size of the same kernel in C, a fixed part of the runtime ("Slower than C"). Choices 914 to 926 approved on 17 November 2026 before the ninety-fourth slice; 927 came from the slice and was approved the same day | Rust's `riscv-rt` and its `#[interrupt]`, with a `static AtomicU64` where SG has `mscratch`; xv6 keeps a pointer for each core the same way |
| The clock, as built | `Instant` (the monotonic clock: `Instant.now()`, `elapsed`, `since`, `plus`, `minus`; not printed), `Duration` (a `long` of nanoseconds that may be negative: `fromNanoseconds` to `fromSeconds`, the whole units cut toward zero, `totalSeconds`, `plus`, `minus`, `times`; printed `1.5s`, `250ms`, `3µs`) and `DateTime` (UTC, nanoseconds since 1970, the years 1677 to 2262: `now`, `utc(...)` giving a `Result<DateTime, DateError>`, `fromUnixSeconds`, the parts, `dayOfWeek`, `unixSeconds`, `since`, `plus`, `minus`; printed in ISO 8601). Methods, not operators; the arithmetic follows the rule of the numbers. Time zones and reading a date: "Time zones and dates in text"; `Task.delay` keeps its `int`. Approved on 15 November 2026 (choices 546 to 548, 551) | Two clocks are two types, so the time between two readings is taken on one clock |
| The language's own module | The clock is written in SG, in a module `std` that a program gets when it names one of its types, seen from every module with nothing written; its fields are its own, `use std` is an error, and only it calls the two readings of the clock, the compiler's. A panic inside it is shown at the call of the program that led there, without the hint about the module's code (since the seventy-fifth slice, row "A panic of the language's own module"). Compiled, the two readings are one call of `clock_gettime`; the interpreter reads the same clocks. Approved on 15 November 2026 (choices 545, 549, 552) | Everything a struct of a program has comes with it, in both the interpreter and compiled code; the first piece of a standard library written in the language |
| A delay on the real clock, in the interpreter | The clock of the run gives the order of the timers; each also waits until its time has really gone by since it was made, so a delay asked for after a long computation still waits. Approved on 15 November 2026 (choice 550) | The output stays the same on every run, and what a program measures is true |
| The clock, measured | Ten million readings of the monotonic clock take 1.01 times `clock_gettime` in C; taking ten million moments apart takes 0.29 times `gmtime_r`. Approved on 15 November 2026 (choices 553 to 555) | |
| Random numbers, as built | `Random` (xoshiro256\*\* seeded with SplitMix64: `Random.fromSeed(long)`, `Random.fromSystem()`; `nextInt(low, high)` and `nextLong(low, high)` from `low` up to `high`, without it, every number as likely, an empty range a panic in every build; `nextDouble`, `nextBool`, `nextUlong`, `fill(ref bytes)`), the same numbers for the same seed in the interpreter, in compiled code and on every system; not `Copyable` and not `Formattable`, lent with `ref`, no generator the program does not make. Written in SG in a second file of the module `std`, which a program gets when it names one of its types. The language has no overloads, so the 64 random bits are `nextUlong()`. Approved on 15 November 2026 (choices 556 to 560, 563) | No `Random.Shared` as in C#: the language has no globals. A copy would give the same numbers again, so a move is the only way to hand a generator over |
| The system's random bytes | `SecureRandom.fill(ref bytes)`, `nextUlong()`, `nextInt(low, high)`, `nextLong(low, high)` and `hexString(length)` (lower-case hex digits, four random bits each) read `arc4random_buf` on macOS and the BSDs and `getrandom` on Linux; never an error, a panic if the system gave no bytes. Only the language's own module calls `Entropy.fill`; the key of a hash table is drawn with the same function. Approved on 15 November 2026 (choices 561, 562) | As Go's `crypto/rand` since 1.24 and Rust's `OsRng`: a `Result` here would invite `?? 0` |
| Random numbers, measured | A hundred million numbers in a range take 1.00 times the same algorithm in C; a gigabyte of the system's bytes 1.00 times `arc4random_buf`. Approved on 15 November 2026 (choices 564 to 566) | |
| Hex and Base64, as built | `Hex.encode(read data)`, `Hex.encodeInto(ref text, read data)` and `Hex.decode(text)`; `Base64.encode` and `decode` (standard alphabet, `=` padding), `encodeUrl` and `decodeUrl` (`-` and `_`, no padding), in `std/encoding.sg`. Decoding gives `Result<List<byte>, DecodeError>`; a space, a line break, a character of another alphabet, misplaced padding or bits left beyond the last byte is `InvalidCharacter(position)`, an impossible length `InvalidLength`. Approved on 19 November 2026, the hundred-and-twenty-fourth slice (choices 1406, 1407) | One text for each set of bytes, and no two readers that disagree; C#'s `FromBase64String` skips white space, Rust's `base64` does not |
| CRC-32, as built | `Crc32.of(read data)` gives the CRC-32 of zlib, gzip, PNG and ZIP (IEEE 802.3, reflected, `0xEDB88320`) as a `uint`; `var c = Crc32()`, `c.update(read piece)` and `c.value()` give the same piece by piece, `value()` between pieces too. In `std/checksum.sg`, sixteen bytes in a step through sixteen tables of 256 numbers that are a const found when the program is compiled: the time of the same algorithm written in C, a tenth of zlib's speed on macOS, where zlib uses the processor's CRC instructions. Approved on 19 November 2026, the hundred-and-twenty-fifth slice (choices 1408, 1414, 1415) | Rust's `crc32fast` takes sixteen bytes in a step where the processor has no CRC instructions, and those where it has; SG has no way to call them yet |
| SHA-256 and HMAC, as built | `Sha256.of(read data)` gives the SHA-256 of FIPS 180-4 as a `byte[32]`; `var h = Sha256()`, `h.update(read piece)` and `h.finish()` give the same piece by piece, and `finish` does not end the hash. `Hmac.sha256(read key, read data)` is HMAC-SHA-256 (RFC 2104). `Bytes.equalInConstantTime(read a, read b)` goes over every byte with no branch on what they hold; lengths that differ give `false` at once. In `std/hash.sg`, plain SG: the time of the same algorithm written in C, a ninth of OpenSSL's speed with the processor's SHA instructions. No other hash and no cipher in `std`. Approved on 19 November 2026, the hundred-and-twenty-sixth slice (choices 1409, 1419-1422) | Rust's `sha2` uses the SHA instructions where the processor has them, and `hmac` with `subtle` compares tags in constant time; Go's `crypto/sha256` and `crypto/hmac` likewise |
| The command line, as built | `var cl = CommandLine("gifcrop", "Crops GIF images.")`, `cl.flag(name, letter, help)`, `cl.option(name, letter, valueName, help)` (the letter a `char?`, `null` for none), `cl.positional(name, help)` (must be given, in order), `cl.rest(name, help)` (the words left, once and last) and `cl.version(text)` (`--version`, no short letter); `cl.parseOrExit(read args)` gives an `Arguments` or ends the program (the help or the version to the output with 0, an error and `Try 'gifcrop --help'.` to the standard error with 2), `cl.parse(read args)` a `Result<Arguments, CommandLineError>`. GNU's way of writing: `--name value`, `--name=value`, `-vq`, `-ovalue`, options after the words, `--`, a lone `-` a word; an option takes the next word whatever it is; a long name is never shortened, and an unknown one is told the declared name at most two edits from it. `a.option(name)` gives the last value, `a.options(name)` all. What is declared or asked wrongly (a name twice, `-h`, a name not declared or declared as another kind) is a panic with what to write. The values are text. In `std/command_line.sg`. Approved on 19 November 2026, the hundred-and-twenty-seventh slice (choices 1410, 1423-1433) Since the hundred-and-forty-second slice (20 November, choice 1594, from the second scan of the library): a word in an error has the characters a reader does not see escaped, and the nearest name is looked for only among names of about its length (a word of a megabyte took a minute in the interpreter). | Rust's `clap` with its builder, which also never shortens a long name; Go's `flag`, which stops at the first word and has no words that must be given; GNU's `getopt_long`, which takes `--verb` for `--verbose` |
| JSON, as built | `Json.parse(read text)` gives a `Result<JsonValue, JsonError>`; `JsonValue` is `Null`, `Bool`, `Integer` (a whole number that fits a `long`), `Number` (every other, written back with a `.` or an exponent), `String`, `Array` or `Object`, a `JsonObject` that keeps its members in order (`add` panics on a name that is there, `set`, `remove`, `get`, `names()`) and finds a name by looking at each up to 16 members, through a table past that. `v.get(name)`, `v.at(i)` and `as*` give `null`, never a panic. `toJson()` on one line, `toJsonIndented(n)` on lines, escaping only the quote, the backslash and the control characters; writing NaN or an infinity is a panic. Strict RFC 8259: no comments, trailing commas, `NaN` or byte order mark, a name twice an error, half of a surrogate pair an error, nesting to 128; a `JsonError` has the line, the column in characters and a `JsonErrorKind`, a character that cannot be seen named by its code. `==` compares objects in any order. In `std/json.sg`, tested with the 318 parsing cases of JSONTestSuite; 1.15 times cJSON's time. Approved on 19 November 2026, the hundred-and-twenty-eighth slice (choices 1411, 1434-1445) Since the hundred-and-forty-second slice (20 November, choice 1594, from the second scan of the library): a key in an error has the characters a reader does not see escaped (`UnicodeCategory.hides`, shared with `Log`, zip names and the command line); `remove` stays linear, as `IndexMap.shift_remove`; a value read takes 50 to 200 bytes a byte of text, written in the library page; `toJson` writes any depth, which `Json.parse` reads to 128. | serde_json's `Value` with `preserve_order`, which reads a name twice and keeps the last (Go's `encoding/json` too); SG refuses it so that two readers cannot disagree. No `#[derive(Serialize)]`: no reflection or macros |
| Log | *Decided on 19 November 2026 (choices 1412, 1416, 1417, 1447-1455).* `Log.trace`, `debug`, `info`, `warn` and `error` write a line to the standard error, `2026-11-19T20:15:03.123Z INFO  message`: the time in UTC to the millisecond and the level padded to five letters. `LogLevel` is `Trace`, `Debug`, `Info`, `Warn`, `Error` or `Off`; `Log.setLevel`, `Log.level()` and `Log.isOn(level)`. A program starts at `Info` or at what `SG_LOG` names (any case), and `setLevel` changes it after; a value that names no level is told in one line and `Info` is used. The compiler writes `Log.debug(text)` as `if Log.isOn(LogLevel.Debug) { ... }`, so the text of a level that is off is never built, and a call in it does not run. The text of a message is never read for anything in it: no lookups (`${...}`), no pattern with arguments. Control characters, the marks of direction, the characters of no width, the byte order mark and the backslash are escaped when written, so one line is one call. A line is made whole and written at once, under the lock of `printError`. On a terminal the level is coloured, never with `NO_COLOR` or `TERM=dumb`, and on Windows only where the console takes the escape sequences. No files, rotation, structured fields or levels for each module | Rust's `log` with `env_logger`, Go's `slog` and .NET's console logger. Lookups are left out for good after Log4Shell (CVE-2021-44228); escaping follows CWE-117 (log forging) and Trojan Source (CVE-2021-42574). Lazy text is the macros of Rust and the interpolated-string handlers of C#, kept to `Log` alone: a general lazy argument would be a closure |
| Log, as built | `std/log.sg`, written in SG. The level is a `static atomic int` found the first time a line asks for it, from `SG_LOG`; a line that is off costs the branch of the static, a load and a compare, measured at 1.01 to 1.10 times a test of an atomic global in C (`bench/log`). A `$"..."` given to `Log` is made a `string` inside the `if`. `System.colourTerminal()`, which only `std` calls, asks whether the standard error is a terminal (`isatty`; on Windows `GetConsoleMode`, turning on `ENABLE_VIRTUAL_TERMINAL_PROCESSING`). `std.Log.debug(...)` is guarded too; a `Log` of the program or of a module named with `use` is not the language's and is left alone (choice 1456). Built on 19 November 2026, in the hundred-and-twenty-ninth slice Since the hundred-and-forty-second slice (20 November, choice 1594, from the second scan of the library): every character of format (Cf), C1, the separators of lines and the fillers are escaped, not only the marks of direction; `\u{e0041}` past U+FFFF. | |
| Regular expressions | *Decided on 20 November 2026 (choices 1457-1464, 1469-1474, 1477-1481).* `Regex` comes with the language, written in SG; TLS and encryption stay with C libraries. RE2's meaning: a match takes time in proportion to the text, so no back references and no look-around. Go's syntax with `\d \w \s \b` in ASCII and `\p` by the general category; `(?i)` by Unicode's simple case folding (C and S of CaseFolding.txt). `Regex.of(pattern)` takes only a pattern written in the program, checked when the program is compiled, and gives a `Regex`; kept in a `static` it is compiled once. `Regex.compile(pattern)` gives a `Result<Regex, RegexError>`, and a pattern written there is checked when the program is compiled too. `isMatch`, `find` (a `Match`: `start` and `end` in bytes), `findAll`, `captures` (by number and by name), `replaceAll` (`$1`, `${name}`, `$$`; `$1x` is group 1 and `x`, a group the pattern lacks a panic), `split`. Only the leftmost match the pattern prefers; a `string`, not bytes. A count at most 1000, a program at most 100,000 steps, groups 250 deep, a name given to two groups an error | Go keeps `regexp` in its library, Rust leaves `regex` to a crate; both mean what RE2 means. The pattern a program writes is known when it is compiled, so its mistakes are compile errors, where Go's `MustCompile` panics and Rust's `unwrap` does |
| Regular expressions, as built | `std/regex.sg`: the pattern is read into a tree, compiled to a program (Thompson's construction) and run by Pike's machine; plain text every match starts with is looked for first. A search makes its own lists of threads (`findAll` and `replaceAll` once), so a `Regex` is never written by a search and a `static` one is read by any number of tasks without a lock. Since the hundred-and-thirty-third slice (20 November, choices 1535-1539, found by the `grep` of 1418): an inner text every match holds is looked for first; lazy DFAs answer `isMatch`, find where the preferred match ends (forwards, in Pike's order) and where it starts (the reverse program, backwards), and Pike's machine runs only for the groups; their states and Pike's machines are kept in the `Regex` in a `shared locked` field, so a `static` one is still searched by any task, taking the lock once a search. `grep` over 100 MB is 1.5 to 1.8 times Rust's `regex` where the DFA reads every byte and faster where an inner text skips; 1.8 to 2.3 times Zig with PCRE2's JIT there (choices 1540-1542 put Zig in the trials in the place of Go). `x*` of an `x` that can match nothing is `(x+)?`, as Go. A pattern written in the program is read by `Regex.problem` when the program is compiled, the way a const is found. Tested by RE2's search tests (1772 cases, none wrong) and, while it was written, 207,000 random patterns and texts against Go's `regexp`; at about Go's speed then. The hundred-and-thirtieth slice Since the hundred-and-forty-second slice (20 November, choice 1594, from the second scan of the library): `x*` counts its body among the 100,000 steps (`((a{1000})*){1000}` was a program of a gigabyte); each DFA is emptied at about 2 MB counted in bytes, the moves past ASCII with them; the slots of the groups grow with the threads alive, by thread, not with the program; a `[:` with no `:]` is looked for once; the items of a class with `(?i)` are folded once, when the class is finished; a repetition after `\Q...\E` repeats its last character, as Go. Since the hundred-and-forty-sixth slice (21 November, choice 1598): Pike's machine copies a thread's slots once, as Rust's PikeVM, its closure walking on the thread's own slots and giving each save back as its stack unwinds; `captures` and `replaceAll` find where the match starts and ends first, by the DFAs or, when they give up, by Pike's machine with two slots, and the machine of every group runs only there. 4,000 groups kept alive take Rust's time with a fifth of its memory. | |
| Compression | *Decided on 20 November 2026 (choices 1465, 1466, 1482-1489).* `Deflate`, `Zlib` and `Gzip` come with the language, written in SG: `compress(read data, level)` gives a `List<byte>`, `decompress(read data, limit)` a `Result<List<byte>, CompressError>`; `writer(StreamWriter, level)` a `Compressor` and `reader(StreamReader, limit)` a `Decompressor` with the calls of a `StreamReader`. The bytes are zlib's at every level, 0 to 9 (a level written in the program outside them is a compile error). Opening always takes a limit, `TooLarge` past it, so a bomb is refused by the signature; strict: bytes after the end are `TrailingData`, checksums and lengths are checked; members of gzip one after the other open as one. `Adler32` comes into `std` beside `Crc32` | Go keeps `compress/*` in its library, Rust leaves `flate2` to a crate; both leave the bound of what is opened to the program. The same bytes as zlib let a test compare bytes and make SG's files the files every other tool makes |
| Compression, as built | `std/compression.sg`: zlib 1.3.1's `deflate.c` and `trees.c` written again in SG, with zlib's licence, and an inflate of tables (9 bits for a literal or a length, 6 for a distance, tables below them). Compared with zlib on 390 cases and 176 streams cut in pieces, all the same bytes, and on 23,000 streams changed at random, which both open into the same bytes or both refuse. Compressing takes about zlib's time, opening 2.6 times it ("Slower than C"). A folder is a module only when its name is spelled as the program writes it (1500). The hundred-and-thirty-first slice (1490-1500) Since the hundred-and-forty-second slice (20 November, choice 1594, from the second scan of the library): `readLine` and `readText` open more of the stream when a line or a character is cut by what is ready (they looped for ever); a limit of `long.MaxValue` no longer overflows; a symbol of codes is begun only with 48 bits held while more input is to come, so a valid stream whose long codes meet the end of the reader's block is no longer `Truncated` (a test made by a generator that follows the inflater's bits). | |
| Zip archives | *Decided on 20 November 2026 (choices 1467, 1490, 1491, 1501-1512).* `ZipReader.open(path)` reads the central directory and checks it before anything is given: a name that could lead out of a folder on any system (`..`, a root, `\`, `:`, NUL, a device name of Windows, empty) is `InsecurePath`, two entries of one name `DuplicateName`, data that lie over each other `Overlap`, more entries than the directory holds `Corrupt`; the archive is refused, not the entry. `entries()`, then `readBytes(read entry, limit)` or `copyTo(read entry, ref writer, limit)`, with `TooLarge`, `SizeMismatch` and `ChecksumMismatch`. `ZipWriter.create(target)` with `add`, `addFrom` and `addDirectory`, each taking the time of the entry, and `close`; zip64 where needed; the writer refuses what a reader refuses. No call unpacks a whole archive to the disk | Go's `archive/zip` reads and writes the same archives but leaves an overlapping bomb, a name of a device and a file named as a folder to the program, and its writer looks at no name; Rust leaves zip to a crate. A name checked where the archive is opened refuses Zip Slip before a program can write the entry |
| Zip archives, as built | `std/zip.sg`, over the DEFLATE of `std/compression.sg`: one inflater for all the entries of a reader and one compressor for a writer, restarted as zlib's `deflateReset` restarts it, so each entry has the bytes of `Deflate.compress(data, 6)`. Go's `archive/zip/testdata` reads as Go reads it but for a file named as a folder (1523), a time of DOS that makes no day (1515) and `NotZip`; Fifield's overlapping bomb is refused when it is opened. After a short entry only the places of the hash it filled are emptied (1524), the same bytes. 70,000 small entries are read in a third of Go's time and written in 96 ms against Go's 42 ms and zlib's own 125 ms: zlib builds the trees of Huffman for every block, which its bytes need. The hundred-and-thirty-second slice (1513-1524) Since the hundred-and-forty-second slice (20 November, choice 1594, from the second scan of the library): names are refused by `Path.joinInside`'s rule part by part (`./x`, `a//b`, `x.txt.`, `CONIN$`, C1), the message says "or name another file on another system"; two names that differ only in case are `DuplicateName`; names in two Unicode forms are not compared (no normalization in `std`), written in the library page; an NTFS time outside 1684-2255 and a zip64 locator past 2^63 are no longer panics. | |
| Folders and files, as built | `File.delete`, `File.move` (never takes the place of a file: `AlreadyExists`, told in the same step as the rename; a file onto itself too), `File.replace` (`rename`) and `File.copy` (a new file, its permissions without setuid, setgid and sticky) are built into `File`; `Directory` (`create`, `createAll`, `delete`, `deleteAll` that never follows a link, `list` sorted by the bytes of the names, `exists`, `current`, `temp`, `createTemp`), `DirectoryEntry`, `FileInfo` and `FileKind` are written in SG in the module `std` over calls of the system only it makes, and do the work of `File.info` and `File.exists`. `IoError` has `AlreadyExists`, `NotDirectory` and `DirectoryNotEmpty`, and `ENOTDIR` is `NotDirectory`. Approved on 15 November 2026 (choices 567 to 573) | |
| Seeking in a file | *Decided on 18 November 2026, before the hundred-and-ninth slice (choices 1211 to 1213).* The streams that are there move: `reader.seek(from)` and `writer.seek(from)` on a `StreamReader` and a `StreamWriter`, with no new type of file. Where to go is a `SeekFrom`: `Start(n)`, `End(n)` or `Current(n)`, and a place before the start is `InvalidInput`. A reader moves inside its block without asking the system; a writer writes what it holds first. The reader of a connection cannot seek, a compile error, since its type says so; a pipe, the program's input and a writer of `File.openAppend` give the new `IoError.NotSeekable`. `File.openWrite(path)` writes from the start over what a file holds, and makes a missing one | Rust's `Seek` and `SeekFrom`, on `File` and `BufReader`; C#'s `FileStream.Seek` and `File.OpenWrite`; C's `fseek` and `"r+"`. A type of its own that reads and writes (Rust's `File`, C#'s `FileStream`) would be one more type for what the streams already do |
| Seeking in a file, as built | `seek` gives the new place as a `Result<long, IoError>`, and there is no `position()` or `length()` beside it. `SeekFrom` is written in SG in the module `std`, each variant with a `long offset`. `Stdin.seek`, `Stdout.seek` and `Stderr.seek` are compile errors, as `seek` on the reader of a connection is. `NotSeekable` is the last variant of `IoError`; `ESPIPE`, and on Windows what is not a file on a disk, is `NotSeekable`. In an async function a reader seeks as it is, as quick as `File.delete`; a writer seeks with `await writer.seekAsync(from)`, which writes what it holds on a thread of input and output. A reader moves inside its block for a `Start` or a `Current` that lands there, forward or back, and asks the size only for `End`; after a seek the check of UTF-8 starts again and an `InvalidText` is still counted from the start of the file. A place past the end is no error; a write there leaves zero bytes before it. `File.openWrite` makes a missing file as `createText` does and has an async form. Built on 18 November 2026, in the hundred-and-ninth slice (choices 1215 to 1221), and approved the same day | Tokio's `BufWriter` flushes before it seeks, as `seekAsync` does |
| A temporary file | *Decided on 18 November 2026, before the hundred-and-ninth slice (choice 1214).* `File.createTemp()` makes a new empty file with a name nobody else has (`sg-XXXXXX`) inside `Directory.temp()`, that only the user of the program can read and write, and gives its path as a `Result<string, IoError>`; removing it is the program's work, as for `Directory.createTemp`. No file without a name that goes when it is closed | C's `tmpfile` has no name and goes when closed, but needs one stream that reads and writes; C#'s `Path.GetTempFileName` and Go's `os.CreateTemp` give a file with a name; Rust has it only in the `tempfile` crate |
| A temporary file, as built | Written in SG in the module `std`, as `File.info` is, on a call of the system only it makes: `mkstemp`, the file closed again; on Windows `CreateFileW` with `CREATE_NEW` and a list of access that gives only its owner anything. No async form, as `File.delete` has none. Built on 18 November 2026, in the hundred-and-ninth slice (choice 1222), and approved the same day | |
| The rest of `<math.h>` | *Decided on 18 November 2026, before the hundred-and-tenth slice (choices 1225 to 1236).* Methods of `float` and `double`, as `sqrt` is: `sinh`, `cosh`, `tanh`, `asinh`, `acosh`, `atanh`, `cbrt`, `exp2`, `erf`, `erfc` by C's names; `x.hypot(y)`; `expMinusOne` and `logOnePlus` (C's `expm1` and `log1p`); `gamma` and `logGamma` (`tgamma` and the logarithm of the size of Γ, without its sign, by `lgamma_r` where the system has it, since `lgamma` writes one variable for every thread); `x.ieeeRemainder(y)` beside `%`, which is `fmod` already; `copySign`, `isFinite`, `isSignNegative`; `x.exponent()`, an `int?` that is `null` for 0, `NaN` and the infinities, and `x.scaleByPowerOfTwo(n)` in place of `frexp` and `ldexp`; `x.fraction()` (`modf`, 0 for an infinity); `nextUp` and `nextDown` from the bits, without `nextafter`; `roundToEven` (`rint`, the rounding mode never changed). Without an operating system the ones of the library are errors and the ones written from the bits are not; in a const the exact ones are in and those the library of each system rounds in its own way are not. The interpreter calls the same functions of C, so both give the same digits on one machine. C's names get a hint (`fmod` is `%`) | Rust's names where they read (`hypot`, `cbrt`), C#'s `IEEERemainder`; Rust's `exp_m1` and C#'s `ExpM1` read as a puzzle. A pair for `frexp` would need a tuple, which the language does not have |
| The rest of `<math.h>`, as built | The library's functions are emitted by their C names (`expm1f` for a `float`); `logGamma` is a helper over `lgamma_r` (over `lgamma` on Windows), `fraction` one over `modf`; `exponent`, `nextUp` and `nextDown` read the bits, and `copySign` is the C compiler's own, so a program without an operating system has them too. Built on 18 November 2026, in the hundred-and-tenth slice (choices 1241 to 1248), and approved the same day | |
| Integers: the absolute value, `clamp`, saturating | *Decided on 18 November 2026, before the hundred-and-tenth slice (choices 1237 to 1239).* `abs` on the signed types follows the rule of overflow for `int.MinValue`, as `-x` does; `tryAbs`, `wrappingAbs`, and `unsignedAbs`, which gives the unsigned type of the same size and never fails; `abs` on an unsigned type is an error. `tryNegate` and `wrappingNegate` on every integer type, named by the panic of `-x`. `x.clamp(low, high)` on integers and fractional numbers, the three meeting as with `+`: a low end above the high end panics in every build, and is a compile error when both are written as numbers; on a fractional number `NaN` stays `NaN`, and an end that is `NaN` panics. `saturatingAdd`, `saturatingSubtract`, `saturatingMultiply`, and `saturatingToX()` from every integer and fractional type, `NaN` giving 0 | Rust's `abs`, `checked_abs`, `unsigned_abs`, `clamp` and `saturating_*`; C# throws for `Math.Abs(int.MinValue)` and has `CreateSaturating` for the conversions |
| Zeroing a secret | *Decided on 18 November 2026, before the hundred-and-tenth slice (choice 1240).* `Memory.zeroSecurely(ref buffer)` writes zeros over a list, an array or a window of plain data, which the C compiler keeps although nothing reads them again, without `unsafe`. Copies made before (an array moved, a list that grew) are not wiped. Built the same day: `memset` and an empty `asm` that takes the address and may read all memory, as glibc's `explicit_bzero` does | C23's `memset_explicit`, not on macOS or Windows yet; C#'s `CryptographicOperations.ZeroMemory`; Rust has it in the `zeroize` crate |
| Searching items in order | *Decided on 18 November 2026, before the hundred-and-eleventh slice (choices 1249 to 1254).* On a list, an array, a slice and a range in front of them (whose places are counted from its start), of `Comparable` items, as `sort` asks: `xs.binarySearch(v)` gives a `nint?`, the place or `null`, as `indexOf` does, and of equal items always the first, so on items in order it gives what `indexOf` gives; `xs.lowerBound(v)` gives the first place whose item is not less than `v`, `xs.upperBound(v)` the first whose item is greater, both a `nint`. That the items are in order is not checked, which would look at each one: out of order the answer is some place among them or `null`, never a panic or a place outside. `double` is searched in the total order of `sort`, so a `NaN` is found. A search with a function that compares or by a key waits for `sortBy` | Rust's `binary_search` gives a `Result` with the place to insert as its error, and any one of equal items, to stay free to change how it searches; C#'s `BinarySearch` gives the complement of that place, Go's a pair. The first of equal items costs nothing: one test of the order at each step, as the fastest searches have it, and one more at the end. The names are C++'s (`lower_bound`, `upper_bound`; Python's `bisect_left` and `bisect_right`) |
| Searching items in order, as built | One helper for each type of item, a list and an array searched through the window of their items; the interpreter takes the same steps, so a `compare` the type wrote is called as often in both. The names of other languages get a hint. Built on 18 November 2026, in the hundred-and-eleventh slice (choices 1255 to 1257), and approved the same day | |
| Waiting under a lock | *Decided on 18 November 2026, before the hundred-and-twelfth slice (choices 1259 to 1265).* `lock jobs as q when q.items.length() > 0 \|\| q.done { ... }`, also with several values (`lock a as x, b as y when ...`): the condition is computed under the locks, each name lent to read, and while it fails the locks are let go and the task sleeps in one step, then takes them again and looks again; the block itself still waits for nothing. Every `lock` block on one of the values that ends wakes those that wait, so there is no `notify` to forget; with nobody waiting that costs one read. That no more will come is said by a field of the program's own (`q.done`), set in a `lock` block; there is no `else`. A task that waits and is the only owner of every value, with no `weak` look at them, waits for what nobody could change: a panic, whose hint is that field, and an owner that lets go wakes those that wait to see it. In an async function it is `await lock ... when`, where the task waits and its thread goes on; without `await` it is an error there. A time limit and a cancellation come later | Ada's protected entries (`entry Take when Count > 0`); C#'s `lock` with `while` and `Monitor.Wait`, Rust's `Condvar::wait_while`, C11's `cnd_wait`, all of which need a separate condition that someone has to notify. A condition computed before the lock is taken would race; one that waits only at the head of the block keeps "A `lock` block does not wait" |
| Waiting under a lock, as built | *Built on 18 November 2026, in the hundred-and-twelfth slice.* The condition does not read an `atomic`, the clock, random bytes, or input and output written in it or in the language's own functions it calls, which change while no `lock` ends and would not wake it; `await lock` without `when` is an error; only a program with `when` has the list of waiters in front of a `locked` value (80 bytes on macOS, still 48 on Linux). Approved on 18 November 2026 (choices 1266 to 1268). The list is guarded by its lowest bit, so an owner can let go inside a `lock` of the same value; a thread sleeps on a condition of its own, an async task is woken through the pool; the interpreter wakes a fiber left the only owner at each change of turn, and stops on waits that wait for each other (399). Choices 1269 to 1275, approved the same day | In compiled code a circle of such waits, or several owners that all wait, waits for ever until the count of waiting tasks of the hardening |
| Paths and the environment | `Path.join` puts a second path that starts with `/` after the first; `fileName`, `extension` (with its `.`, none for a `.` only at the start or the end) and `parent` give a view of the path. `Environment.get(name)` gives a `Result<string, VariableError>`; the variables are only read, and the folder the program works in is not changed. The interpreter asks the system through Rust's `std::fs`, one call of the system each, and compiled code through `FILES_RUNTIME`. Approved on 15 November 2026 (choices 574 to 577) | |
| Folders, measured | Listing a folder of ten thousand files takes 0.99 times `readdir` and `qsort` in C; a million `File.info` 1.00 times `stat`, once a short path is ended on the stack. Approved on 15 November 2026 (choices 578 to 580) | |
| Waiting for a task in one line | `wait` takes the task a call gives (`Task.delay(100).wait()`, `Task.run(f, x).wait()`), as `await` does; a borrowed task stays an error. Approved on 15 November 2026 (choice 581) | Code that is not async pauses without a variable for the delay |
| A panic of the language's own module | Shown at the call of the program that led there, as `#[track_caller]` does in Rust, without the hint that named the module's code; a call between functions of the module keeps the program's place. In compiled code each function of the module takes the place of its call as one more parameter; the methods the runtime calls on its own (`format`, `equals`, `compare`, `hash`, `dispose`) do not. Approved on 15 November 2026 (choices 582, 583) | The line shown is one the reader wrote; one more parameter, which costs nothing measured |
| `for` over what `try` opens, compiled | The collection is computed once before the first round, so the `try` leaves the function there as at the start of any statement. Approved on 15 November 2026 (choice 584) | |
| The C, without warnings | Every compiled program builds under `-Wall -Wextra -Werror` with clang and gcc in the tests; a function nobody calls is left a warning, and three of gcc's analyses that see paths that cannot happen are allowed only in the one program each is in. What SG lets a program leave unused is marked used in the C. A failed call of the system never reads as success (`errno` 0 becomes `EIO`). Approved on 15 November 2026 (choices 585 to 587) | A warning of the C compiler fails a test instead of reaching a user; whether a variable written and never read becomes an error is an open question |
| A variable nothing reads, as built | One never used and one only written are errors at the name, the second with its first write; a write through a borrow, `shared`, `weak`, raw memory or a slice is a use, through a `T*` it is not. A parameter is not looked at, nor a `var` of a `Disposable` type, of a struct with one in its fields, or a `shared` owner of one (changed in the slice: decided for a `Disposable` type alone). The names of `for`, of patterns and of `as` and `lock` blocks are held to it (the last two added in the slice), the hint the pattern written again with `_`; a write to a copy of a struct has the borrow as its hint. Approved on 15 November 2026 (choices 588 to 592) | |
| A name that hides another | A local variable, the name of a `for`, of a `match` arm or of an `is` (also one seen after its `if` or `while`) may not take the name of a local variable or a parameter of a block around it that is still open: `var x = 1` and, in a block inside, `var x = 2` is a compile error, as in C# (CS0136), not a second variable as in Rust; so is a local of the function's body with the name of a parameter. The error points at both and says to give the new variable another name. Blocks side by side may use one name (two `for i`, the arms of one `match`), and so may the code after a block has ended, since a name's scope starts where it is declared: that much is looser than C#, whose scope is the whole block. `_` is not a name. A field is never hidden, since it is reached only through `this`; the names of functions, types and modules are not part of the rule. It is a check of the checker alone. *Decided on 17 November 2026, during the ninety-first slice; built in the ninety-second, its details decided before it (choices 886 to 894) and approved on 17 November 2026* | "Caught when compiled": a name that means two things in one function is a mistake waiting to happen, and Rust's shadowing is mostly for changing a type, which SG writes with a new name. The rule found three in the repository's own code, one a parameter of the language's own module |
| Which system | `OperatingSystem.isWindows()`, `OperatingSystem.isLinux()` and `OperatingSystem.isMacOS()` give a `bool` known when the program is built. Both branches of an `if` on one are checked on every system, and the C of only the branch of the system the program is built for is written, so the other may call what this system has not. Any program may ask, not only the language's own module (the author's choice). A program built without an operating system is on none. *Decided and built on 17 November 2026, in the ninety-seventh slice (choice 980)* | C#'s `OperatingSystem.IsWindows()`, Rust's `cfg!(windows)`: a mistake in the branch of Windows is seen on macOS too. Rust's `#[cfg]` and Go's `_windows.go` leave the code of the other system unchecked |
| The rule, built and followed in C | The checker notes what each name stands for, and `unread.rs` tells reads from writes after every other check, so it never hides another error; a use found another way counts for every variable of that name before it, so a read is never missed. Compiled code no longer marks these names used (`for _` and parameters keep it), and the C compiler's warnings check the rule again. Approved on 15 November 2026 (choices 593 to 596) | |
| Every value of a call | *Decided on 16 November 2026, in the seventy-seventh slice (choices 614 to 616), and approved.* A call that gives a value is a compile error when the value is not used: a function or a method of your own, one of the language's own module, a built-in method and a function of C alike, and the value after `try` and `await` too. `xs.removeLast()`, `seen.add(x)`, `try cmd.status()` and `noisy(4)` alone are errors; `_ = xs.removeLast()` drops a value on purpose, where the reader sees it. It replaces "Methods that change and give" and "Functions of your own": there is no list of calls that may stand alone | Zig refuses every value thrown away the same way; Rust warns only on what is marked `#[must_use]`. No cost at run time |
| Other programs, as built | `Command.create(program)` with `arg`, `args`, `directory`, `setVariable`, `removeVariable`, `clearVariables` and `input`; `run()` gives `Result<void, ProcessError>`, `output()` `Result<string, ProcessError>` (what the program wrote, UTF-8), `status()` `Result<ExitStatus, IoError>` and takes no end for an error. No shell reads the words. `ExitStatus` is `Code(byte)` or `Signal(int)`; `ProcessError` is `Io(IoError)` (it could not start, or its output was not UTF-8; `try` wraps an `IoError` in it) or `Failed(ExitStatus)`, any end but 0. A name is looked for in the folders of `PATH` that start with `/`, by code written in SG; a path with `/` is taken from this program's folder. The program gets a copy of the environment with the changes, never changing ours; a word or a name it cannot take is `IoError.InvalidInput`. What was printed goes out first. An async function does not make these calls. Approved on 16 November 2026 (choices 597 to 606, 608) | Go's `exec.Command` with Rust's `Command` methods; the search skips `.` as Go's does since 1.19 |
| Descriptors a program gets | Only the input, the output and the standard error reach a program started: every descriptor the runtime opens is close-on-exec, and one this program was given open is closed for the child as well (`POSIX_SPAWN_CLOEXEC_DEFAULT` on macOS, `posix_spawn_file_actions_addclosefrom_np` on glibc 2.34 and later; the interpreter marks them in the child before it starts). A glibc older than 2.34 still passes one this program was given. Approved on 16 November 2026 (choice 607) | Four `open` calls of the runtime were found without close-on-exec, and fixed |
| Other programs, in the interpreter and in C | The interpreter uses Rust's `std::process::Command` and copies the child's output and standard error through pipes; compiled code uses `posix_spawn` and one `poll` loop that writes the input and reads the output straight into the `string`. A program that reads or writes a file asks glibc for POSIX 2008. Approved on 16 November 2026 (choices 609, 610) | |
| Other programs, measured | Five thousand runs of `true` take 0.70 times the time of `posix_spawnp` in C (the search asks `stat` and `access` where `posix_spawnp` tries to start the program in each folder); keeping 125 MB of output takes 1.49 times C's, all of it the UTF-8 check (without it, the time of C). Approved on 16 November 2026 (choices 611 to 613) | In "Slower than C" with the other checks of text |
| A program that runs | `cmd.start()` gives a `Result<Process, IoError>` and goes on. `pipeInput()`, `pipeOutput()` and `pipeError()` ask for pipes, only for `start`; a stream not asked for is ours, and a text of `input` is written by a thread while the program runs. `takeInput()` gives a `StreamWriter?`, `takeOutput()` and `takeError()` a `StreamReader?`, each `null` once taken; they are owned and go to other tasks. Writing to a program that stopped reading is `IoError.ConnectionReset`, never the signal. `wait()` takes the `Process`, closes the input it still holds and lets go of its readers, and gives a `Result<ExitStatus, IoError>`; `kill()` sends 9, `terminate()` 15, `id()` gives the number. Approved on 16 November 2026 (choices 617 to 621, 623, 630) | Rust's `spawn` and `take()` of its pipes, Go's text written by a goroutine; a program is taken away only by a wait, so its number never names another one |
| A program is waited for | A `Process` in a local variable that is not waited for on every path is a compile error, as a task; one that ends without a wait (a way out with an error, a field, an item) is stopped with the signal 9 and waited for. A name of `is Ok(p)` is lent and not seen by the rule. Approved on 16 November 2026 (choice 622) | Rust leaves a running program and a zombie; tokio's `kill_on_drop(true)` and Go's `CommandContext` stop it |
| Other programs, the async way | `runAsync`, `outputAsync`, `statusAsync` and `waitAsync`, with a token that may come last; a cancel stops the program with the signal 9, and a token cancelled before the call starts nothing. Compiled, the end is waited for on the loop of the network: the pidfd on epoll on Linux, `EVFILT_PROC` on kqueue on macOS, a thread with `waitid(WNOWAIT)` where Linux has no pidfd; the pipes are read on the loop as a connection is. The interpreter waits on a thread and writes the shared output of a started program when it is waited for. Approved on 16 November 2026 (choices 624, 625, 628, 629) | As tokio on Linux; Go holds a thread for each wait, which would fill the 512 threads of input and output |
| Async methods | A method of a struct or an enum, and a function of a type, can be `async`. One that lends `this` is awaited where it is written; one that takes it by value may start as a task under the thread rule. `create`, `format`, `equals`, `hash` and `compare` are not async. A function or a method of the language's own module may leave out a last `read CancellationToken`, which is then `CancellationToken.None`. Decided by the user on 16 November 2026, in the seventy-eighth slice (choices 626, 627), and approved | The async versions of `Command` and `Process` are written in SG; C# and Rust have async methods. A function of the program still takes what it declares, since there are no overloads |
| Other programs, async measured | Reading the output of `cat` line by line takes 0.85 times `popen` and `getline` in C, 1.03 times the async way; five thousand `statusAsync` of `true` take 0.71 times `posix_spawnp` and `waitpid`. Approved on 16 November 2026 (choices 631 to 633) | |
| Unicode tests of a character | *Decided on 16 November 2026, before the seventy-ninth slice (choices 635 to 638).* On `char`, from the tables of Unicode 17.0: `isLetter` (general category L), `isNumeric` (N), `isAlphanumeric`, `isUpper` (Lu), `isLower` (Ll), `isControl` (Cc); `toUpper` and `toLower` give a `char` by the simple mapping (`'ß'.toUpper()` is `'ß'`). Not on `byte`, which keeps the ASCII methods. Out: normalization, grapheme clusters, sorting by a language, width, names in Unicode | C#, Go and Java take the categories; Rust's `is_alphabetic` is wider and its `to_uppercase` gives an iterator, whose type cannot be written here |
| The case of a text | *Decided on 16 November 2026, before the seventy-ninth slice (choices 639 to 642).* `toUpper` and `toLower` give a new `string` by the full mappings, with `Σ` at the end of a word as `ς` (Rust's `to_uppercase` and `to_lowercase`). `equalsIgnoreCase` compares by the full folding without allocating, `foldCase` gives the folded text. No language is taken into account; the Turkish and Azeri mappings are left for later | One answer on every machine; C#'s `ToUpper` follows the culture of the machine |
| One set of Unicode tables | *Decided on 16 November 2026, before the seventy-ninth slice (choice 636).* The tables are made by a script from the Unicode Character Database (each file checked against its SHA-256) and kept in the repository; the data files are not. The interpreter and compiled code read the same tables, not Rust's own `char` methods. In compiled code they are only in a program that uses them | The two sides give the same answers whatever version of Unicode Rust knows |
| The general category of a character | *Decided on 20 November 2026 (choices 1475, 1476), and built in the hundred-and-thirtieth slice.* The tables take the general category of every code point (five bits of its record). `c.category()` gives a `UnicodeCategory`: Unicode's 29 long names as SG writes a name (`UppercaseLetter` ... `Unassigned`; no `Surrogate`, which no `char` is), `abbreviation()` the short one (`Lu`). Written in `std/unicode.sg` over a lookup only `std` calls | C#'s `char.GetUnicodeCategory` gives the same; Rust's library has none, Go asks `unicode.Is(unicode.Lu, r)` |
| Datagrams | *Decided on 16 November 2026, before the eightieth slice (choices 648, 652 to 658), and built in it.* `UdpSocket.bind(address)` takes what `TcpListener.bind` takes; `sendTo(bytes, to)` sends one datagram whole and `receiveFrom(ref buffer)` gives a `Received` with `count` and `from`; `connect(to)` gives `send` and `receive` a default address, and `ConnectionRefused` when nobody listens there. Every method lends the socket with `read`, so a `shared` socket serves several tasks; no `close`. The async versions take a token that may come last. A datagram that does not fit is `IoError.MessageTooLong`. Multicast, broadcast, the options of a socket and `peek` later. Approved the same day | Rust's `UdpSocket` without a crate; Rust and Go cut a datagram that does not fit short without a word |
| `SocketAddress` | *Decided on 16 November 2026, before the eightieth slice (choices 649 to 651), and built in it.* Written in SG in the language's own module: two `ulong` for the host, whether it is v6, and the port, `Copyable`, `Equatable`, `Hashable`. `parse` reads a number only (`InvalidInput` otherwise) and it is printed as RFC 5952 asks; `lookup` and `lookupAsync` give a `List<SocketAddress>` in the order the system gives, `HostNotFound` as for `connect`. The zone of a v6 address is not kept. Approved the same day | One reading and one printing for the interpreter and compiled code |
| The addresses of a connection | *Decided on 16 November 2026, before the eightieth slice (choice 660), and built in it.* `stream.localAddress()` gives a `SocketAddress`, `stream.remoteAddress()` a `Result<SocketAddress, IoError>`: `ConnectionReset` once the other side has broken the connection off (`ENOTCONN`). Approved the same day | Rust's `peer_addr` gives a `Result` too |
| Datagrams in compiled code | *Built in the eightieth slice (choices 656, 659).* A socket blocks, so a call without async is one call of the system; an async call asks the system not to wait (`MSG_DONTWAIT`) and waits on the loop of the network through a copy of the socket, since several tasks may wait for one; a receive is `recvmsg`, whose `MSG_TRUNC` says the datagram did not fit. The echo takes the time of C without async and 1.19 times C (2.41 on Linux) the async way. Approved on 16 November 2026 | In "Slower than C" with the two wakes of the TCP echo |

## Deliberately deferred

- **comptime:** a const given the value of a call was built in the hundred-and-fourth slice and taken out in the hundred-and-forty-fifth ("No call in a const", choice 1606); a value a function finds is a `static`. The rest of comptime, making types when the program is compiled (Zig's way to generics), stays out.
- **Not in the first version:** macros. Async was in this line until 14 November 2026; its direction is now decided (below), and it is built after threads.
- **After the first version:** implementing an interface outside the definition of the type (`extend`), using an interface
  as a type (`read Shape`), associated types, default method bodies, operator definitions, constant parameters, a variable that `is not` opens into the outer scope, a `sync` that pushes the data to disk after `close` (`Flush(true)` in C#), generic type aliases (`using Link<T> = shared checked T`), lazy list chains (like LINQ).

*Decided on 15 November 2026: no overloads, for now.* Two functions or methods do not share a name; a second form takes a
name of its own (`nextUlong()` beside `nextLong(low, high)`). An overload costs nothing at run time, since the call is
chosen when the program is compiled, but it meets the rules SG already has: a number takes the type it is used as, a
`byte` widens by itself and a value goes where a `T?` goes, so `f(5)` beside `f(int)` and `f(long)` would need the
ranking of conversions that C++ and C# have; the name of a function is a value (`apply(double, 5)`) and would stop
naming one; and a message would list candidates instead of saying what is missing. Go, Rust and Zig have none. If
the need grows, the doors are overloads by the number of arguments only, with no ranking, or default values of
parameters; both cost nothing at run time.

*Deferred on 15 November 2026: properties.* A struct has fields, and what does work is a method: `text.length()`,
`source.token()`. A property (C#'s `get` and `set`) would look like a field and run code, so `x.y` would no longer
tell that it is cheap (`source.token` costs an atomic count); what a getter gives, owned or lent, would be a second
contract; and a setter has no place in memory, so `ref x.y` and `x.y += 1` would need rules of their own. The common
use, read from outside and written from inside, is a `public readonly` field. Talked through again if a real need
shows up.

*Deferred on 12 November 2026: generics of your own.* They cost nothing at run time, but they are the largest source of
concepts in the design (type parameters, `where`, your own `interface`, associated types, conditional `Copyable`), and
eleven slices of the interpreter ran without them. `Result<T, E>`, `T?`, `List<T>`, `HashMap<K, V>` and `HashSet<T>`
are known to the compiler and stay generic; the built-in interfaces stay as names written after `:`. A container of your
own is written for the type it holds. If a real need shows up it is talked through with that example. The rows moved
out of the decisions table, as they were:

| Topic | Decision | Reason |
|---|---|---|
| Generics | C# notation, compiled separately for each type | No run-time cost, in exchange the binary grows |
| Form of `where` | Several conditions as `where T : A, B`, a separate `where` for each subject, in any order: `where K : Hashable where r from name`. At type level `struct TopList<T> where T : Comparable`, and a function that uses the generic writes the condition again. A method can put a condition on the type's parameter: `void sort(ref this) where T : Comparable`, and if the type does not meet the condition only that call gives an error | C# notation. C# has no condition on a method, but without the deferred `extend` this is how `List<T>.sort()` is written |
| `for` pattern | Works by pattern, like `foreach` in C#. `takeItems()` is called for `for x in xs`, `readItems()` for `read`, `refItems()` for `ref`, and the returned value implements `Iterator<T>`. If the value is already an `Iterator` it is used directly | It could not be written with an interface, because associated types and overloading on the receiver are deferred |

### Async, the direction

*Decided on 14 November 2026, to be built after threads.* Async takes C#'s behaviour, compiled the way Rust compiles it:
`async` functions, `await`, the same `Task<R>` that `Task.run` gives, and cancellation tokens. The rows of threads in the
decisions table ("Starting a task" to "Tasks in the interpreter") were chosen so that nothing in them changes when async
comes.

```
async Result<int, IoError> countLines(string path, CancellationToken token) {
    var text = try await File.readTextAsync(read path, read token)
    int lines = 0
    for line in text.lines() { lines += 1 }
    return Ok(lines)
}

async Result<void, IoError> main() {
    var source = CancellationSource.after(5000)          // cancelled after five seconds
    var first = countLines("a.txt", source.token())      // Task<Result<int, IoError>>, already running
    var second = countLines("b.txt", source.token())
    print(try await first + try await second)
    return Ok()
}
```

| Topic | Direction | Reason |
|---|---|---|
| How it is compiled | Each `async` function becomes a state machine; the machines of the functions it awaits are put inside its own. A task takes one allocation when it starts and none for each `await`; there is no garbage collector | Rust's cost. C# allocates a `Task` for each async call and collects it later |
| Starting | An async function called without `await` gives a `Task<R>` that has started, and runs on the caller until its first `await` that has to wait, as in C#. `await f()` takes no allocation. `f()` alone on a line is an error, as `Task.run(...)` is ("Every task is waited for") | In Rust a future does nothing until it is polled, so a forgotten `.await` runs nothing and only a warning says so |
| One runtime | A pool of threads, a clock for delays and threads for input and output that has to block come with the language, and only programs that use tasks carry them. *Changed on 15 November 2026, before the sixty-first slice:* a file cannot be waited for with epoll or kqueue, since it is always ready, so a read or a write of a file runs on a thread of its own and wakes the task when it is done, as Tokio does it; the loop that waits for the network (epoll, kqueue, IOCP) comes with the network. *Built on 15 November 2026, in the sixty-fifth slice:* one thread waits with epoll or kqueue for every socket a task waits for ("The loop of the network"). *Changed on 16 November 2026, in the eighty-first slice:* that thread is one of the pool, the one with nothing to do, as Tokio's driver. *Changed on 16 November 2026, in the eighty-third slice:* each thread of the pool has a queue of its own, which the others steal half of when they have nothing, and a slot for the task that the task it runs woke; a task woken outside the pool goes on a queue they share, as in Tokio | Rust has several runtimes (tokio, async-std, smol) whose libraries do not fit each other |
| Cancellation | `CancellationSource` (`source.token()`, `source.cancel()`, `CancellationSource.after(ms)`); a token is passed as a parameter, and a task stops only where it looks at its token, as in C#. Dropping a handle cancels nothing, and a handle cannot be dropped anyway | No task stops at a place it did not choose |
| Waiting the wrong way | `t.wait()` in an async function is an error whose hint is `await t`; a call that blocks (input and output that is not async, or a function that does it) in an async function is an error whose hint is its async version or `Task.run`. `Task.run` of a function that blocks runs it on threads of its own; the compiler knows which functions block, so the program does not choose | C#'s best known deadlock is waiting for async code from code that is not async (`.Result`, `.Wait()`), and a call that blocks inside async code starves the pool in C# and Rust alike. Tokio makes the program choose between `spawn` and `spawn_blocking` |
| Locks | `await` inside a `lock` block is an error ("A `lock` block does not wait"); there is one kind of lock. An ordinary `lock` can be used in async code, since nothing inside it waits | Rust needs a second, async lock to hold across `await`, and holding the first one there deadlocks or does not compile |
| No `Pin` | A state machine is never a value the program holds; a `Task` lies on the heap and does not move | Rust needs `Pin` because a future is a value that could move while something points into it |
| Calling itself | An async function that calls itself, directly or around, is an error whose hint is to keep the call's `Task` in a variable, which puts it on the heap | A state machine that holds itself would have no end; Rust writes `Box::pin` |
| Not taken from C# | `async void`, `ConfigureAwait` and the synchronization context, `ValueTask`, `.Result`, exceptions (an error is a `Result`, `try await f()`) | Each is a second way, or a known trap |
| Waiting for several | `Task.whenAll`, `Task.whenAny`; `whenAny` cancels nothing, and the tasks that lost are still waited for. *Built on 15 November 2026* ("A list of tasks and its end", "What `Task.whenAny` gives") | The rule "Every task is waited for" holds |
| A list of tasks and its end | *Decided on 15 November 2026, before the sixty-third slice.* A `List<Task<R>>` is given to `Task.whenAll`, or emptied by a loop `while tasks.length() > 0` with no `break`, after which the compiler counts its tasks as waited for; anything else is a compile error. A way out with an error waits for what is left in it ("An exit with an error waits for a task") | The loop that takes tasks one by one ends only when none is left, which the compiler can see as it sees `while x is null`. Swift's task group waits for what is left when it ends and drops what they give, which would let an error go unseen |
| What `Task.whenAny` gives | *Decided on 15 November 2026, before the sixty-third slice.* `await Task.whenAny(ref tasks)` gives what the first task to end gave and takes that task out of the list, as Rust's `JoinSet::join_next` | C# gives the task that ended, so that its state can be looked at; an SG task has no state beside what it gives, so the value comes in one step |
| No `isCompleted` | *Decided on 15 November 2026, before the sixty-third slice.* A task cannot be asked whether it has ended without waiting for it. What C# keeps on a task (`IsFaulted`, `IsCanceled`, `Exception`) is in what an SG task gives: an `Error(...)`, a `Cancelled` | A loop that asks without waiting holds its thread: in the interpreter, where tasks change hands only where one waits, it would never end. `Task.whenAny` answers which task has ended |
| A writer dropped in async code | *Decided on 15 November 2026, before the sixty-fourth slice.* A writer an async function drops without `closeAsync` (on a way out with an error, at the end of a block, as a field, an item or a `shared` value that ends, or given another value) has its block written and its file closed on a thread of input and output, and the function awaits that where the writer is dropped; the error is lost, as when a writer ends without `close` in code that is not async. The writers and the tasks left behind on a way out end together, in the reverse order of their names, one after the other. A cancelled token does not stop the closing | C#'s `await using` and Python's `async with` await the closing on the way out of an exception; Rust has no async drop, and Tokio's `BufWriter` loses what it holds. Before, the writer was closed on a thread of the pool, which held it while it wrote |
| The async methods of a writer | *Decided on 15 November 2026, before the sixty-fourth slice.* What the block of 64 KB can take is written into it in place, without waiting, as a read the block can answer ("A read the block can answer, in place"); a full block goes out on a thread of input and output while the task waits, `flushAsync` waits only when the block holds something, and `closeAsync` always goes to a thread. `Stdout` and `Stderr` have the async methods too, done in place and in order with `print`; `closeAsync` on them is an error | A function given a `ref StreamWriter` works when it is given `Stdout`, and writing a line costs no wait unless a block is full |
| Channels | When tasks send each other messages, two can wait for each other, which no compiler can see. If every task waits and no input, output or timer is pending, it is a panic that says so, as Go does; in the interpreter this is exact. *Decided on 18 November 2026, before the hundred-and-thirteenth slice (choices 1276 to 1289).* `var jobs = Channel<int>(16)` is the end that receives, with room for 16 items, always at least one, taken once (no channel without a limit, none with no room); `jobs.sender()` gives a `Sender<int>`, `.clone()` one more end of either kind, and several receivers share the items, each to one of them in order. `tx.send(x)` waits while the channel is full and gives `false` when no end receives any more, the item dropped; `jobs.receive()` waits while it is empty and gives `null` once every sender has ended and nothing is left: there is no `close`. `for job in jobs` receives until then. `sendAsync` and `receiveAsync` in async code; a token may come last on the four, which then give a `Result` whose error is `Cancelled`. No `select`, `tryReceive` or `length`. An item follows the thread rule and is never a `T?`; a send or a receive is not in a `lock` block; a `for` over a channel while a sender this function made of it lives is a compile error. What is left ends with the last end | Never a program that hangs without a word.
