Rust's `mpsc` ends the same way, by `drop`, but has one receiver and needs a tuple; Go's `close` twice is a panic and its unbuffered channel and `select` are a second way; C#'s `Writer.Complete()` can be forgotten |
| Channels, as built | *Built on 18 November 2026, in the hundred-and-thirteenth slice.* The async versions are awaited where they are written; a `for` over a channel in a variable does not take it; a wait the channel lets go on is not cancelled, and a cancelled send drops its item; a channel of a `StreamWriter` is not here yet; an item not taken is dropped inside the call. In compiled code one block holds the header and the ring, a thread sleeps on a condition of its own and an async task is woken through the pool; an item wakes the first receiver that waits, and the last end of a kind all of the other kind. The interpreter looks at those that wait at each change of turn, and stops on tasks that wait for each other (399). Choices 1290 to 1299, approved on 18 November 2026 | In compiled code tasks that wait on channels for each other wait for ever until the count of waiting tasks of the hardening |
| Time zones and dates in text | *Decided on 18 November 2026, before the hundred-and-fourteenth slice (choices 1300 to 1310).* The offset of the system's time zone at a moment comes from the system, through one more call that only `std` makes (`localtime_r` on Linux and macOS, which reads `TZ`; on Windows the rules Windows keeps for each year). `DateTimeOffset` is a moment with its offset from UTC: `d.toLocal()`, `d.withOffset(offset)` (`InvalidOffset` for a day or more), `DateTimeOffset.local(y, m, d, h, mi, s)` from what the clocks of the place show (`SkippedLocalTime` in the hour summer time skips, the earlier moment in the hour it repeats), `t.toUtc()` back, no `now()`. Its parts are those of the place; `offset()` is a `Duration`; `plus` keeps the offset; equal by moment and offset, in order by moment then offset. Printed `2026-11-18T12:30:05+03:00` (`+00:00` for 0, `:ss` for the old local mean times). `DateTime.parse` and `DateTimeOffset.parse` read RFC 3339 strictly, the first taking the moment to UTC; `DateError` gains `InvalidText(position)`, `InvalidOffset` and `SkippedLocalTime`. No pattern of your own: the parts and `{n:D2}` write any form. No zones by name yet | `DateTime` stays in UTC and in 8 bytes, so a local time is never taken for UTC; Go's one `time.Time` compares the place with `==`. A format written with the parts is checked when the program is compiled, where a pattern is read when it runs |
| Time zones and dates in text, as built | *Built on 18 November 2026, in the hundred-and-fourteenth slice.* A `DateTime` where a `DateTimeOffset` goes, or the other way, is an error whose hint is `toUtc()`, or `toLocal()` and `withOffset`. An offset with a part of a second is `InvalidOffset`. `parse` checks the whole form first, then the parts as `DateTime.utc` does, then the offset, then the range with the fraction. `local` reads the offsets a day before, at and a day after, and keeps each moment at which its offset is in force. Choices 1311 to 1317, approved on 18 November 2026 | macOS takes the rules of a `TZ` written out only from 1970 on; the offset is read again at each call |
| A cancellation in a `Result` | *Decided on 15 November 2026.* An error of its own type, the built-in `Cancelled`: `token.check()` gives a `Result<void, Cancelled>`, `Task.delay(ms, read token)` a `Task<Result<void, Cancelled>>`. `IoError` has the variant `Cancelled(Cancelled cancelled)`, so async input and output still gives an `IoError`, and `try token.check()` turns one into the other by "Error conversion" | A cancellation is told apart from a failure without a second error type in every signature. The cost: a `match` that names every variant of `IoError` needs one more arm |
| The names of async input and output | *Decided on 15 November 2026.* C#'s: the name of the function that holds the thread with `Async` at the end (`File.readTextAsync`, `reader.readLineAsync`), and a token that may come last, lent with `read` | One name to learn for each piece of work, and the token is not written where nothing is to be cancelled |
| The network, as written | *Decided on 15 November 2026, before the sixty-fifth slice.* TCP first: `TcpListener.bind("host:port")`, `listener.port()`, `listener.accept()`, `TcpStream.connect("host:port")`, `stream.receive(ref buffer)` (what has come, how many, 0 at the end) and `stream.send(bytes)` (all of them), each without async and with it (`acceptAsync`, ...), with a token that may come last. An address is one text, `[::1]:80` for v6; `bind` looks no name up and never waits. Four new variants of `IoError`: `ConnectionRefused`, `ConnectionReset`, `AddressInUse`, `HostNotFound`. A socket has no buffer and closes where it ends. `receive` and `send` are C#'s names for a socket: `read` is a word of the language. *Changed on 15 November 2026, before the sixty-sixth slice:* `send` goes through a `read` borrow, and a connection is read through a buffer by a reader of its own ("Lines over a connection") | Rust and Go write the address as one text; C# has both kinds of call. An async version beside each call is the rule of the files ("The names of async input and output") |
| A cancel ends a wait | *Decided on 15 November 2026, before the sixty-fifth slice.* A cancel ends a wait for a socket at once, and nothing that came is lost. A wait for a line of an input that can make the program wait (a pipe, a terminal) ends too: the read goes on on a thread, and what it reads stays in the reader's block for the next read | macOS cannot wait for a terminal with kqueue or `poll`, so the input is not waited for on the loop; a thread that reads on is what C# and Tokio do too |
| Lines over a connection | *Decided on 15 November 2026, before the sixty-sixth slice.* `conn.reader()` gives a `StreamReader` that borrows the connection and reads it through a block of its own: `readLine`, `lines()`, `readText`, `readBytes`, `readSome` and their async versions work on it, and a function that takes a `ref StreamReader` reads a file, the input and a connection alike. `send` goes through a `read` borrow, so the connection sends while the reader holds it; `receive` still needs a `ref`, so a `receive` while the reader is used again is a compile error, and bytes are never taken from under its block. Neither goes through a `shared` connection, which tasks on several threads may own. The reader lives no longer than the connection: it is kept in a variable and lent | Rust, Go and Zig read a socket through a separate buffered reader too; Rust's trick is `Write for &TcpStream`. No copy of the socket (`dup`), so the other side sees the end when the connection ends |
| The longest line over a connection | *Decided on 15 November 2026, before the sixty-sixth slice.* The reader of a connection gives no line, and no text, longer than 64 KB (`conn.reader(limit)` for another limit): a longer one is `IoError.LineTooLong`, and so is every read after it. A reader of a file or of the input has no limit | The other side of a connection could make the memory grow without end; Go's `bufio.Scanner` has the same 64 KB. Safe by default, as everywhere in SG |
| A read that gives what is there | *Decided on 15 November 2026, before the sixty-sixth slice.* `reader.readSome(ref buffer)` on every `StreamReader`: what the block holds, or what one read of the system gives when it is empty, at most what the buffer holds; 0 at the end. `readBytes` still fills the buffer unless the end comes first | Rust's `BufReader::read` and C#'s `Stream.Read`. A message whose length is not known is read without waiting for a full buffer |
| The loop of the network | *Decided on 15 November 2026, before the sixty-fifth slice.* In compiled code one thread waits for the sockets with epoll (Linux) or kqueue (macOS) and wakes the task through the pool (*changed on 16 November 2026, in the eighty-first slice:* a thread of the pool with nothing to do waits on the loop itself, one at a time, and runs the tasks it wakes; at most one idle thread at a time is woken to look for work, Tokio's rule; *changed on 16 November 2026, in the eighty-third slice:* a queue for each thread, from which the others steal); a socket is waited for once each time it is not ready (*changed on 16 November 2026, in the eighty-second slice:* a socket goes on the loop once and stays until it is closed; the loop writes what is ready into the socket's slot, where its tasks wait). In the interpreter the scheduler waits for the sockets with `poll` when no task can run | One runtime, carried only by a program that uses the network the async way |

### Calling C, the direction

*Decided on 15 November 2026, before the sixty-ninth slice.* Calling C comes in three slices: raw memory (`raw T`, `unsafe`,
`Memory`), then `extern` blocks and their calls, then `Disposable` on types of your own, opaque C types and C structs, with a
library wrapped in a safe type. The rows below were decided together; those of raw memory are built in the sixty-ninth slice, and
`extern` blocks, their calls, C text and a buffer for C in the seventieth; `Disposable` on types of your own, `Closable` on
them, opaque C types and structs by value in the seventy-first.

| Topic | Direction | Reason |
|---|---|---|
| Declaring C functions | `extern "z" { int compress(...) }`: a block per library, which is linked (`-lz`); `extern { }` is the C library itself. C names are written as C writes them, the one place the camelCase rule does not hold *Built on 15 November 2026, in the seventieth slice:* at the top level of a file only, one function on each line, with no body; the functions are private to their module unless `public` is written on one, and a C function declared in two modules is declared the same way in both | Rust's and Zig's form; there are no attributes for C#'s `[DllImport]` |
| Headers | No C header is included: the prototype is written from the SG declaration *Built on 15 November 2026, in the seventieth slice:* compiled code includes headers of its own for the runtime, so a C function is declared under a name of the runtime and bound to its C name with a label (`__asm__`), and clashes with no header | Nothing is looked for, and nothing clashes with the names of the runtime. A wrong declaration is not always caught by the C compiler, as in Rust |
| Safety | Every call of an `extern` function is written inside `unsafe`; there is no `safe` mark for now *Built on 15 November 2026, in the seventieth slice.* | The risky way is chosen on purpose. A safe type wraps a library, and its users see no `unsafe`. A `safe` mark (Rust 2024's) can come if the noise asks for it |
| What a signature holds | The integer types, `float`, `double` and `bool` as their C types; `raw T` as `T*`; `ref T` as `T*` and `read T` as `const T*`, for the time of the call; `read CString` as `const char*`. `string`, `List` and `T?` are not in a signature *Built on 15 November 2026, in the seventieth slice:* `ref T` and `read T` of plain data that is not an array (C takes the address of the first item, which `buffer.pointer()` gives); `char` is an error whose hint is `byte`; a struct by value comes with C structs *Built on 15 November 2026, in the seventy-first slice:* a struct of plain data by value, as an argument and as what C gives; `ref raw T` is C's `T**` | What C can take without a copy that nobody sees |
| Text | `CString.from(read text)` makes an owned text that ends in a zero byte, an error for a zero byte inside; `unsafe string.fromCString(p)` copies what C gives and checks that it is UTF-8 *Built on 15 November 2026, in the seventieth slice:* `CString.from` gives a `Result<CString, TextError>`, the `position` of the zero byte; `string.fromCString` takes a `raw byte` and gives a `Result<string, TextError>` | A `string` has no zero at its end, and is always UTF-8 |
| A buffer for C | `unsafe buffer.pointer()` gives a `raw byte` to a slice or an array; its count is passed apart *Changed on 15 November 2026, before the seventieth slice:* `buffer.pointer()` is written only as an argument of a C function (inside its `unsafe`), on a list, an array or a slice of plain data, and gives a `raw T`; in the call it counts as a `ref` of the buffer. The interpreter can then copy the items to C and back, and no pointer outlives the call; memory C keeps comes from `Memory.allocate` | C takes a pointer and a count |
| C in the interpreter | The interpreter writes a small C file for the `extern` functions a program uses, builds it as a shared library with `cc` and opens it with `dlopen`; no libffi *Built on 15 November 2026, in the seventieth slice:* in a folder of its own that only the user can open, made new for each run; and `Memory.allocate` and `Memory.free` are C's `calloc` and `free` in the interpreter too, so memory goes between SG and C both ways | No new dependency, and the interpreter and compiled code stay one language |
| C and tasks | A `raw T`, and a value that holds one, does not go to another task *(16 November 2026, the ninetieth slice, choice 842: unless it is of a type written `: unsafe Sendable`, its writer's promise)* | Whether a C library may be used from two threads is not known |
| C and async | A call of an `extern` function does not count as holding the thread ("Nothing holds the thread in async code"); a long one is wrapped in `await Task.run(...)` | As `print`; a rule would make most of C an error in async code |
| Later | Calls from C back into SG, `errno`, functions with a varying count of arguments (`printf`), `pkg-config`, Windows; `sg build -L path` for where libraries lie *(16 November 2026: `errno` came in the eighty-fourth slice, calls from C into SG in the eighty-fifth, a varying count of arguments in the eighty-sixth; `-L` and Windows are planned in "Windows, the direction", choices 941 and 942; `-L` came in the ninety-sixth)* | |
| Raw memory | A `raw T` points at plain data (numbers, `bool`, `char`, raw pointers, and arrays and structs of them), laid out as C lays it, or is a `raw void`, C's `void*`, which is cast before anything else is done with it. It may be `null` (no `raw T?`); `Memory.allocate<T>(n)` gives zeroed memory, a count below 0 or more than an address can count is a panic; `Memory.copy` copies items, also over itself | What C shares is plain data; owners in raw memory would need rules of their own |
| What `unsafe` is for | An item of raw memory (`p[i]`), `p + n`, a cast between raw pointers, `Memory.free`, `Memory.copy`, and since the seventieth slice `extern` calls and `string.fromCString` (since the eighty-seventh `Memory.view` too); making, copying and comparing a pointer, and `Memory.allocate`, are not. `unsafe { ... }` around statements, `unsafe` in front of a value, binding as `try` does. An `unsafe` that nothing needs, and one inside another, are errors | The word stays where the checker really stops following; there are no warnings |
| Checking raw memory | The interpreter checks each item against the block it came from: outside it, after `free`, through `null`, a block given back twice, are panics. Compiled code checks nothing; the tests run it under the sanitizers | Rust's Miri, built into the reference |
| `Disposable` on a type of your own | *Decided on 15 November 2026, before the seventy-first slice, and built in it.* `struct T : Disposable` has one `void dispose(ref this)`, on a struct (not an enum for now). It runs where the last owner of the value ends (the end of a block, a value written over, an item taken out of a list, the last `shared` owner), before the fields end; not on a panic or `exit`. `x.dispose()` is an error, and a method that takes `this` ends the value where it ends, so a type that frees something there keeps a field that says so. A `Disposable` type is not `Copyable` and not plain data, so it is not in raw memory or in a signature of C; it is in collections, `T?` and `shared` values, and goes to another task unless it holds a `raw T` | C#'s `IDisposable` without `using`, Rust's `Drop`. A `dispose` that nobody calls cannot be called twice |
| `Closable` on a type of your own | *Decided on 15 November 2026, before the seventy-first slice, and built in it.* `: Closable` asks for a `close(this)` that gives a `Result`, and a local of the type is closed before it ends, as a `StreamWriter` is; the hint is `try x.close()`. The type's own methods are exempt for `this` | The error of the last step (a stream's last bytes, a flush) is not lost; `dispose` cannot give it |
| Compiled `dispose` | *Decided on 15 November 2026, before the seventy-first slice:* `dispose` is a C function; a move known when the program is compiled costs nothing, one that depends on a condition keeps a flag on its local. *Built differently in the slice, and approved on 15 November 2026 (choice 538):* a `Disposable` struct has one byte more, set where the value is moved out, and its drop looks at it, as a moved text leaves a `NULL` and a moved enum a tag that is no variant | The back end already empties every place a value is moved out of and tests it where it is dropped; the byte is that rule for a struct with nothing else to empty |
| A type of C seen only behind a pointer | *Decided on 15 November 2026, before the seventy-first slice, and built in it.* `extern "sqlite3" { struct sqlite3 }`: a struct without a body in an `extern` block, written only as `raw sqlite3` (and `ref raw sqlite3` for C's `sqlite3**`); an item of it, `p + n`, `Memory.allocate<sqlite3>`, a field and a value of it are errors. Private to the module unless `public`; the same name in two modules is two types | What C does not show, SG does not guess; one pointer type cannot be handed to a function that takes another |
| Structs of C | *Decided on 15 November 2026, before the seventy-first slice, and built in it.* A struct of plain data is laid out as C lays it out, with no mark; its fields have names of SG, and only their order and types count. It goes to C by value and comes back by value. Unions, bit fields, `packed` and alignment come later; an enum of C is an `int`, a pointer to a function a `raw void` *(16 November, the eighty-fifth slice: a pointer to a function is a `CFunc<...>`)* | Rust's `#[repr(C)]` is what every plain struct already is |
| A variable nothing reads | *Decided on 15 November 2026, before the seventy-sixth slice, and built in it.* A local variable whose value nothing reads is a compile error: one never used, and one only written. Writing the variable, `x += 1`, and a write to one of its fields or items are not reads, unless the write goes through a borrow, a `shared` value or raw memory; every other use is one, a method called on it and `ref x` given to a call among them. A parameter is not looked at, nor a variable of a `Disposable` type or of a struct that holds one. The name of a `for` and of a pattern are held to it too, with `_` for one not needed. A value written over before it is read, or given after the last read, is not looked at yet | Go makes an unused variable an error, Rust warns; a write to a copy (`var p = rect.corner; p.x = 0`) is the mistake gcc warned on in the C, and the hint is the borrow (`var p = ref rect.corner`). No cost at run time: the checker alone |

Once threads and async are built, a program in SG is promised at compile time: no data race (Rust promises this too); no
deadlock from the order of locks, and none from locks and waits, as long as no channels are used; a wait under a lock (`lock
... when`) for a value no other task owns is always caught, and waits under locks that wait for each other are caught in the
interpreter (in compiled code with the hardening before the preview), as are tasks that wait on channels for each other; a `for`
over a channel that a sender of the same function would keep from ending is a compile error; no `await` while a lock is held; no task cancelled without
being told; no task forgotten or left running, and no error of one lost; no deadlock from waiting for async code the wrong
way and no pool starved by a call that blocks, which C# and Rust both allow; and one runtime. A logic race (a value read in one
`lock` block and written in another, changed by another task in between) is not caught; a two-step change is written in one
block. These are promises of the design until they are built and tested; the README says so only then.

### Reaching what C reaches, the direction

*Decided on 16 November 2026, after the eighty-third slice.* No preview of the language comes out while there is work
that C can do and SG cannot. The rows below were decided together (choices 703-718); each slice decides the details of
its own part before it is written. *Reached on 17 November 2026, with the ninety-fourth slice: each of the seven
programs of the threshold runs and is tested. The slices came out one later than planned (the ninety-second became a
rule of names, "A name that hides another"), so the kernel is the ninety-fourth. Windows is talked through next.*

| Topic | Direction | Reason |
|---|---|---|
| The threshold | It is reached when each of these runs and is tested: a program that keeps its data in sqlite; a client that fetches a page over HTTPS with libcurl; a server that ends cleanly on Ctrl-C; a library written in SG and called from C; a small kernel that boots in QEMU, writes to its serial port and takes a timer interrupt; a queue without locks, built on atomics; a reader of a binary header with a union and bit fields | A list of programs can be checked; "everything C can do" cannot |
| The order | 84: a C file beside the program, the variables of C, `errno`, a pointer as an integer and back, the difference and the order of two pointers. 85: functions of SG that C calls (`export`), pointers to functions of C both ways (`CFunc`), the state a callback reaches. 86: calls of C with a varying count of arguments, and sqlite wrapped. 87: libcurl wrapped. 88: signals. 89: unions, bit fields, `packed` and alignment. 90: atomics and `volatile`. 91: a library built from SG. 92: building without an operating system. 93: the kernel in QEMU *(17 November 2026: the ninety-seventh slice written, 980 to 998. Decided the same day: the tests on Windows are run whole when a release is near, not after every slice, since a run on the machine of Windows takes most of an hour; the CI still runs on every push and nobody waits for it, what it finds being made right before the release; while a slice of Windows is written, a few programs are tried there)* | The C file comes first: it opens macros, inline functions, assembly and intrinsics at once. sqlite needs callbacks, libcurl a varying count of arguments |
| A C file beside the program | `extern "./native.c" { ... }` names a C file of the module's folder instead of a library; it is compiled into the same unit as the program, so a `static inline` function of it is inlined and costs nothing. The interpreter builds it into the library of its wrappers | One door for what a header gives and a library does not: macros, inline functions, assembly, intrinsics, `setjmp`. Rust takes it through the `cc` crate and a second build script |
| The variables of C | `extern { int opterr }`: a variable of C, read and written only inside `unsafe`. SG has no variable of its own that is assigned freely; what lives for the whole program is a `static` ("Globals, as decided") | Some C libraries keep their state in variables; a kernel's interrupt keeps its state where the handler finds it |
| `errno` | Read right after the call of C it belongs to; the interpreter keeps it as each call of C ends, so that its own work does not change it; an `IoError` is made from its number | Rust's `last_os_error`; a C library answers most failures this way |
| Functions of SG that C calls | `export int compareInts(raw void a, raw void b) { ... }`: a signature of C types only, the name C sees as written. A panic inside ends the process with its message; nothing unwinds through C | Callbacks, and the functions of a library built from SG, are the same thing |
| Pointers to functions of C | A type of its own, `CFunc<raw void, raw void, int>`, apart from `Func`; the name of an `export` function is a value of it, and one that comes from C (`dlsym`, a field of a struct) is called only inside `unsafe`. A `Func` does not go to C | A pointer from C may be wrong, and a `Func` may take types C does not know |
| The state a callback reaches | There are no lambdas and no globals that are assigned freely (a `static` is read only, atomic or locked), so the state of one call goes through C's `void* userdata`: `unsafe Memory.addressOf(ref state)` gives a `raw void`, and `unsafe (ref State)data` lends it again inside the callback. That the value lives long enough is the program's promise; the interpreter keeps the addresses it gave in a table and panics on a wrong type or a value that has ended | Rust's way; the interpreter checks it as it checks raw memory |
| Signals | Not handlers written by the program: `Signals.listen(...)` and a wait for the next one (`next()`, `nextAsync(token)`), as in Go and Tokio; while a listener lives, those signals do not end the program. A handler of C is still possible inside `unsafe`, through `export` and `CFunc`. Built in the eighty-eighth slice ("Signals, as built") | A handler runs between any two steps of any thread; almost nothing is safe inside it |
| A varying count of arguments | `int curl_easy_setopt(raw CURL handle, int option, ...)`: what comes after is of C types, a number constant an `int` unless cast *(16 November, before the eighty-sixth slice, choice 752: a number written without a type is an error there, so the cast is always written)* | `printf`, `ioctl`, `fcntl` and libcurl's options need it |
| A pointer as a number | `(nuint)p`, `(raw void)n`, `p - q` (a count of items) and `p < q`; none of them reads memory | `SQLITE_TRANSIENT` is `(void*)-1`; allocators and kernels compute addresses |
| The layout of C | Unions, bit fields, `packed` and alignment; a field of a union is read inside `unsafe`. The notation is decided in their slice *(16 November 2026, after the eighty-seventh slice: two more will be done, their slice not chosen yet. A `raw T` that is not aligned for `T` is a panic where it is read or written, in the interpreter and in checked compiled code; and a window that writes, a `ref T[]` over raw memory beside `Memory.view`, for a buffer C wants filled)*. Built in the eighty-ninth slice, with both ("The layout of C, as built") | C structs as C lays them out, binary formats, the registers of devices |
| Three systems | SG runs on Linux, macOS and Windows. Linux and macOS come first and Windows later, but nothing is built in a way Windows cannot take: what the language says holds on all three, what the runtime asks of the system goes through a function of its own, and the C the back end writes is read by the compilers of all three (on Windows Clang, since the C uses statement expressions). The parts already built on what only Linux and macOS have (the loop of the network on `epoll` and `kqueue`, threads and locks of `pthread`, signals, starting a program, `dlopen` in the interpreter, blocks aligned beyond 16 bytes given back with `free`) are looked over before the slice of Windows. *Decided on 16 November 2026, during the eighty-ninth slice* *(17 November 2026, the ninety-first slice: so is a library on Windows, a `.dll` with its `.lib`, pinned so that it is not unloaded, choice 874)* *(17 November 2026: looked over, "Windows, the direction"; the import library of a `.dll` is `x.dll.lib`, choice 929)* | Three systems are where programs run; going back later is dearer than keeping the way open |
| Atomics and `volatile` | Built-in atomic types, sequentially consistent by default, with forms that take an order; `volatile` as calls (`unsafe Memory.readVolatile(p)`, `writeVolatile(p, v)`), not as a qualifier of a type *(16 November 2026, before the ninetieth slice, choice 843: the call takes a place in raw memory, `Memory.readVolatile(p[0].status)`, so that a field of a block of registers is reached)*. Built in the ninetieth slice ("Atomics and `volatile`, as built") | Queues without locks; the registers of devices. Rust's form for `volatile` |
| A library built from SG | `sg build --library` gives a `.a` or a `.so` and a header made from the `export` functions *(built in the ninety-first slice: "A library built from SG, as built")* | C calls SG |
| Without an operating system | `sg build --freestanding` with a cross compiler, compiled code only: what needs the system (`print`, `File`, tasks) is a compile error with a hint, `string` and `List` work when the program gives an allocator, a panic goes to a function of the program, and `main` is not needed *(built in the ninety-third slice: "Without an operating system, as built")* | Kernels, boot loaders, small devices |
| The kernel shown | A RISC-V kernel on QEMU's `virt` machine *(built in the ninety-fourth slice: "The kernel shown, as built")* | Its start is the simplest, and its serial port and timer are plain |
| Windows | Not part of the threshold; talked through once the threshold is reached *(17 November 2026: talked through, "Windows, the direction")* | A platform of its own: IOCP, its processes, its paths |

### Windows, the direction

*Decided on 17 November 2026, after the threshold was reached (choices 928 to 942).* SG runs on Linux, macOS and
Windows ("Three systems"); the rows below say how Windows is reached. Each slice decides the details of its own part
before it is written, as before.

| Topic | Direction | Reason |
|---|---|---|
| Testing on Windows | GitHub's machines with Windows run `cargo test` on x86-64 and on 64-bit Arm for every push to the branch of Windows, with GitHub's own `actions/checkout` pinned to its commit as the only action and a token that only reads. A virtual machine with Windows on 64-bit Arm is where a slice is tried while it is written. Linux and macOS are still tested where the code is written. No Wine (928) *(17 November 2026, the ninety-seventh slice: built. `.github/workflows/windows.yml`; the repository allows only GitHub's own actions, pinned to their commits; 996)* *(17 November 2026, the hundred-and-second slice: with the last slice of Windows merged, the CI runs on every push to `main`; it installs QEMU, at a fixed version whose installer is checked against its SHA-256, for the kernel; 1072, 1078)* | Wine is not Windows; the machines of a public repository cost nothing |
| The machines and the compiler | `x86_64-pc-windows-msvc` and `aarch64-pc-windows-msvc`; Windows 10 1809, Windows Server 2019 and later, 64-bit only. Clang, with its own driver, not `clang-cl`, and LLVM's `lld-link`; of Microsoft's tools only the libraries and the SDK. C's library is linked into the program (`/MT`), so a program needs nothing installed; each DLL then has a heap of its own, and memory goes back through a function of the one that gave it. The compiler is `CC`, then `clang` on `PATH`, then `C:\Program Files\LLVM\bin\clang.exe`, with a hint to install LLVM and the Build Tools when none is there. A program is `x.exe`; `--library` gives `x.lib`, `--shared` gives `x.dll` with `x.dll.lib`; a debug build writes `x.pdb`. Building for Windows from macOS or Linux comes later (929) *(17 November 2026, the ninety-seventh slice: built for programs and tests, `--library` and `--shared` waiting for C's slice. `-fuse-ld=lld -fms-runtime-lib=static`, the manifest written beside the C and embedded by LLVM's linker, `/STACK:8388608`, `/DEPENDENTLOADFLAG:0x1000`, `x.exe` when `-o` names no extension; 983)* *(17 November 2026, the hundred-and-first slice: `--library` and `--shared` built, `x.lib` made by `llvm-lib`; the libraries of the system and `legacy_stdio_definitions`, where Microsoft keeps `printf` and its kin as functions, named by the C itself; `sg build` copies beside a program the DLLs it linked from the folders of `-L`, and those they need, since Windows has no `rpath`; 1054, 1057, 1062)* *(17 November 2026, the hundred-and-second slice: a debug build, which is not optimised, asked the C compiler's library for a division of 128 bits, which Windows does not link; the runtime divides in 64 bits there, and a test holds every program to it on every system; 1070)* | The libraries a program links on Windows are built for Microsoft's ABI; Rust's names for the files, since a static library and an import library would both be `x.lib` |
| C's `long` | `CLong` and `CULong` are as large as C's `long` and `unsigned long` on the system: 64 bits on Linux and macOS, 32 on Windows. They are types of their own, not other names of `int` or `long`: a value crosses with a cast (`(CLong)n`, `(long)r`), there is no arithmetic on them (the hint is `(long)r + 1`), `==`, `<` and printing work, and a number written without a type goes into one only if it fits in 32 bits, on every system. In an `extern` block they are written where C has `long`; `long` there stays C's `int64_t`. The examples and tests that wrote `long` for C's `long` are put right (930) *(17 November 2026, the ninety-fifth slice: built. A value crosses on its own where that holds on every system, by the rule of mixing integers: an `int` becomes a `CLong`, a `CLong` a `long`; the other way is a cast that checks this system's size. No range, method, `atomic` or bit field of one either, and a `match` on one is looked at as 64 bits. curl and zlib take them, zlib's `uLong` everywhere and the size of its stream from `Memory.sizeOf`; 943 to 949)* | Rust's `c_long` is another name of `i64` or `i32`, so code that compiles on Linux does not on Windows; and the same sum overflows on one system and not on the other |
| Text at the edge of the system | Windows is called through its functions of UTF-16, the text turned at the edge. A name, an argument or a variable that is not valid UTF-16 is the error one that is not UTF-8 is elsewhere, its position counted in bytes of UTF-8. To a console the text goes through `WriteConsoleW`, a character split between two writes kept until it is whole and a byte that is not UTF-8 shown as `�`, as a terminal shows it; the code page of the console is not changed. From a console the input is read with `ReadConsoleW`, Ctrl-Z at the start of a line ending it. Files are binary: `writeLine` writes `\n` on every system. Every `.exe` and `.dll` carries a manifest: UTF-8 as the code page of C's functions (from Windows 10 1903), long paths, Windows 10 and 11. Names of variables are matched without case, as Windows matches them. A text of UTF-16 for calling Win32 from SG comes later (931) *(17 November 2026, the ninety-seventh slice: built for the output, the standard error, the console's input that waits, the command line and the variables. C's `printf` and its kin reach the console through the runtime; a name that is not UTF-16 becomes WTF-8, which is not UTF-8; 984 to 986)* | A program behaves the same on the three systems, and a library of C linked into it opens a path of UTF-8 |
| Paths and files | On Windows `/` and `\` both separate and `Path.join` writes `\`; `C:\`, `C:`, `\\server\share\`, `\\?\` and `\\.\` are roots, and a root at the start of the second part of `join` is dropped, as `/` is. `Path.joinInside(folder, name)` gives an `InvalidInput` for a name with `..`, a root, a drive, a `:`, the name of a device (`CON`, `NUL`, `COM1`) or a dot or a space at its end, on every system the same, so that a name from outside stays inside the folder. A long path goes to Windows as `\\?\` without the program seeing it. A file is opened so that others may read, write, rename and delete it, and a deletion takes the name away at once (POSIX semantics, from Windows 10 1809), as on Linux and macOS. `File.move` never takes the place of a file, `deleteAll` follows no link or junction and works from the handle of each folder, `createTemp` makes a folder only its owner may enter. `Directory.list` gives names in the order of their bytes of UTF-8. On Windows the code of `Other(code)` is Win32's (932) *(17 November 2026, the ninety-fifth slice: `Path.joinInside` built on Linux and macOS, a name of several parts taken, a `\` refused on every system, `a/../b` refused too; `Path.join` keeps a `..` as it is written. A link inside the folder is still followed; opening by the handle of the folder comes later; 950 to 955)* *(17 November 2026, the ninety-seventh slice: built on Windows. A path goes as `\\?\` from 248 characters, as in Rust; `join` keeps a separator the first part ends with; `deleteAll` holds the handle of every folder it is in, so none can be swapped for a link; a folder opened as a file is `IsDirectory` and a file on the way `NotDirectory`, as on Linux; 987; The hundred-and-forty-second slice (choice 1594): `joinInside` refuses C1 control characters too; `\\?\UNC\` is read byte by byte, so a character of several bytes there is no panic.)* | A name that is safe on one system must not lead out of the folder on another |
| Signals | `Interrupt` is Ctrl-C and Ctrl-Break. `Terminate` is the console closed, the user logged off and the system shut down; while a listener lives, Windows is held until the program ends, within the few seconds Windows gives. `Hangup`, `Quit`, `User1`, `User2` and `WindowChange` never come on Windows, and listening for them is no error. `Signals.raise` works there too, with the default of a signal when nothing listens. `kill`, `terminate` and `signal` end another program with `TerminateProcess` and the code 1, since Windows has no polite stop to send (`signal(Signal.WindowChange)` does nothing). Services of Windows come later (933) *(17 November 2026, the hundredth slice: built. The handler of the console hands the signal on itself, on the thread Windows gives it, and does not return from a `Terminate`; a raised signal with no listener ends the program as Ctrl-C does (`0xC000013A`). The command line is read by the runtime, not by `CommandLineToArgvW`, whose `shell32` brings `user32`, and a console program with `user32` is not told of logging off or shutting down. A listener of `Interrupt` turns off the ignoring of Ctrl-C a program was started with, as a listener on Linux and macOS takes the place of an ignored signal, and it is not turned back on, since Windows does not tell whether it was; 1042 to 1046)* | Go's mapping; a program written once is right on both |
| Other programs | `CreateProcessW`, the command line quoted by the rules C's library reads it with. A program named without `/` or `\` is looked for in the absolute folders of `PATH`, with `.exe` added when it has no extension, and never in the folder the program works in. A `.bat` or `.cmd` runs only when named with its extension, its arguments escaped for `cmd.exe` as Rust does since CVE-2024-24576, and one that cannot be escaped is an `InvalidInput`. The child inherits only its three streams; names of variables have no case. `ExitStatus.Code(int)` on every system, a code with its highest bit set printed in hex (`exited with code 0xC0000005`). A child does not end with its parent, as on Linux and macOS; job objects come later (934) *(17 November 2026, the ninety-fifth slice: `ExitStatus.Code(int)` built; `exit(code)` keeps a `byte`, the code every system keeps whole; 956, 957)* *(17 November 2026, the hundredth slice: built. A folder of `PATH` in quotes is taken whole; the program is named by its handle until it is waited for; the pipes are named pipes, overlapped on our end, and a cancelled read is cancelled with `CancelIoEx` instead of `CancelSynchronousIo`, so the call never reads on and nothing is lost; a cleared environment keeps `SystemRoot`, as Go keeps it; `waitAsync` waits on the pool of threads of the system; the tests start `sg-test-tool` in place of `sh` and its kin on every system; 1032 to 1047)* | `CreateProcess` looks in the working folder first, where a planted program would run |
| The runtime | Threads of `_beginthreadex`, `SRWLOCK` and `CONDITION_VARIABLE`, behind functions of the runtime; the main thread has 8 MB of stack, as on Linux and macOS. The loop of the network polls through `\Device\Afd` on an I/O completion port, as mio and Tokio do, so the scheduler of the eighty-first to eighty-third slices stays as it is; a listener takes `SO_EXCLUSIVEADDRUSE` and never `SO_REUSEADDR`, and a socket is not inherited. Pipes and files are read and written the async way on the threads of input and output. Blocks aligned beyond 16 bytes come from `_aligned_malloc`; `Instant` from `QueryPerformanceCounter`, `DateTime.now` from `GetSystemTimePreciseAsFileTime`, `Task.delay` from a timer of high resolution, `SecureRandom` from `ProcessPrng` (935 to 937) *(17 November 2026, the ninety-seventh slice: blocks aligned beyond 16 bytes, the clocks and `ProcessPrng` built; a block of `_aligned_malloc` goes back through `_aligned_free` everywhere the back end gives one back; 988, 989)* *(17 November 2026, the ninety-eighth slice: threads, locks, conditions, the pool and the clock of delays built; every lock, condition and thread of the runtime goes through one layer, pthread on Linux and macOS, so no name of pthread is taken on Windows; the pool counts the cores of every group; the clock sleeps on the timer of high resolution and an event, and fifty delays of 1 ms take about 97 ms there; a write to the console holds its stream's lock; 999 to 1013)* *(17 November 2026, the ninety-ninth slice: the loop of the network built on AFD and a completion port, a question to the driver asked once and again while tasks wait; a listener and a datagram socket hold their port alone, and a datagram that came back is told as on Linux and macOS, only to a connected socket; names looked up in UTF-16; files and the input stay on the threads of input and output, and a cancelled wait for the input leaves its thread reading; Windows tells of a refused connection after about two seconds; 1015 to 1030)* | A recursion that runs on Linux runs on Windows; on Windows `SO_REUSEADDR` lets another program take the port; a delay of a millisecond does not take sixteen |
| Tests | `sg test --compiled` starts itself for each test (`--only N`) on every system, and `fork` goes. A debug or test build counts the blocks its runtime gives and takes back and reports a leak as the program ends, on every system; LSan still runs on Linux and macOS. A check a system does not have (TSan on Windows) is said in the output, not passed over (938, 939) *(17 November 2026, the ninety-sixth slice: built on Linux and macOS. The program starts itself again through `posix_spawn`, from the path the system gives; the count is of the blocks of values, raw memory and what the runtime keeps for the process left out, looked at where the `shared` values are, and one below zero is reported too; 972 to 978)* *(17 November 2026, the ninety-seventh slice: on Windows the program of tests starts itself with `CreateProcessW`; the address and undefined-behaviour sanitizers are tried and a missing one is said, leaks are the count of blocks, and the line says the thread sanitizer is not there; 991, 993)* *(17 November 2026, the ninety-eighth slice: a program on several threads runs on Windows under the sanitizers the machine has, and the line says how many ran without the thread sanitizer; 1011)* | One path for the three systems; a check never passes in silence |
| The interpreter and C | The interpreter runs on Windows with declarations of its own of Windows' functions, with no crate. Its wrappers are built into a DLL. A DLL is looked for in the program's folder and in `System32`, never in the working folder or on `PATH`. `C.lastError()` gives the system's last error (`GetLastError` on Windows, `errno` elsewhere) and `IoError.fromCode` takes it; `IoError.fromErrno` takes `C.errno()`, the number of C's library, which on Windows is not Win32's (940) *(17 November 2026, the ninety-fifth slice: `C.lastError()`, `C.setLastError(n)` and `IoError.fromErrno` built, `errno` on Linux and macOS; `IoError.fromCode(C.errno())` and `IoError.fromErrno(C.lastError())` are errors with the right one as the hint; 958 to 960)* *(17 November 2026, the hundred-and-first slice: built. The wrappers are a DLL with C's library as a DLL (`/MD`), the one the interpreter is linked with, so memory and `errno` are one with it; "the program's folder" is the folder of its first file, and the folders of `-L` are looked in too; the folder of the DLL is removed by the next interpreter once its process has ended; `GetLastError` is kept by every call of C beside `errno`; the functions of C in the branch of another system of an `if` on `OperatingSystem` are not linked; the interpreter carries the manifest of compiled programs; 1050 to 1052, 1058, 1059, 1063)* | A DLL put where a program is started would be loaded otherwise |
| Libraries | `sg build`, `run` and `test` take `-L folder`, and the interpreter looks for DLLs there too. `extern "z", "zlib" { }` names a library by the names it has on the systems; the first one found is linked, and the error names the ones tried and where. On the CI of Windows the libraries of the examples come from vcpkg (941), whose zlib is `libz.lib` since 1.4, so zlib is named `"z", "zlib", "libz"` (1258) *(17 November 2026, the ninety-sixth slice: built on Linux and macOS. A folder of `-L` is also written into the program as its `rpath`, and one others may write into is an error; whether a name is there is asked of the C compiler by linking an empty program with it, only for a block of several names and once a link has failed; 963 to 971)* *(17 November 2026, the hundred-and-first slice: built on Windows. The libraries come from vcpkg as DLLs with their import libraries, not built into the program, which would bring C's library of `/MT` into the interpreter's DLL and leave libcurl's libraries of the system to be named by hand; a folder of `-L` may hold a `:` and is not checked for others writing into it, since nothing of it is written into the program; `extern "m"` and `"c"` link nothing there; 1053 to 1056, 1064)* | zlib is `-lz` on Unix and `zlib.lib` with vcpkg, and Windows has no `pkg-config` |
| The order | 95: `CLong`, `Path.joinInside`, `ExitStatus.Code(int)`, `C.lastError` and `IoError.fromErrno`. 96: `-L`, an `extern` of several names, tests without `fork`, the count of blocks. Both on Linux and macOS. Then Windows, on a branch of its own merged after each slice when the CI is green: 97 the base (the CI, the compiler, the interpreter built, memory, printing, panics, files and paths, the clock, random numbers, the environment); 98 threads, tasks, locks, atomics and the async scheduler; 99 the network and async input and output; 100 other programs, signals and the console's input; 101 C (the interpreter's calls of C, DLLs, a library of SG as a DLL, the examples with vcpkg); 102 everything tested on both machines, measured against C, and the documents. A test of what Windows does not run yet is listed with its reason in `tests/windows-pending.txt`, which shrinks with each slice and is empty after the last; a preview of the language comes after (942) *(17 November 2026, the hundred-and-second slice: done. Everything is tested on Windows, the program without an operating system and the kernel in QEMU included, and the list is gone; the programs of `bench` are measured against C there too; 1068 to 1079)* | What does not need Windows is done first, where it is tested today |

## Open questions

- Generics: closed on 12 November 2026 as "deferred", see the section above.

- Notation. The [C# variant](02-syntax-draft.md) is the current draft, and it will be stressed with example programs.
- Packages: *20 November 2026 (choices 1551, 1552):* a package is the URL of a repository and a version tag,
  pinned by the SHA-256 of its contents, with versions chosen by minimal version selection and no registry,
  as Zig's and Go's; fetched by the system's `git` (1558 in place of `git`, `curl` and `tar`). XML goes into a package of the
  language's own, not into `std` (1551 replaces 1413). The choices beneath it were made on 20 November 2026
  (1555-1564, rows "Packages" and "Packages, as built" above; the library boundary is the folder `internal`,
  1561); fetching, the lock, the cache and the commands were built in the hundred-and-thirty-eighth slice
  (1571-1588), and made safer after the second review of safety in the hundred-and-thirty-ninth (1591).
- Stack overflow on deep structures: freeing a long linked list is recursive. For types that contain themselves the compiler could turn freeing into a loop,
  and the overflow could be a readable panic. Not tested.
  *19 November 2026:* a list of one link per node is freed in a loop in compiled code (README 13). The interpreter panics with *stack overflow* when
  writing or comparing a value nested too deep for its stack (README 1343). Compiled code still ends at the guard page with no message, as deep
  recursion does; that is the open stack depth question.
- Inside `Func<read T, U>`, `U` cannot depend on the argument, so `orders.map(o => read o.customer)` does not compile and `.clone()` is needed. Whether this has to be overcome has not been tested.
- Type-first grammar without semicolons: the distinction between `Node* n` and `a * b`, and between `Node? x` and `a ? b : c`,
  needs lookahead. The end-of-line rule must also be settled at the interpreter stage. A `T { ... }` initialiser cannot be told apart from a block
  inside a condition (`if x == Node { ... } {`); parentheses could be required there, as in Rust.
  *12 November 2026:* the end-of-line rule, the place of `{`/`else` and the statement rule were approved and are in the decisions table. The `Node* n` and `Node? x`
  distinction was resolved by the statement rule. `T { ... }` inside a condition was also decided the same day ("Struct construction in a condition").
  This item is closed.
- The language the compiler is written in. Today the checker, the interpreter and the C back end are written in Rust, as the
  [roadmap](03-roadmap.md) says, which counts writing the language in itself among the traps. A note to come back to, not a
  goal: after the showcase program, look again at whether the compiler should be written in SG. It would be the largest program
  in the language and show the claim on itself; since SG compiles to C, the C it writes for its own compiler could be kept in
  the repository, so building from nothing needs only a C compiler. Against it while the language still changes every few
  days: each change would break the compiler's own code. Rust's compiler moved from OCaml to Rust, Go's from C to Go, C#'s
  from C++ to C#, Zig's from C++ to Zig; Swift's is still mostly C++, and TypeScript's is moving from TypeScript to Go for
  speed. Noted on 13 November 2026.
- A read that gives what is there. `reader.readBytes(ref buffer)` fills the buffer unless the end comes first (row "Bytes piece
  by piece"). For a file that costs nothing, but on a pipe, a terminal or, later, the network the call waits until the buffer is
  full: a server could not answer a message of 100 bytes while it waits for 4 KB. When the network comes, a second read that
  gives what is ready (Rust's `read`, Go's `Read`, with a short count that is not the end) may be needed beside it; whether it
  is, and its name, are to be decided then. Noted on 14 November 2026. *For the network, settled on 15 November 2026:*
  `stream.receive(ref buffer)` gives what has come ("The network, as written"); for a pipe or a terminal it is still open.
  *Settled for every reader on 15 November 2026, before the sixty-sixth slice:* `reader.readSome(ref buffer)` ("A read that
  gives what is there").
- A variable given values that are never read: `var p = rect.corner` followed by `p.x = 0` and nothing more writes to a copy
  (a `Copyable` struct is copied), which may well be a mistake: the writer meant the corner of the rectangle. gcc warns on
  it in the C; SG allows it for now, and compiled code tells the C compiler the variable is used. Whether it becomes a
  compile error with a hint (`ref` to change the original, `_ = ` to show it is meant), as Go makes an unused variable
  one, is to be decided in a slice of its own. Noted on 15 November 2026, in the seventy-fifth slice. *Decided on 15 November
  2026, before the seventy-sixth slice:* a compile error ("A variable nothing reads"). A value written over before it is
  read, or given after the last read, is not looked at yet; whether it should be is still open.

## Performance note

Notation does not affect the machine code: `read`, `ref`, `from` and type names disappear at compile time. Decisions about meaning do.
None of this has been measured, and what follows are expectations.

| Effect | Decision | Compared to Rust |
|---|---|---|
| Same | `T*`, `read`, `ref`, `T?`, `enum`, `match`, `Result`, `try`, monomorphization, bounds checks, `locked` | The same rules, the same optimisation information |
| Better | Panic does not unwind | Like Rust's `panic=abort` setting: no cleanup path is generated, and the binary gets smaller |
| Same | Overflow is checked in debug and wraps in release | Changed on 12 November from "checked in release too". One difference in the C that is generated: a wrapping operation is written with unsigned arithmetic, so the C compiler cannot assume "this never overflows" as it does for C's own `int` |
| Worse | `shared` is always atomic | More expensive than `Rc`, only on `share` and when an owner lets go |

This goal holds only with a back end at the level of LLVM. A tree-walking interpreter is for testing the language and gives no performance.

*First measurement, 12 November 2026.* The first slice of a back end writes C and lets the C compiler optimise
(integers, functions, structs, `read`/`ref`). Against the same programs written by hand in C, with the same `-O2`, and
with overflow checked in release as the design then said: trial division 0.97 times the time of C, recursive Fibonacci
1.38, a Collatz loop 1.59. The check cost nothing where arithmetic is rare and up to about 60% in a loop that is nothing
but arithmetic. On this measurement the overflow rule was changed to Rust's (checked in debug, wrapping in release); after the
change a release build runs the three programs in 0.96, 0.98 and 1.00 times the time of C
([interpreter/README.md](../interpreter/README.md)). The first program with memory (text read byte by byte, compared, copied to the heap and freed) runs in 1.05 times the
time of C: that is the cost of the checks that stay, on every index and every range. The first program that follows
pointers (a linked list of a million nodes built with `new`, walked and freed) runs in 1.01 times the time of C: a
`Node*?` is one pointer and `is not null` one comparison. The first program with an enum (an expression tree
built, evaluated with `match`, rewritten and freed) runs in 1.02 times the time of C written as a tagged union and a
`switch`. The first program with errors as values (numbers read out of text, an error found three calls deep and
passed up with `try`) runs in the time of C that returns error codes (0.99 to 1.02): a `Result` is a tag and a value,
and `try` is one test. The first program with a list (a million numbers added, changed through their index, added
up and freed) runs in 1.02 times the time of C that keeps a growing array by hand. One thing it showed about the
overflow rule: in a loop that is nothing but arithmetic on its counter, wrapping can cost what C's undefined signed
overflow saves (1.28 on such a loop, until the arithmetic was taken out of the measurement). Rust pays the same.
The first program with a hash table (a million numbers counted under fifty thousand keys) runs in 1.20 times the time
of C; all of it is the keyed hash (SipHash-1-3, as in Rust), and with the plain hash of the C program the two take the
same time. Printing two million structs first took 1.22 times the time of C's `printf`, because each printed value was
allocated; written into one block that the program keeps, it takes 0.57 times (a `printf` reads its format on every call).

## Next steps

1. Reach 10-15 example programs in total, and compare the notation across 2-3 variants. Written so far: calculator, stack, threads, `List`, log
   summary, folder tree, order rules, cities and roads (graph), stock and invoice (modules, `use`), HTTP request (byte slice), sensor window (fixed-size array), stock tests (`test`, `assert`; approved on 12 November). The next example has not been chosen.
2. A tree-walking interpreter in Rust, along the path of Crafting Interpreters. Details:
   [roadmap](03-roadmap.md). Started on 12 November, the code is in the `interpreter/` folder. The first slice works: functions,
   `int`/`long`/`nint`/`bool`/`string`, `if`/`while`/`for`, `print`, `assert`, `$"..."`, overflow and conversion panics.
   Second slice (the same day): structs, `T*` and `new`, `T?` and `null` (`is null`, `??`, flow narrowing), `read`/`ref`,
   static type checking and the check for moved variables; the linked list in the syntax draft runs unchanged.
   Third slice (the same day): methods (`this`, `read this`, `ref this`), calls through the type and `T(args)`,
   `take`/`replace`, moving a field out of an owned value (tracked per field); the stack example runs. The six small choices made in this slice were approved on 12 November 2026 and are in the decisions table:
   [interpreter/README.md](../interpreter/README.md), "Rules from the third slice".
   Fourth slice (the same day): `enum`, `match` (expression and statement, `when`, `_`, `var other`, the check that every
   variant is handled, `return`/`break`/`continue` from an arm) and `is` with a variant; an expression tree with `Expr*` runs.
   The six small choices made in this slice were approved on 12 November 2026 and are in the decisions table:
   [interpreter/README.md](../interpreter/README.md), "Rules from the fourth slice".
   Fifth slice (the same day): the built-in `Result<T, E>`, `Ok`/`Error`, `try` (on a `Result` and on a `T?`, with
   error conversion), `??` on a `Result`, `?? return`, the unused `Result` error, `_ = f()`, `Result<void, E> main()`,
   `tryAdd` and its siblings; the calculator runs from the tokens on (`examples/calculator.sg`), and `pop` in the stack
   example is now written with `try` as in the design. The seven small choices made in this slice were approved on 12 November 2026 and are in the decisions table:
   [interpreter/README.md](../interpreter/README.md), "Rules from the fifth slice".
   Sixth slice (the same day): `byte`, character literals, `text[i]`, `text[a..b]` and a `match` on a number; the
   calculator now runs whole, with its lexer reading real text. The choices made in this slice were approved on 12 November 2026 and are in the decisions table:
   [interpreter/README.md](../interpreter/README.md), "Rules from the sixth slice".
   Seventh slice (the same day): `: Copyable` and `: Formattable` on structs and enums, as the two built-in interfaces;
   a `Copyable` value is copied instead of moved, a `Formattable` one is printed (with its own `format` or field by field),
   and the error of `main` must be `Formattable`. The choices made in this slice were approved on 12 November 2026 and are in the decisions table:
   [interpreter/README.md](../interpreter/README.md), "Rules from the seventh slice".
   Eighth slice (the same day): the built-in `List<T>` and `HashMap<K, V>` (construction, `add`, index, `get`,
   `getOrAdd`, `for` in its three forms); the calculator keeps its variables in a `HashMap` as in the design, and the
   log summary runs without the files. The choices made in this slice were approved on 12 November 2026 and are in the decisions table:
   [interpreter/README.md](../interpreter/README.md), "Rules from the eighth slice".
   Ninth slice (the same day): `HashSet<T>`, `indexOf` / `contains` / `sort` on lists of numbers and text, `string.join`,
   and the first piece of the borrow check: a collection cannot be changed inside its own `for` (the direct forms; a change through another `ref` waits for the full check).
   Its choices were approved on 12 November 2026 and are in the decisions table: "Rules from the ninth slice". A collection is also written as text now (`print(xs)`).
   Tenth slice (the same day): `: Equatable`, `: Hashable` and `: Comparable` as built-in names; `==` and `<` on your own types,
   `indexOf` and `sort` on lists of them, and keys of your own in a `HashMap`. A collection is `Equatable` if what it holds is. Its choices were approved on 12 November 2026 and are in the decisions table:
   [interpreter/README.md](../interpreter/README.md), "Rules from the tenth slice".
   Eleventh slice (the same day): `test "name" { }` blocks and the test command (`sg test file.sg`); the stock tests of the design run in one file.
   The few things the design left open were approved the same day ("Test command" in the decisions table).
   On 12 November the design was made smaller again: lambdas and capture were removed (a function value is the address of a named function), and generics of your own, `where` and your own `interface` were deferred.
   Twelfth slice: function values (`Func<A, R>`) in the interpreter; the order rules example runs. Its small choices were approved the same day ("Function value details").
   Thirteenth slice: the first part of the borrow check, borrows that do not go together (one `ref` or any number of `read`, until the last use; the same place lent twice in one call). How it is found was approved the same day ("Borrows that do not go together").
   Fourteenth slice: the second part, a borrow does not outlive its owner. `from a, b` on a function that returns a borrow is parsed and checked in both directions (what is returned, and what the result borrows from at the call); a borrow of a local cannot be returned or kept past the block. Its choices were approved the same day ("A borrow does not outlive its owner", "`var` and a borrowed variable").
   Fifteenth slice: the third part, types that carry a borrow (`struct Token<from source>`, a field that is a `read`, `from source` in a method and `from x.source` outside); the calculator borrows its input instead of copying it. Its choices were approved the same day ("Following a type that carries a borrow").
   The borrow check is in place in its three parts. What it leaves out: a type with two sources, and storing a borrow from outside through `ref`.
   Sixteenth slice: the first piece of a back end. `sg build` writes the program as C (integers, `bool`, functions, structs, `read`/`ref`, `print`, the same panics) and the C compiler makes the program; every program it accepts is compared with the interpreter. The first measurements are in the performance note. Its choices were approved the same day ("Back end"), and the overflow rule became Rust's on the first measurement ("Integer overflow", "Narrowing conversion").
   Seventeenth slice: text in the back end (`string`, `read string`, ranges, comparison, `$"..."`), and the first owned values: a value is freed where its variable ends, a move empties the place it leaves. Every compiled program is also run under the address and undefined-behaviour sanitizers. Its choices were approved the same day ("Owned values in compiled code").
   Eighteenth slice: the heap and missing values in the back end (`T*`, `new`, `T?`, `null`, `??`, `try` on a `T?`, `take`, `replace`); the linked list, the stack and the account examples are compiled. Its choices were approved the same day ("Heap and missing values in compiled code"). 
   Nineteenth slice: enums in the back end (a tag and a union), `match` on an enum and on an integer, `when`, `var other`, `is` with names; the shapes and the expression-tree examples are compiled. A `when` condition may no longer move a value, and compiled code computes the parts of an expression from left to right. Its choices were approved the same day ("Enums in compiled code", "`when` only looks", "Order of evaluation").
   Twentieth slice: `Result<T, E>` in the back end (`Ok`, `Error`, `try` with the error wrapped for the caller, `??`, `match`, `is`, `Result<void, E> main()`); `try` and `?? return` are compiled wherever nothing that owns memory was computed before them in the statement. Its choices were approved the same day ("`Result` in compiled code").
   Twenty-first slice: `List<T>` in the back end (a pointer, a length and a capacity; `add`, `xs[i]`, `get`, `removeLast`, `reverse`, `clear`, `for` in its three forms, searching and sorting numbers and text, `string.join`). Its choices were approved the same day ("`List` in compiled code").
   Twenty-second slice: writing and comparing in the back end (`print` of structs, enums and lists, the struct's own `format`, `==` and `<` on them, searching and sorting lists of your own types with a sort that keeps equal items in order, a `Formattable` error of `main`). Its choices were approved the same day ("Writing and comparing in compiled code"); printing a struct without an allocation is left for later.
   Twenty-third slice: types that carry a borrow and function values in the back end (a field that borrows is a pointer, a function value is an address); the order rules example is compiled, and the calculator waits for `HashMap` alone. Its choices were approved the same day ("Borrows and function values in compiled code").
   Twenty-fourth slice: `HashMap` and `HashSet` in the back end, as hash tables with a keyed hash, and the interpreter's maps and sets rebuilt the same way; the calculator and the log summary are compiled. Its choices were approved on 13 November ("`HashMap` and `HashSet` in compiled code"); the keyed hash stays the default.
   Twenty-fifth slice (13 November): printing a struct, an enum or a collection allocates nothing in compiled code, and most places where the back end asked for a name are gone (a `for`, a `match` or an `is` on a value that has no name; a right side of `&&`, `||`, `??` that needs a text for a moment; a method on a `T*` that has no name). Its choices are in the interpreter README ("Choices made in the twenty-fifth slice") and were approved the same day, two of them changed on the way: the block for printed values is given back when one line made it larger than 64 KB, and the values of a line are computed before any of them is written, in the interpreter too.
   Twenty-sixth slice (13 November): modules in the checker and the interpreter, built as decided on 11 November ("Module", "Visibility", "Consequences of visibility", "`use`", "`use` details"), with `readonly` fields: the stock and invoice example runs as `examples/shop`, its tests with the test command on the folder. What building them asked for (a file or a folder as a program, which folders are read, a name of the file's own module before a name brought in by `use`, `readonly` all the way down) is in the interpreter README ("Choices made in the twenty-sixth slice", 60 to 72) and was approved the same day ("A program is a file or a folder" to "Modules in messages"). 
   Twenty-seventh slice (13 November): modules in the back end. `sg build` takes a file or a folder, a compiled panic names the file of its module, and the stock and invoice example is compiled and compared with the interpreter. A function can give back a `read T?` in compiled code (the example's `Warehouse.get`), and `null` or a borrowed `T` can be given where a `read T?` is expected; a `read T?` is a pointer to the `T` or no pointer ("A borrow that may be missing, in compiled code", decided the same day). The other choices are in the interpreter README ("Choices made in the twenty-seventh slice", 73 to 77) and were approved the same day ("Modules in compiled code").
   Twenty-eighth slice (13 November): the two places the back end still refused. A `match` gives its value inside a larger expression, and `try`, `?? return` and a `match` whose arm leaves stand after an argument or a field that owns memory and has no name: the value is kept under a name and freed on the way out. Every compiled program is now looked at for leaks by `cargo test`. Its choices are in the interpreter README ("Choices made in the twenty-eighth slice", 78 to 82) and were approved the same day ("Leaving in the middle of a line, in compiled code").
   Twenty-ninth slice (13 November): the test command on compiled code. `sg test <path> --compiled` builds the program that runs the `test` blocks, each in a process of its own, and reports them as the interpreter does; `cargo test` compares the two reports, runs the compiled tests under the sanitizers and looks at every passing test for leaks. Its choices are in the interpreter README ("Choices made in the twenty-ninth slice", 83 to 89) and were approved the same day ("Tests in compiled code").
   Thirtieth slice (13 November): fixed-size arrays (`T[N]`) and slices (`read T[]`, `ref T[]`) in the checker and the interpreter, with `text.asBytes()` and `string.fromUtf8`; the sensor window example runs. A hole in the borrow check was found and closed: a borrow of a value that has no name could be kept in a variable. Its choices are in the interpreter README ("Choices made in the thirtieth slice", 90 to 103) and were approved the same day ("A range that is written through" to "A borrow of a value with no name").
   Thirty-first slice (13 November): arrays and slices in compiled code. An array is a C array inside a struct, kept where its variable or its field is; a slice is a pointer and a count; `text.asBytes()` and `string.fromUtf8` copy nothing. The sensor window example and every test of arrays and slices is compiled and compared with the interpreter, and a buffer on the stack takes the time of the same buffer in C (`buffer`, 0.99). One message changed in the interpreter too: a range with an end outside the items is "out of range", not "ends before it starts". Its choices are in the interpreter README ("Choices made in the thirty-first slice", 104 to 112) and were approved the same day ("Arrays and slices in compiled code", "A range with an end outside").
   Thirty-second slice (13 November): the bit operators (`&`, `|`, `^`, `~`, `<<`, `>>` and their assignments) and numbers in hex and binary (`0xde`, `0b1010`, `1_000_000`), in the checker, the interpreter and compiled code together. `&`, `^` and `|` bind tighter than a comparison, a shift has the type of its left side, and a shift count follows the overflow rule (a compile error where it is known, a panic in a debug build, cut to the bits of the type in a release build). The sensor window example is written as the design wrote it. Its choices are in the interpreter README ("Choices made in the thirty-second slice", 113 to 124) and were approved the same day ("Bit operators", "Shifts", "Writing a number", "A number as a condition").
   Thirty-third slice (13 November): the other integer types, `sbyte`, `short`, `ushort`, `uint`, `ulong` and `nuint`, in the checker, the interpreter and compiled code together, with the mixing rule decided before the slice ("The other integer types", "Mixing integer types"). `>>` brings in zeros on the unsigned types, a number is never unsigned on its own, and an index stays a `nint`. A hash (XXH64) written with `ulong` takes the time of the same hash in C, once the eight bytes it reads are cut out of the data first. Its other choices are in the interpreter README ("Choices made in the thirty-third slice", 127 to 137) and were approved the same day ("A number next to another type" to "Unsigned types in compiled code"). The places where SG is measured slower than C and a fix is in sight are kept in the interpreter README ("Slower than C, and could be better"), to be talked over when their time comes. Thirty-fourth slice (13 November): `float` and `double` in the checker, the interpreter and compiled code together, on the rows "Number types" and "`float`, `double` and `NaN`". A fractional number takes the type it is used as, a number becomes fractional on its own only when the type holds it exactly, the arithmetic is the hardware's with nothing fused, a cast to an integer follows the overflow rule, and a number is printed with the fewest digits that read back as it. The n-body simulation takes the time of the same program in C. Its choices are in the interpreter README ("Choices made in the thirty-fourth slice", 138 to 152) and were approved the same day ("Writing a fractional number" to "Fractional numbers in compiled code"); the mixing rule was kept strict after its cost was looked at. Thirty-fifth slice (13 November): the tools for numbers, in the checker, the interpreter and compiled code together: `T.parse(text)` on every number type gives a `Result` with the built-in `NumberError` (the row "File API" decided the `Result`), the constants `MinValue`, `MaxValue`, `NaN`, `Infinity`, `Pi` and `E`, `tryToX()` and `wrappingToX()` (the row "Narrowing conversion"), whose names the panics of a cast and of an overflow now give, and the math methods (`pow`, `exp`, `log`, `sin` and the others, `fusedMultiplyAdd`, `min`, `max`). Reading a million numbers out of text takes about the time of `strtoll` and `strtod` in C. Its choices are in the interpreter README ("Choices made in the thirty-fifth slice", 153 to 166) and were approved the same day ("Reading a number out of text" to "Reading fractional numbers in compiled code"); a fractional number past the type was changed to `Infinity` on the way. Thirty-sixth slice (13 November): text, in the checker, the interpreter and compiled code together: `char` (the row "`char`"), `\u{...}` escapes, the ASCII tests of `char` and `byte`, going over a text with `chars()`, `split()`, `splitWhitespace()` and `lines()` (parts that look at the text, kept from changing while they are used), `trim()`, `indexOf`, `lastIndexOf`, `contains`, `startsWith`, `endsWith`, `equalsIgnoreAsciiCase`, and `try` on a `read T?`. The log summary is written as the design wrote it. Its choices are in the interpreter README ("Choices made in the thirty-sixth slice", 167 to 181) and were approved the same day ("`char` is not a number" to "Text in compiled code"); 167 changed the row "`char`": a character constant with nothing around it is a `char`, not a `byte`. Thirty-seventh slice (13 November): building a text piece by piece, in the checker, the interpreter and compiled code together. A `string` is its own builder: `text.write(x)` adds at its end whatever `print` takes, as `f.write` does, `text.clear()` empties it and keeps its memory, and `string.withCapacity(n)` takes room at the start; a text is not written into itself, and `+=` on text is an error whose hint is `write`. Building a million lines takes the time of `sprintf` into one buffer in C. Its choices are in the interpreter README ("Choices made in the thirty-seventh slice", 182 to 191) and were approved the same day ("Building a text" to "Building text in compiled code"). Thirty-eighth slice (13 November): files, the input and the command line, in the checker, the interpreter and compiled code together. A file is read or written whole (`File.readText`, `readBytes`, `writeText`, `writeBytes`, `appendText`), each with a `Result` whose error is the built-in `IoError`; text read from a file or the input is checked to be UTF-8 (the row "`string` guarantee"); `Stdin.readLine(ref line)` reads the input line by line into one text, as the row "File API" wrote `reader.readLine`; `main(List<string> args)` gets the command line, `printError` writes to the standard error and `exit(code)` ends the program with a code. Reading two million lines takes 1.08 times `getline` in C, which does not check them. A file read piece by piece (`StreamReader`, `StreamWriter`, `Closable`) is left for a slice of its own. Its choices are in the interpreter README ("Choices made in the thirty-eighth slice", 192 to 204) and were approved the same day ("Files read and written whole" to "Files and input in compiled code"); on the way the input was named `Stdin` instead of `Console`. Thirty-ninth slice (13 November): files read and written piece by piece, in the checker, the interpreter and compiled code together, on the rows "File API" and "Close error". `File.openText` gives a `StreamReader` (`readLine`, `readText`, `lines()`) and `File.createText` a `StreamWriter` (`write`, `writeLine`, `flush`, `close`), with a buffer of 64 KB in both; `Stdin` is a `StreamReader` and `Stdout` and `Stderr` are `StreamWriter`s, which every function has as `ref`. The `Closable` check of the row "Close error" is in the checker; beside the design's text, `return Error(...)` is let go like `try`, and `exit` is checked. Copying a log of 125 MB line by line takes 0.93 times `getline` and `fprintf` in C. Its choices are in the interpreter README ("Choices made in the thirty-ninth slice", 205 to 215) and were approved the same day ("Files read piece by piece" to "Streams in compiled code"); the row "Close error" got a note for 211 and 212. Fortieth slice (14 November): a writer that appends and bytes read and written piece by piece, in the checker, the interpreter and compiled code together. `File.openAppend(path)` gives a `StreamWriter` that writes after what the file holds; `reader.readBytes(ref buffer)` fills a buffer of bytes unless the end comes first and gives how many, and `writer.writeBytes(bytes)` writes bytes as they are, through the same reader and writer as text, so `Stdin`, `Stdout` and `Stderr` read and write bytes too. Copying a log of 125 MB in pieces of 4 KB takes 0.94 times `fread` and `fwrite` in C. Its choices are in the interpreter README ("Choices made in the fortieth slice", 216 to 222) and were approved the same day ("A writer that appends" to "Bytes in compiled code"); the cost of 217, a call that waits until its buffer is full, is an open question for the network. Forty-first slice (14 November): writing a number, in the checker, the interpreter and compiled code together. A value in `$"..."` takes a width after `,` and a letter after `:`, as in C# (`{n,5}`, `{name,-10}`, `{b:X2}`, `{mask:b8}`, `{n:D3}`, `{x:F2}`); this takes up "a format specifier inside `$"..."`", which had been left for after the first version. `T.parseHex(text)` reads hex digits on every integer type. Printing two million lines with widths and letters takes 0.89 times `printf` in C. Its choices are in the interpreter README ("Choices made in the forty-first slice", 223 to 233) and were approved the same day ("A width and a letter in `$"..."`" to "Writing a number in compiled code"). Forty-second slice (14 November): `list.addAll(slice)`, and `sort` and `reverse` on an array and through a `ref T[]`, in the checker, the interpreter and compiled code together, on the row "Arrays and slices, details". `addAll` copies the items of a `read T[]` to the end of a list when they are `Copyable` (the HTTP example in 05 already used it), and a list is not added to itself; `xs[2..5].sort()` puts a range in order where it is. Gathering a gigabyte of bytes in pieces of up to 255 takes the time of `memcpy` in C. Its choices are in the interpreter README ("Choices made in the forty-second slice", 234 to 241) and were approved the same day ("`addAll`" to "`addAll` and slice sorting in compiled code"). Forty-third slice (14 November): patterns inside patterns, `match` on text and methods on enums, in the checker, the interpreter and compiled code together. A pattern stands where a name stands (`Add(Number(0), right)`, `Word("let")`, `Paint(Red)`); this takes up "nested patterns", which had been left for after the first version. A `match` that misses a value, or has an arm that can never run, is a compile error that names the value.
