A `match` on text has texts as its patterns, and an enum has methods, so it can be `Comparable` and write its own `format` and `equals`. Sorting words by a `match` on text takes 1.03 times a chain of `memcmp` in C. Its choices are in the interpreter README ("Choices made in the forty-third slice", 242 to 253) and were approved the same day ("Patterns inside patterns" to "Patterns and enum methods in compiled code"). Forty-fourth slice (14 November): the rest of the patterns, in the checker, the interpreter and compiled code together. Patterns are joined with `or`, as in C#, each naming the same values (first written `|`, changed during the slice, since `|` is an operator on numbers in SG); numbers and characters come in ranges written `..=`, which take both ends (`'a'..='z'`; decided before the slice, so that `'a'..'z'` does not quietly leave out `z`); `true`, `false` and `null` are patterns, and on a `T?` any other pattern looks at the value, inside a variant too. A `match` can look at a `bool` and at a `T?`, and below an arm `null` a name takes the value without `?`. The check of a `match` on a number now counts the values (decided during the slice; the row "`match` on a number" changed). Sorting bytes into the classes of a lexer takes 0.87 times a chain of `if`s in C. Its choices are in the interpreter README ("Choices made in the forty-fourth slice", 254 to 262) and were approved the same day ("Ranges in patterns" to "The rest of the patterns in compiled code"). Forty-fifth slice (14 November): the names of `is` and narrowing along a chain, in the checker, the interpreter and compiled code together. An `is` on the left of `&&` names values for its right side and for the block of the `if` or `while` whose condition the chain is (`if int.parse(read text) is Ok(value) && value > 0 { ... }`), as in C#; not in `else`, not after the `if`, not beside `||` (decided before the slice). `x is not null` narrows along `&&` the same way, and, its mirror image added during the slice, `x is null` narrows along `||` (`if text is null || text.length() == 0 { return 0 }`); this extends the row "`T?` check". In compiled code a name is one pointer set inside the condition where its test holds. Its choices are in the interpreter README ("Choices made in the forty-fifth slice", 263 to 269) and were approved the same day ("The names of `is` along `&&`" to "Chains in compiled code"). Forty-sixth slice (14 November): the order of an assignment, in the checker and compiled code (the interpreter already worked so), on the row "Order of evaluation". The right side is computed first, then the place is found and written, as in Rust (decided during the slice: compiled code took the place first, so the two programs printed in different orders, and an item could be written through an address that the right side had moved). A borrow that the right side reads last now ends before the write, so `b = Box { n = r.n + 1 }` where `r` borrows `b` is accepted, and what the place uses counts as used after the right side. Its choices are in the interpreter README ("Choices made in the forty-sixth slice", 270 to 274) and were approved the same day ("The order of an assignment" to "Assignments in compiled code"). Forty-seventh slice (14 November): `text.replaceAll(old, new)` and `text.repeat(n)`, in the checker, the interpreter and compiled code together. The name is not `replace`, because `x.replace(value)` already puts a new value in a place on every type (decided before the slice). Both give a new text, so a call whose result is not used is a compile error; an empty `old` is refused as the separator of `split` is, and a negative `n` panics. Rewriting lines with them takes 0.94 times the same program in C. Its choices are in the interpreter README ("Choices made in the forty-seventh slice", 275 to 281) and were approved the same day ("`replaceAll`" to "Replacing and repeating in compiled code"). Forty-eighth slice (14 November): the rule of "A new text that is not used" taken to every built-in method that only gives a value (`line.trim()`, `name.clone()`, `count.toString()`, `distance.abs()`, `xs.contains(3)` alone), in the checker alone. The built-in methods that change their value and give one too (`removeLast`, `next`, the `add` and `remove` of a set, the `remove` and `getOrAdd` of a map) and the functions you write may stand alone. Its choices are in the interpreter README ("Choices made in the forty-eighth slice", 282 to 286) and were approved the same day ("The value of a built-in method" to "The words of an unused value"). Forty-ninth slice (14 November): narrowing under `!` and after a `while`, in the checker alone, on the row "`T?` check". A condition is read as a whole: `!` swaps what a test of `null` says (`if !(x is null)` narrows like `x is not null`), where `a && b` holds both sides held and where `a || b` fails both sides failed. A `while` ends only when its condition fails, so after `while x is null { ... }` the variable is not `null`, unless a `break` leaves the loop. On the way a hole that was there before was closed: a `break` inside a `match` arm within an expression was not seen, so a function that ended with `while true { ... }` could reach its end without a value, and an `if` whose condition empties a variable with `take` (`b is null || b.take() is null`) no longer narrows it after the `if`. Its choices are in the interpreter README ("Choices made in the forty-ninth slice", 287 to 292) and were approved the same day ("Narrowing under `!`" to "A variable the condition empties"). Fiftieth slice (14 November): the names of `is not`, and the names seen after an `if` or a `while`, in the checker, the interpreter and compiled code together. The names of `x is not Circle(r)` (and of `!(x is Circle(r))`) are seen where the test fails: on the right of `||`, in `else`, after an `if` whose block leaves, and after a `while` that only its test ends (`if int.parse(read text) is not Ok(value) { return -1 }` and then `value`), as in C# and as Rust's `let ... else`; the names of `is` are seen after an `if` whose `else` leaves. The value with no name that such an `if` looks at stays until the block around it ends. In compiled code an `is` with names on a value made by a call after the first `&&` or `||` is now compiled when that value owns no memory. Its choices are in the interpreter README ("Choices made in the fiftieth slice", 293 to 300) and were approved the same day ("The names of `is not`" to "Names in compiled code"). Fifty-first slice (14 November): numbers read out of bytes and written into them, in the checker, the interpreter and compiled code together. `T.fromLittleEndian(bytes)` and `T.fromBigEndian(bytes)` on `short`, `ushort`, `int`, `uint`, `long` and `ulong` read the number that exactly as many bytes as the type takes write (decided before the slice: a window of another width is a compile error where the program shows its width, a panic otherwise), and `n.toLittleEndian()` and `n.toBigEndian()` give its bytes as a `byte[N]` where the value is (also decided before the slice). XXH64 reads with them in the time of C, and its overflow-checked build went from 3.6 to 1.23 times C. Its choices are in the interpreter README ("Choices made in the fifty-first slice", 301 to 308) and were approved the same day ("Numbers out of bytes" to "Bytes and numbers in compiled code"). Fifty-second slice (14 November): the overflow test of an index, in compiled code alone, since nothing a program can see changes. In a checked build an index or an end of a range written `a + c`, with `c` a constant and the sum signed, is no longer tested by itself: the sum wraps, one that overflowed is negative, the check of the place refuses it, and only then is the overflow told, with the message and the line the test of the sum gave. XXH64's overflow-checked build now takes the time of its release build. Measured on the way: the checked builds of `buffer` and `vector` were not paying for an index but for the tests of a sum kept in a loop (`sum += b`), which are real; the "Slower than C" list says so. Its choices are in the interpreter README ("Choices made in the fifty-second slice", 309 to 314) and were approved the same day ("An index written `a + c`" to "Index sums in compiled code"). Fifty-third slice (14 November): the overflow tests of a sum kept in a loop, in compiled code alone. In a checked build, before a loop that adds to a sum declared before it (`sum += b`, each value a constant or of a narrower type, in a loop with no loop and no call inside) starts, the number of rounds that cannot overflow the sum is worked out from its value; the loop is written twice, the first copy runs those rounds without the test and the second takes over with it, so a sum that does overflow stops the program at the same step and with the same message. The checked build of `buffer` went from 2.6 times C to the time of C. On the way a difference between the interpreter and compiled code was found and fixed: the variable of `for i in a..b` is a copy of the count made in each round, so changing it in the body does not change the rounds (the interpreter already worked so; the design had not said). Its choices are in the interpreter README ("Choices made in the fifty-third slice", 315 to 321) and were approved the same day ("A sum kept in a loop" to "The variable of `for i in a..b`"). Fifty-fourth slice (14 November): the first part of the work on several owners, which was split into four before the slice (`shared` and `weak`, then `checked`, then compiled code, then threads and `locked`). `shared T`, `share x`, `weak T`, `share w`, `same(a, b)` and `.clone()` as the rows "Shared ownership" to "Copy of `shared T`" wrote them, in the checker and the interpreter; what a `shared` value points to is only read until `checked` comes. Type aliases (`using NodeRef = shared Node`, the row "Type alias") in the checker, the interpreter and compiled code. The leak report of the row "`shared` cycle" waits for `checked`, since without it no cycle can be made. Its choices are in the interpreter README ("Choices made in the fifty-fourth slice", 322 to 333) and were approved the same day ("`shared` is read until `checked`" to "The leak report comes with `checked`"). Fifty-fifth slice (14 November): the second part of that work, in the checker and the interpreter. `shared checked T` and `weak checked T`: what such a value points to is changed through any of its owners, and each access is checked against the borrows of it in progress ("Interior mutability"); a conflict through the same variable is a compile error and one through another variable a panic that shows both lines ("`checked` conflict"). The blocks `read x as n { }` and `ref x as n { }`, `this` with a type (`read FolderRef this`, the row "`this` with a type") and the leak report of the row "`shared` cycle", for a program and for each test. The design's folder tree, its cities and roads (without the lambda) and the doubly linked list of the syntax draft run as written. How long a borrow lasts is narrower than the row "`checked` borrow duration" says: a read or a write borrows nothing after it, and a value lent to a call is borrowed until the call returns (choice 335). Its choices are in the interpreter README ("Choices made in the fifty-fifth slice", 334 to 346) and were approved the same day ("`shared checked` as written" to "The cities without a lambda"). Fifty-sixth slice (14 November): the third part of that work, in compiled code. A `shared` value is a pointer to the value with the count of its owners and the count of its `weak` lookers in front of it; the counts are atomic, as the row "Shared ownership" decided. A `shared checked` value has the borrows of it in progress in front of the counts, checked where the checker opened the value, with the interpreter's two lines on a conflict, in a release build too. The leak report of the row "`shared` cycle" is in a debug build and in the tests, as the design wrote; a release build keeps no list of the values that are alive. The design's folder tree takes 1.53 times the same tree in C with plain pointers; the atomic counts are about a third of the difference. Its choices are in the interpreter README ("Choices made in the fifty-sixth slice", 347 to 355) and were approved the same day ("A `shared` value in C" to "A `for` takes the owner in C"). Before the fifty-seventh slice (14 November), the fourth part, the rules of threads were decided ("Starting a task" to "Tasks in the interpreter"): C#'s `Task.run(f, args)` and `Task<R>` in place of `spawn`, every task waited for on every path, a `lock` block that does not wait, so that locks and waits cannot deadlock, and no global variables; with them the direction of async, C#'s behaviour compiled the way Rust compiles it ("Async, the direction"). Fifty-seventh slice (14 November): that fourth part, in the checker and the interpreter. `Task.run(work, x)` and `Task<R>`, `t.wait()`, every task waited for on every path (an exit with an error lets it go, and the task is waited for there, as a writer is closed), the thread rule on what a task is given and gives back, `shared locked T` and `weak locked T`, the `lock` block with one value or several, and the check that nothing in a `lock` block waits, followed through the calls of the whole program. In the interpreter a task runs on a thread of its own when it is waited for, so the output is the same on every run. The counter of the syntax draft and the two files of the examples run as written; compiled code comes in the fifty-eighth slice. Its choices are in the interpreter README ("Choices made in the fifty-seventh slice", 356 to 370) and were approved the same day ("A task runs when it is waited for" to "The leak report of `locked`"); 359 was changed on the way: a value a task gives is used or dropped on purpose. Fifty-eighth slice (15 November): that fourth part in compiled code. A task is a thread of the system with a stack of 8 MB, started at once; `Task.run` takes one allocation for the thread, the function, its arguments and what it will return, and `wait` joins the thread. A `shared locked` value has a mutex of the system in front of its counts; `lock` takes several values in the order of their addresses and gives them back on every way out of the block. A panic holds the output to the end, so two do not mix and nothing is written after one, and names the task it happened in (the innermost; the interpreter now does the same); a line of `print` was whole already. `Stdout`, `Stderr` and the input are safe from several tasks, the block where printed values are written is one for each thread, and a program without tasks pays for none of it. Every program with tasks gives the interpreter's output and runs clean under the thread sanitizer; four tasks adding to a locked total take the time of the same program in C with threads and a mutex. It was run on Linux too, in a container, where it found that a program ending in a panic cannot be looked at for leaks (a panic does not unwind, so whether a leak tool sees what it owned is up to the C compiler); such programs are now counted and left out of the leak check. Its choices are in the interpreter README ("Choices made in the fifty-eighth slice", 371 to 383) and were approved the same day ("A task is a thread of the system" to "Leaks after a panic in the tests"); 372 added a note to the row "A panic in a task, shown". Fifty-ninth slice (15 November): async, the first part, in the checker and the interpreter, on the rows of "Async, the direction". `async` in front of a function makes its call give a `Task<R>` that has started and runs until it first waits, as in C#; `await t` takes the task, `await f(read xs)` runs `f` as part of the caller's task and can borrow, while a call without `await` follows the thread rule; `Task.delay(ms)` lets time go by, and `main` can be async. `wait` in an async function, anything that holds the thread there (input and output that is not async, and a function that does it, found on the whole program), `await` in a `lock` block and an async function that awaits itself are compile errors. In the interpreter every task that runs code is a fiber, a thread of its own of which one runs at a time, and the clock of the run moves only when every fiber waits, so the output is the same on every run. Cancellation and input and output that is async come next, then compiled code. Its choices are in the interpreter README ("Choices made in the fifty-ninth slice", 384 to 402) and were approved the same day ("Async comes in parts" to "A `lock` block that returns"). Sixtieth slice (15 November): async, the second part, in the checker and the interpreter, with the two rows decided before it ("A cancellation in a `Result`", "The names of async input and output"). `CancellationSource()` and `CancellationSource.after(ms)` make a source, `source.token()` gives a token and `source.cancel()` cancels, through a `read` borrow too, so a `shared` source is cancelled from another task; a task looks at its token where it chooses (`token.check()`, `token.isCancelled()`, `Task.delay(ms, read token)`). The functions of `File` and the methods of a reader have async versions that take a token last; a built-in async call given a borrow is awaited where it is written. In the interpreter that input and output is done when it is called. The design's example writes `source.token()` now, since SG has no properties. Its choices are in the interpreter README ("Choices made in the sixtieth slice", 403 to 416) and were approved the same day ("Methods of a source and a token" to "A struct without fields, written"). Sixty-first slice (15 November): async in compiled code, the third part; before it, the work left was split in two (this slice, then input and output the async way) and the row "One runtime" was changed: files are read and written on threads of their own, and the loop of epoll and kqueue waits for the network. An async function is a machine as Rust writes one: its variables live in a frame, the frame of an async function it awaits in place lies inside its own, and it comes back after an `await` with a jump to where it stopped. A call without `await` takes one allocation, the task and its frame together, and runs on the caller's thread until it first has to wait; a pool of threads, one for each core, runs it on from there, and a thread of its own keeps the delays. Cancellation is compiled as well, and every program of the two slices before that does no input and output the async way gives the interpreter's output, under the thread sanitizer too. What async adds to a call that does not wait was measured at 1.08 times the same program in C. Its choices are in the interpreter README ("Choices made in the sixty-first slice", 417 to 431) and were approved the same day ("An async function in C" to "What async costs, measured"). Sixty-second slice (15 November): async in compiled code, the fourth part: input and output the async way, with three choices made before it. The work of `File.readTextAsync` and the others, and of the async methods of a reader, is done on threads of input and output while the task waits; the threads are started as work comes, up to 512, and one with nothing to do for ten seconds ends, as Tokio's threads for blocking work do. The token is looked at before the work, as in the interpreter. A read that the block of a reader already holds is done in place, without a thread: reading two million lines of the input the async way takes 1.37 times the same program in C, where handing every read to a thread would take 84 times; what is left is the trip to a thread and back for each block of 64 KB, in "Slower than C". A task that waits for a line of the input holds no thread of the pool, which the interpreter cannot show. `try` and `await` stand on the right of `&&` and `||` in compiled code now. Its choices are in the interpreter README ("Choices made in the sixty-second slice", 432 to 440) and were approved the same day ("Async input and output in compiled code" to "Reading the input the async way, measured"). Sixty-third slice (15 November): async, the fifth part: a list of tasks, `Task.whenAll` and `Task.whenAny`, in the checker, the interpreter and compiled code together, with three choices made before it ("A list of tasks and its end", "What `Task.whenAny` gives", "No `isCompleted`"). A `List<Task<R>>` is kept where a task is kept and has only `add` and `length`; `await Task.whenAll(tasks)` gives what every task gave, in the order of the list, and `await Task.whenAny(ref tasks)` what the first to end gave, taking it out of the list. In compiled code each list has a group that its tasks tell as they end. Its choices are in the interpreter README ("Choices made in the sixty-third slice", 441 to 452) and were approved the same day ("A list of tasks, as written" to "Taking tasks out of a list, measured"). Sixty-fourth slice (15 November): async, the sixth part: streams opened, written and closed the async way (`File.openTextAsync`, `File.createTextAsync`, `File.openAppendAsync`, `writeAsync`, `writeLineAsync`, `writeBytesAsync`, `flushAsync`, `closeAsync`), in the checker, the interpreter and compiled code together, with two choices made before it ("A writer dropped in async code", "The async methods of a writer"). In compiled code a writer an async task drops is put on the task's list and closed on a thread of input and output where the task awaits it, and the interpreter now ends the names of a block in the reverse order they were declared. Its choices are in the interpreter README ("Choices made in the sixty-fourth slice", 453 to 465) and were approved the same day ("Streams the async way, as written" to "Copying a file the async way, measured"); one changed what was decided before it ("A function that is not async drops a writer"). Sixty-fifth slice (15 November): the network, TCP first (`TcpListener`, `TcpStream`, `bind`, `accept`, `connect`, `receive`, `send` and the async versions), and a cancel that ends a wait for a socket or for a line of the input, in the checker, the interpreter and compiled code together, with the choices made before it ("The network, as written", "A cancel ends a wait", "The loop of the network"). Compiled, one thread waits for the sockets with epoll or kqueue; in the interpreter the scheduler waits with `poll`. Its choices are in the interpreter README ("Choices made in the sixty-fifth slice", 466 to 479) and were approved the same day ("`receive` and `send`" to "The network the async way, measured"). Sixty-sixth slice (15 November): lines over a connection, in the checker, the interpreter and compiled code together, with the choices made before it ("Lines over a connection", "The longest line over a connection", "A read that gives what is there"): `conn.reader()` is a `StreamReader` that borrows the connection, `send` goes through a `read` borrow, a line longer than 64 KB is `IoError.LineTooLong`, and `readSome` is on every reader. Its choices are in the interpreter README ("Choices made in the sixty-sixth slice", 480 to 490; 489 fixes a task freed while a thread of input and output still woke it) and were approved the same day ("The reader of a connection, as built" to "A task woken after it was freed"). Decided on 15 November 2026: before new features, the places in the interpreter README's "Slower than C" are taken one at a time. Sixty-seventh slice (15 November): the first of them, the check that text is UTF-8: a reader checks its block at once instead of each line, and the check itself looks at sixteen bytes at a time; reading two million lines takes 0.82 times `getline` in C where it took 1.08, and over a connection 1.14 where it took 1.46. Its choices are in the interpreter README ("Choices made in the sixty-seventh slice", 491 to 495) and were approved the same day ("Checking UTF-8 a block at a time"). Sixty-eighth slice (15 November): printing a fractional number, which wrote the number with 1, 2, ... 17 digits and read each back: compiled code finds the digits with Dragonbox, whose tables `sg` makes itself, and the interpreter with Rust's shortest digits; the rule became the fewest digits that read back and the nearest of them, a tie to the even digit, as in JavaScript and Python (row "Printing a fractional number"). A million numbers print in a third of the time of `printf("%.17g")` in C, where they took six times. Its choices are in the interpreter README ("Choices made in the sixty-eighth slice", 496 to 500) and were approved the same day ("Printing a fractional number with Dragonbox"). After it the work on "Slower than C" was put aside for the moment, and a plan was made for what the language lacks before a preview: calling C in three slices ("Calling C, the direction"), then the clock, random numbers, folders and the environment, running another program, Unicode tables and UDP, and only then the preview itself (several errors at once, installing, editor support, a guide). Sixty-ninth slice (15 November): raw memory, the first of the three, in the checker, the interpreter and compiled code together: `raw T`, `unsafe { ... }` and `unsafe` in front of a value, `Memory.allocate<T>(n)`, `p[i]`, `p + n`, casts, `Memory.copy` and `Memory.free`. The interpreter checks every item against the block it came from; compiled code checks nothing, and a ring buffer over raw memory takes the time of C. Its choices are in the interpreter README ("Choices made in the sixty-ninth slice", 501 to 516) and were approved the same day ("Raw memory, as built" to "Raw memory, measured"). Seventieth slice (15 November): the second of the three, in the checker, the interpreter and compiled code together: `extern` blocks and their calls inside `unsafe`, the libraries they link, `CString`, `string.fromCString` and `buffer.pointer()`; the interpreter calls C through a library of wrappers it builds with `cc` and opens with `dlopen`, compiled code calls C directly, and zlib is wrapped in a module whose users write no `unsafe` (`examples/zlib`). Its choices are in the interpreter README ("Choices made in the seventieth slice", 517 to 530) and were approved the same day ("Calling C, as built" to "Calling C, measured"). Seventy-first slice (15 November): the last of the three, in the checker, the interpreter and compiled code together: `Disposable` and `Closable` on structs of the program, types of C seen only behind a pointer (`struct sqlite3` in an `extern` block), and structs of plain data given to C and given back by value; zlib's stream is wrapped in a `Deflater` that gives back what zlib holds when it ends (`examples/zlib`), and compresses a gigabyte in 1.04 times the time of the same calls in C. Its choices are in the interpreter README ("Choices made in the seventy-first slice", 531 to 544) and were approved the same day ("`Disposable`, as built" to "A library that keeps state, wrapped"). Seventy-second slice (15 November): the clock, the first item of the plan after calling C, in the checker, the interpreter and compiled code together: `Instant` (the monotonic clock), `Duration` (a span of time in nanoseconds) and `DateTime` (the calendar in UTC), written in SG in the language's own module `std`, which a program gets when it names one of them; only the two readings of the clock are the compiler's. A delay in the interpreter now waits its time on the real clock too, also after a long computation. Ten million readings of the clock take the time of `clock_gettime` in C, and taking ten million moments apart takes 0.29 times `gmtime_r`. Its choices are in the interpreter README ("Choices made in the seventy-second slice", 545 to 555) and were approved the same day ("The clock, as built" to "The clock, measured"). Seventy-third slice (15 November): random numbers, the next item of the plan, in the checker, the interpreter and compiled code together: `Random` (xoshiro256** seeded with SplitMix64, the same numbers for the same seed everywhere, not `Copyable`, lent with `ref`, no generator the program does not make) and `SecureRandom` (the random bytes of the system, for keys and tokens, with `hexString`), written in SG in a second file of the module `std`; only the random bytes of the system are the compiler's, and the key of a hash table is drawn with the same function. The language has no overloads, so the 64 random bits are `nextUlong()` and `nextLong(low, high)` is the range. A hundred million numbers in a range and a gigabyte of the system's bytes take the time of the same code in C. Its choices are in the interpreter README ("Choices made in the seventy-third slice", 556 to 566) and were approved the same day ("Random numbers, as built" to "Random numbers, measured"). Seventy-fourth slice (15 November): folders, paths and the environment, the next item of the plan, in the checker, the interpreter and compiled code together, with the twelve decisions taken before it. `File.delete`, `File.move` (never takes the place of a file, told in the same step as the rename), `File.replace` and `File.copy` are built in; `Directory`, `DirectoryEntry`, `FileInfo`, `FileKind`, `Path`, `Environment` and `VariableError` are written in SG in three more files of the module `std`, over calls of the system that only that module makes, and `File.info` and `File.exists` are done there too. A listing is sorted by the bytes of the names, `deleteAll` never follows a link, and the variables of the environment are only read. `IoError` has three more variants. Listing a folder of ten thousand files and asking about a file take the time of `readdir` and `stat` in C. Its choices are in the interpreter README ("Choices made in the seventy-fourth slice", 567 to 580) and were approved the same day ("Folders and files, as built" to "Folders, measured"). Seventy-fifth slice (15 November): three small leftovers of the slices before it, in the checker, the interpreter and compiled code. `wait` takes the task a call gives in one line (`Task.delay(100).wait()`), as `await` already did; a panic inside the language's own module is shown at the call of the program that led there, without the hint that named the module's code, as `#[track_caller]` does in Rust (compiled code hands the place of the call to the functions of the module as one more parameter); and `for` over what a `try` opens is compiled; `assert(a < b)` on a struct with its own `compare` no longer makes the C compiler warn, and the C of every program now compiles under `-Wall -Wextra -Werror` with clang and gcc, which the tests hold it to (one warning left on: a function nobody calls). Its choices are in the interpreter README ("Choices made in the seventy-fifth slice", 581 to 587) and were approved the same day ("Waiting for a task in one line" to "The C, without warnings"). Seventy-sixth slice (15 November): a local variable nothing reads is a compile error, decided before the slice ("A variable nothing reads"): one never used and one only written, the name of a `for` and of a pattern with `_` for one not needed, a write to a copy of a struct with the borrow as its hint. The checker notes what each name stands for, and a pass of its own tells reads from writes after every other check; compiled code no longer tells the C compiler these names are used, so its warnings check the rule a second time. It found 51 names nobody used in the code already written. Its choices are in the interpreter README ("Choices made in the seventy-sixth slice", 588 to 596) and were approved the same day ("A variable nothing reads, as built", "The rule, built and followed in C"). Seventy-seventh slice (16 November): another program run and waited for, decided before the slice: `Command` with `arg`, `args`, `directory`, `setVariable`, `removeVariable`, `clearVariables` and `input`, and `run`, `output` and `status`, written in SG in the language's own module over two calls of the system. No shell reads the words, a name is looked for only in the folders of `PATH` that start with `/`, an end with anything but 0 is an error of `run` and `output`, the program gets an environment of its own and none of our descriptors (four `open` calls of the runtime were found without close-on-exec), and an async function does not make these calls. The value of every call is now an error when nobody uses it (`try cmd.status()`, `xs.removeLast()` or a function of your own alone, `_ =` to drop it), closing a gap the slice found; it replaces the two rows that let some calls stand alone. Its choices are in the interpreter README ("Choices made in the seventy-seventh slice", 597 to 616); they were approved the same day. Seventy-eighth slice (16 November): a program started and talked to while it runs, decided before the slice: `cmd.start()` gives a `Process`, the pipes asked for with `pipeInput`, `pipeOutput` and `pipeError` are taken out of it once as streams (`takeInput`, `takeOutput`, `takeError`, each a `T?`), `wait` takes it and closes the input it still holds, `kill` and `terminate` stop it, and one that is not waited for is a compile error in a local variable and stopped with the signal 9 anywhere else. `runAsync`, `outputAsync`, `statusAsync` and `waitAsync` take a token that may come last, and a cancel stops the program. For them, and chosen by the user during the slice, a method can now be async, and a function of the language's own module may leave out a token that comes last. Its choices are in the interpreter README ("Choices made in the seventy-eighth slice", 617 to 634); they were approved the same day. Seventy-ninth slice (16 November): the Unicode tables (17.0), decided before the slice: `isLetter`, `isNumeric`, `isAlphanumeric`, `isUpper`, `isLower` and `isControl` on `char` by the general categories, `toUpper` and `toLower` on `char` one character for one and on a text by the full mappings with Final_Sigma, `equalsIgnoreCase` and `foldCase` by the full folding, no language taken into account (the Turkish mappings left for later), and one set of tables made from the Unicode Character Database for the interpreter and compiled code. Its choices are in the interpreter README ("Choices made in the seventy-ninth slice", 635 to 647); they were approved the same day. Eightieth slice (16 November): datagrams, decided before the slice: `UdpSocket` (`bind` as `TcpListener.bind`, `sendTo`, `receiveFrom` giving a `Received` with `count` and `from`, `connect` with `send` and `receive`, every method through a `read` borrow, no `close`, the async versions with a token), `SocketAddress` written in the language's own module (`parse`, `lookup`, `lookupAsync`, printed as RFC 5952 asks), the new variant `IoError.MessageTooLong` for a datagram that does not fit, and the two addresses of a `TcpStream`; multicast and broadcast later. Its choices are in the interpreter README ("Choices made in the eightieth slice", 648 to 666); they were approved the same day ("Datagrams" to "Datagrams in compiled code"). Eighty-first slice (16 November): the first of three slices toward the scheduler of Tokio, written in the C of the runtime with no library, decided before the slice after a measurement found the async echo 28 times C on Linux: the loop of the network has no thread of its own, a thread of the pool with nothing to do waits on it, and one thread at a time is woken to look for work. Its choices are in the interpreter README ("Choices made in the eighty-first slice", 667 to 677); they were approved the same day ("One runtime", "The loop of the network", "The network in compiled code", "The network the async way, measured"). Eighty-second slice (16 November): the second of the three, a socket goes on the loop of the network once, the first time a task waits for it, edge-triggered, and stays until it is closed; a wait is no call of the system, and the copy of a socket made for each wait of a listener or a datagram socket is gone. Its choices are in the interpreter README ("Choices made in the eighty-second slice", 678 to 689); they were approved the same day ("The loop of the network", "The network in compiled code", "The network the async way, measured"). On the way it was found that macOS gives `EAGAIN` to `MSG_DONTWAIT` while another thread receives from the same socket, though a datagram is there, so a datagram socket now stops blocking on its first async call, as a connection does. Eighty-third slice (16 November): the last of the three, each thread of the pool has a ring of 256 tasks of its own, from which the others steal half when they have nothing, and a slot for the task that the task it runs woke, which runs next, three in a row at most; a task woken outside the pool goes on a queue the threads share. The async echo takes 0.89 times C on macOS and 7.4 times on Linux (18.7 before, Tokio 5.5), with 450,000 calls of `futex` instead of 1.1 million. Its choices are in the interpreter README ("Choices made in the eighty-third slice", 690 to 702); they were approved the same day. On the way, Tokio's rule that the thread which finds work always wakes the next was found to wake a thread for nothing on each async read of the input, so it wakes the next only if work is left. The current state is in
   [interpreter/README.md](../interpreter/README.md). The code, comments and error messages are in English. Since 12 November 2026 the design documents are in English too.

3. Once the language is complete: write a program that is known and used in the industry, and has not been rewritten in Rust yet, in SG. Noted on 13 November. The speed-ups reported for programs rewritten in Rust come mostly from leaving an interpreted language for compiled code; C or Zig would have given the same. SG's claim is that speed together with memory safety in a language as small as C, and a real program is where it can be shown. The program was chosen on 13 November: **gifsicle**, the C tool that shrinks and edits GIF images. It has not been rewritten in Rust (there is a crate that wraps its C code, and one part of it, the lossy LZW compressor, was rewritten); its work is bytes and bits (LZW decoding and encoding, cutting down the colours, resizing), so the same algorithm can be measured against the C original; and it has had a memory bug of the kind SG rules out (CVE-2023-36193, a heap overflow set off by a crafted GIF). The program does not decide the order of the slices: the language goes on in its own order, and the program is taken up when the language is further along. Others that were looked at: jq (has a Rust clone, jaq, and is slow C, so being faster than it would not show much about the language), cmark (comrak is a line-by-line Rust port), and a part of Redis (the best known, but it needs the network, an event loop and time first).

## Dropped decisions

These no longer hold and are here for the record. Apart from the data race and recoverable error items, they were dropped on 5 November
together with the generation model and the arena:

- Run-time generation checks for use-after-free (generational references).
- An 8-byte pointer whose top 16 bits are the generation. The decision on generation width (16 bits, mask, no TBI) had been made
  the same day, and it lost its subject when the model was dropped.
- Retiring a slot that overflows.
- A slab allocator and a standard arena API as part of the language.
- `ref` being a second-class parameter mode that is only passed downwards.
- Data races being out of scope in the first version. With threads present, a non-atomic counter leads to a double free,
  so the subject touched memory safety; the thread rule is in the decisions table.
- Settling for escape analysis and avoiding lifetime notation altogether.
- Recoverable errors being left out because they need unwinding. The reasoning was wrong: an error returned as a value
  needs no unwinding (6 November).

Removed on 11 November to make the language smaller. All of them can be added back later, and it is hard to remove something once it has been added:

| Removed | Instead | Cost |
|---|---|---|
| The heavy version of `Closable`: tracking the value everywhere, spreading to structs, the ban on generics and `shared`, `IgnoreClose`, the deferred `try using var` | The version that looks only at local variables, "Close error" in the decisions table | If a value moved into a field or a collection is dropped without being closed, the compiler does not see it |
| Automatic `Ok` | `return Ok(value)`, `return Ok()` | A few characters on every success return |
| The "assign back on every exit" check when moving a field through a `ref` | Always `take` or `replace` | Negligible, Rust is the same |
| A block that produces a value on its own, `var x = { ... }` | The variable is declared and assigned inside `if/else`. The block in a `match` arm stays | A few lines |
| `ThreadFunc`, `shared Func` | Thread work starts with `spawn f(args)` *(14 November: with `Task.run(f, args)`)* | A work queue that takes lambdas (a thread pool) cannot be written in user code |
| `string.fromBytes` | `string.fromUtf8(bytes)`, `.clone()` for a copy | None |

Removed on 12 November. The measure, in the author's words: performance and simplicity are not given up. Lambdas lost on both:

| Removed | Instead | Cost |
|---|---|---|
| Lambda notation (`o => ...`) and capture: borrowing without `new`, moving with `new`, the hidden `checked` on what a lambda writes to, `Func<...>*` as an owner, the rules for writing after a copy and for moving out of a lambda | A function value is the address of a named function ("Function value" in the decisions table). Data a rule needs is passed as a parameter or carried in an `enum` variant | A few more lines where a one-line lambda stood; in exchange no run-time check, no heap, and about ten rules fewer |
| The list methods that take a lambda: `filter`, `map`, `any`, `forEach`, `removeAll`, `sortBy` | A `for` loop; `sort` with `Comparable` | The loop is written out. The machine code is the same or better |

## Notation mapping

The C# direction was chosen on 5 November. The background essays (`topics/`), the memory model and the notes below are still
in the old notation:

| Old | New |
|---|---|
| `*T` | `T*` |
| `?*T` | `T*?` |
| `&T`, `&x` | `ref T`, `ref x` |
| `while cur \|n\|` | `while cur is not null` |
| `orelse` | `??` |
| `fn f(a: T) -> U` | `U f(T a)` |
| `a: T` (field) | `T a` |
| `Node { value: v }` | `Node { value = v }` |
| `i32` | `int` |

## Notes on the first drafts

Noticed on 5 November 2026, on a second reading of the first day's texts.

### Gaps in the first memory model

The memory model gaps in this section lost their subject when the generation model was dropped, and they are kept for the record.

- **An arena cannot be invalidated "in a single operation".** The model both keeps the generation in the slot header and says "when the arena
  dies all slot generations increase, a single operation". With a generation per slot, the work is proportional
  to the number of slots. For a truly single operation the generation must be at arena level, and then where the check
  looks, with the same pointer layout, has to be solved again.
- **ARM TBI does not cover 16 bits.** Solved: 16 bits and a mask were chosen, the reason is in the decisions table.
  The sentence "on ARM, TBI gives the mask for free" in the memory model does not hold.
- **Retired memory is not negligible.** The memory model says "the memory loss is negligible". With a 64-byte
  slot and 1 million alloc/free per second, at 16 bits that makes about 84 MB a day and about 30 GB a year
  (a calculation, not a measurement). The physical memory of a fully retired page must be given back to the operating
  system. The loss then shifts to the virtual address space, but a single long-lived object left on a page prevents
  the page from being given back.
- **If an empty page is given to another size class, generations can match by chance.** The slot boundaries shift, and an old
  pointer lands on a new slot. New generations must start above the highest old generation on the page.
- **What happens if `&` is taken to a field on the heap?** The model defines `&T` for local variables.
  `&n.value` is undefined. If this expression produces an unchecked `&T`, use-after-free comes back when the function it is
  passed down to frees the object. It looks as if a reference to a heap field has to produce a generation-checked
  `*T`.
- **2-10 percent is not this design's figure.** That measurement is for Vale's layout. Here, on every dereference,
  the slot is additionally computed from the page header and the generation is read from the header. The claim "cache behaviour is the same as
  C" also holds only for pointer size. The cost must be counted as unknown until it is measured.

### Reading list links

20 links were opened on 5 November. Two of them could not be verified by the automatic check and should be looked at in a browser:
Graydon's post (504) and the MIT Press page (403). Whether the error is permanent is not known. The information that
development of Vale has stopped comes from a secondary source and was not verified.
